Addendum  III 
Funding proposal package f or FP 255  
 
Summary  
This addendum contains the following seven parts:  
a) A funding proposal titled "Transforming Livelihoods through Climate Resilient, 
Low Carbon, Sustainable Agricultural Value Chains in the Lake Region Economic 
Bloc, Kenya "; 
b) No-objection letter issued by the national designated authority(ies) or focal 
point(s);  
c) Environmental and social report(s) disclosure;  
d) Secretariat ’s assessment;  
e) Independent Technical Advisory Panel ’s assessment;  
f) Response from the accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel's 
assessment; and  
g) Gender documentation.

• Accredited Entities should provide summary information in the proposal with cross -
reference to annexes such as feasibility studies, gender action plan, term sheet, etc.  
• Accredited Entities should ensure that annexes provided are consistent with the details 
provided in the funding proposal. Updates to the funding proposal and/or annexes must be reflected in all relevant documents.  
• The total number of pages for the funding proposal (excluding annexes) should not 
exceed 60. Proposals exceeding the prescribed length will not be assessed within the usual 
service standard time.  
• The recommended font is Arial, size 11.  
• Under the  GCF Information Disclosure Policy , project and programme funding proposals 
will be disclosed on the GCF website, simultaneous with the submission to the Board, 
subject to the redaction of any information that may not be disclosed pursuant to the IDP. 
Accredited Entities are asked to fill o ut information on disclosure in section G.4.

A.21. Executive summary  (max.  750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)  
Kenya remains one of the most vulnerable countries to climate change and increased climate variability due to the 
continued importance of agriculture as an economic activity. The Lake Victoria Region, comprised of 14 counties , also 
referred to as Lake Region Economic Bloc (LREB), is one of the main agricultural  producing regions of the country . It 
is facing impacts from the combination of increased temperatures, more frequent and intense droughts, and higher 
mean annual rainfall , leading to increased flooding and runoff. These are going to impact agriculture, which employs 
72% of the working population in LREB . The local adaptive capacity is low to moderate  at best , as most smallholder 
farmers are producing on small areas of land  with limited means and without risk -reducing mechanisms. At the same 
time, low productivity creates pressure on land and natural resources, and cropland expansion is accelerating the 
degradation of ecosystems. As a result, emissions are also increasing, as marginal and fragile lands are being deforested.  
This project seeks to implement a transformation of agricultural value chains  towards low -carbon, climate resilient 
pathways  through locally -led approach . The project builds on the private sector , smallholder farming networks  and the


---
*Page 7*
---

A A A A 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 |  PAGE 3 
 strength of the cooperative movement in Kenya and creates a mechanism for technology transfer and upscale, 
leveraging the knowledge and expertise that exists in national and international farming networks. Focusing on six 
priority value chains (dairy, poultry, coffee, tea, fruit tree s and African  leafy  vegetables), the project intends to transfer 
and upscale both adaptation and mitigation technology at each value chain’s production, harvesting, processing,  and 
marketing stages.  
The project is structured around 4 Outcomes. Outcome 1 builds on the devolution process to improve county -level 
government services that facilitate upscaling of climate resilient, low carbon, sustainable value chains with a particular 
focus on how governments support cooperatives and vulnerable farmers. The project also works with county 
administrations to invest in the community -based sustainable management and restoration of agricultural landscapes 
as a basis for climate resilience in the agriculture sector (Outcome  2). Activities under Outcome 3 transfer production, 
harvest,  and processing technologies directly to farmers and their organi zations in order to reduce emissions, 
strengthen resilience, and optimize natural resources management in response to climate variability and climate change impacts . Under Outcome 4, the project will improve the farmers’ access to market s by working with value chain actors, 
and replication and scalability  will be leveraged through private sector financing to support de- risking through Kenya 
Commercial Bank  (KCB) , a Direct Access Entity (DAE) in Kenya, Co -operative Bank of Kenya and Equity Bank.  
Additional grant co- financing is mobilized through the Government of Denmark, FAO,  and the Government of Keny a. 
The project will be  delivered through FAO as an accredited entity, with FAO and Agriterra as co- Executing Entities, and 
the Government of Kenya will execute the activities they are co- financing.  
The project targets  572,000 direct beneficiaries, who are mainly smallholder farmers (men and women) . The financial 
internal rate of return of the project is 23% with a benefit -cost ratio of 1.11; and 25% and 1.13 respectively for RPC 
8.5 and RPC 2.6 scenarios for a 20- years period at 18% discount rate. The project will be able to earn US $ 10.7 
million as a net present value under the same discount rate for 20 years under RPC 8.5 (US $ 12.9 for RPC 2.6). The 
project can breakeven the project expenditure plus the beneficiaries’ investments after 9 years (pay -back period) of 
project commencement for both climate change scenarios.  
The project will directly contribute to the following targeted results in alignment with the GCF’s Updated Strategic  Plan 
(USP- 2); T1 (by supporting the country’s adaptation and mitigation goals), T4 Food ( support 572,000 smallholder 
farmers  adopting low -emission climate- resilient agricultural practices ) and T5 Ecosystems ( 30,540 ha to be conserved, 
restored and sustainably managed)  through T9 locally -led approach.  The Project will also support dissemination of 
climate information to communities, directly contributing to T3 CIEWS. The Project also aligns with T10 (by  linking 
supported cooperatives and SMEs with the partner private banks for loans ).

B.1. Climate context (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)  
B1.1  DEVELOPMENT PROBLEM OF K ENYA : AGRICULTURE  PRODUCTIVITY , VULNERABLE SMALLHOLDRS 
AND POVERTY.  
1. Agriculture contributes to 26% of Kenya’s GDP  and 65% of Kenya’s total exports4. Out of Kenya’s 47.6 million 
people, 18.3 million (38%) are employed in the formal sector5 while  the remaining 29.3 million people (62%) work 
in the informal sector, primarily in small scale agriculture and pastoralism6. More than two thirds of rural Kenyans 
who largely depend on agriculture and livestock sectors (67% or 18.7 million people) are experiencing 
multidimensional poverty.  The Lake Region Economic Bloc (LREB), which consists of 14 counties located in the 
Lake Victoria Basin (LVB), is a vital agricultural region in Kenya and one of the most densely populated regions in 
Kenya  where around 30% of the country’s population lives in 5% of the country’s territory7. In the LREB, 72% of 
the population relies on agricultural output for food security and livelihoods8.  
2. Lake Victoria, the largest freshwater lake in Africa and the 
second largest in the world, is entirely fed by rainwater9 and 
provides the source of freshwater for Kenya . Most of the land 
surrounding the l ake is used for cropland cultivation, in 
addition to some forested areas  (Error! Reference source 
not found.10). While the LREB is historically known for its 
high agricultural potential, population pressure and reduced productivity in the agriculture sector have increased the 
vulnerability of smallholder farmers to climate change and 
reduce environmental resilience, affecting both the 
livelihoods of the population and GDP.  Most crop farmers in 
LREB are smallholders, with chronically low productivity
11. 
Livestock farmers also have small herds with limited coping capacity and low productivity. Developed cash crop value chains, such as tea and coffee, are also produced by 
smallholders pooling their production through farmer organizations and cooperatives . For additional details on 
agriculture in LREB, please refer to Feasibility Study (FS), Part B, section 1.3.  
3. The sector has witnessed reduction in productivity of  the crop 
and livestock systems targeted by the project such as African leafy vegetables, tea, coffee, banana, as well as milk and 
egg, resulting in unstable incomes, and threatening food 
security. Overall, increasing rainfall variability has already led 
10% of the population in the LVB  to suffer chronic food 
insecurity, requiring short -term emergency food relief and 
long-term development programs
12. Key development challenges of the agricultural sector exacerbating 
vulnerability to climate change include poorly developed value chains, limited infrastructure, high rainfall 
dependence,  and limited financial resources.  For additional details on socio- economic development indicators, 
please refer to FS Part B section 1.2.  
Figure 1: Lake Victoria basin

plan- 2015- 2030- 2016/  4.  LREB  aims  to coordinate and facilitate service delivery; promote trade linkages, investment, and economic activity; 
enhance public investment and promote developmental research, and innovation in social planning and 
coordination of activities by creating communities of practice and centers  of excellence. Importantly, the 14 counties 
of the LREB share a common vision of sustainable development and are mobilized to take joint action against 
environmental degradation, poverty, and climate change, recognizing potential large -scale impac ts on people’s 
livelihoods due to its high population density13. However, there has been  less climate investment/support provided 
for th is region compared to other regions such as arid and semi -arid (ASAL ) regions . 
B.1.2 CLIMATE -DEVELOPMENT NEXUS, CLIMATE DRIVERS AND CLIMATE CHANGE IMPACTS14 
5. The LREB is a vital agricultural region in Kenya with  the largest source of freshwater and host almost 30% 
of the country’s population . It plays a major role in ensuring food security and income for the entire 
country . It is essential to establish sustainable and climate resilient pathways for agriculture in this region to benefit  
the national economy. However, there are increased observed and projected climate  hazards in the region which 
negatively impact agricultural production and productivity . Smallholder s and the value chain actors are vulnerable 
to the climate change impacts and have limited capacities in addressing these challenges.  Please refer to Annex 
2, FS, Section 6.1 for more on the geographical targeting of the project.  
B.1.2.1 Climate Hazard  
B.1.2.1a LREB Historical Climate Trends  
6. From 1981 to 2010, the annual average of both daily maximum and minimum surface temperatures 
increased by 0.8°C (0.03 °C per year) in the eastern and southernmost areas, and by 1.1°C (0.04°C per year) 
in the western and northernmost areas  15 (Figure Error! Reference source not found. ). The increases in daily 
minimums and maximums in the LREB are greater than in the central and easternmost areas of Kenya. These 
increasing trends from the  W5E5 dataset were statistically significant.  
7. Northern Busia and Kakamega, Trans -Nzoia, Bungoma, and Nandi have experienced a total annual 
precipitation increase of 230 mm (15%) from 1981 – 2010. All other LREB counties have experienced a 
60mm ( -5%) decrease in precipitation.  While the northern regions have a net increase in precipitation, rainfall 
during the March- April-May (MAM) season has decreased by 14% and the October -November -December  (OND) 
season has increased by 25%  (Figure 9, Annex 2, Feasibility Study, Part A) . 
8. During the MAM rain season (the long rain season), dry spells have increased, with  up to 50 dry days, with a 
maximum duration of 10 consecutive dry days (Figure 14, Annex 2, Feasibility Study, Part A). Southern LREB 
counties are experiencing more dry days during critical rainy seasons, despite receiving more total precipitation. In 
the northern LREB counties, the distribution of total precipitation is not equal. The number of days per year with precipitation exceeding 20mm has increased by 5 to 6 days from 1981 to 2010 (Figure 15, Annex 2, Feasibility 
Study, Part A). ENSO has intensified and increased the frequency of heavy rainfall events, leading to flooding in 2014, 2015, 2018, and 2019
16. 
9. Flooding is historically the most common extreme event in the LREB . However,  trends are evolving  
(Figure  24, Annex 2, Feasibility Study, Part A ). Northern LREB counties at higher latitudes are experiencing growing  
total precipitation, while central counties at lower altitudes and closer to Lake Victoria are see ing higher daily 
minimum and maximum temperatures increases . Southern counties are having more dry days. This increase in 
temperature is leading to 12- 24 additional days per year with maximum surface temperatures above 35°C in 
Kisumu, Busia, and Siaya (Figure 12, Annex 2, Feasibility Study, Part A).  
B.1.2.1b LREB Climate Projections - RCP  2.6 and 8.5    
10. There is a model agreement (at least 60% of the models agree in the sign of the climate change signal) in daily maximum and minimum surface temperature change.  
11. Table  1 and Table 2  below show the projected temperature increases under RCP  2.6 and 8.5 for the LREB counties 
(Figure  16, Annex 2, Feasibility Study, Part A)  in the near term (2039)  and long term (2100) . All counties  are 
expected to have increased annual average of daily maximum (  
12. Table  1) and minimum surface temperatures ( Table  2) from 2010 to 2100.

13. There is a high level of agreement among model s regarding the climate change signal f or total annual precipitation . 
It is projected to decrease by up to 50mm ( -3%) in the mid- term (2040- 2069); increase by 50mm (+3%) in the long-
term (2070- 2099) under RCP2.6; and increase by 100mm (+7%) in the long- term under RCP8.5, when compared 
to the baseline period of 1976- 2005 ( Figure 17, Annex 2, Feasibility Study, Part A) . The changes in s easonal  
precipitation for the LREB counties vary (see Table  3, Figure 18, Annex 2, Feasibility Study, Part A ). In both 
RCP  2.6 and 8.5 scenarios , the June- July-August -September (JJAS) rainy season decreases by 2070. However, 
the October -November -December (OND) rainy season, increases under both scenarios by 2070,  with a much 
greater increase in the RCP  8.5 scenario. Despite the slight decreases in seasonal precipitation by 2040, the 
increase in total precipitation during the OND rainy season increase the likelihood of seasonal flooding.

14. Extreme heat events, dry spells, and heavy rainfall events are projected to increase in the future in the LREB. 
Under RCP2.6, the annual number of extreme heat days (Tmax>35°C) is expected to remain stable at 20 days 
as in the baseline period. U nder RCP8.5, the number of extreme heat days will increase by 20 days by 2040 and 
50 days by 2100 for all LREB counties except for Bomet, Nandi, Kericho, and Trans -Nzoia17 (Figure  20, Annex 2, 
Feasibility Study, Part A). B oth RCP  2.6 and 8.5 show an increase in the annual number of dry days . Under 
RCP  2.6, the number of dry days will increase from 160 to 210 days by 2040 and to 220 days by 2100 (Figure 21, 
Annex 2, Feasibility Study, Part A). Seasonally, there  will be 5 more dry days in the MAM season under both 
RCP2.6 and RCP8.5 by 2100. This will reach 18- 20 days for Trans -Nzoia, Bungoma, Busia, Kakamega, and Nandi; 
and 12 to 16 days for Siaya, Vihiga, Kisumu, Kericho, Homa Bay, Migori, Kisii, Nyamira, and Bomet counties. B oth 
RCP  2.6 and 8.5  show an increase in, the number of heavy rainfall events  per year which will average around 10 
days by 2040 and 20 days by 2100, compared to the current average of 5 days  (Figure  23, Annex 2, Feasibility 
Study, Part A).  
B.1.2. 2 Climate exposure and vulnerability: observed and p rojected c limate change impact s 18 
15. Smallholders are the beneficiaries of this project and are vulnerable to the current and future impacts of climate 
variability and climate change. The smallholders in the LREB are characterized as follows (please refer to Annex 
2, FS, Part B, section 6.1.3 for details on selection of participants):  
• Working small land parcels, typically less than 2 acres.  
• Depending on a single or few commodities for livelihoods with limited diversification.  
• With limited access to off farm employment.

16. Small holder  farmers dominate Kenya’s agricultur al sector19 comprising  72% of the total working 
population in the LREB.  However, these farmers face chronic low productivity  and limited alter native  livelihoods , 
working on small land parcels  with limited divers ification , depending on low input, rain- fed production of a single or 
few commodities for livelihoods , with limited access to off farm employment and asset ownership20. Even in  
developed cash crop value chains, such as tea and coffee, smallho lders participate by  pooling their production 
through farmer organizations and cooperatives.  Further , the adoption of improved technologies and practices 
among smallholders, such as  improved seed varieties, mechanization services, proper fertilizer  use, input 
utilization , and access to extension services remains relatively low. Production systems are typically extensive and 
have high impact  on natural resources per unit of output  (please refer to Annex 2, FS, Part B, section 6.1.3 for 
details on selection of participants) .  
17. Smallholders’ livelihoods in the LREB are highly vulnerable to increasing temperatures to  inter -annual 
rainfall anomalies. These anomalies  result in dry spells, drought events , extreme rainfall and flooding, 
which cause compounded socio- economic impacts across the country. These impacts lead to  losses of 
about 8% of the GDP every five years21. For example, a 2007 study on the economic impacts of climate change 
on crops in Kenya found that decreased productivity from increasing temperatures could range between 32$/ha 
and 178$/ha22. The severe drought from 1998 to 2000 caused $2.8 billion USD in losses through damages to crop 
yields, livestock production, and freshwater availability23. Projected increasing maximum and minimum 
temperatures , along with changes in the length and intensity of the long rainy season (MAM), which is the main 
growing period responsible for 26% of the GDP, will have significant impacts on Kenya’s rainfed agricultural and economic systems. These impacts particularly affect smallholder agri -businesses, leading to significant losses in 
revenue
24. Furthermore, riverine floods are responsible for GDP losses of up to 5.5% and 60% of weather -related 
casualties25. The country lacks climate -proofed infrastructure, such as roads, bridges, dams, and water pipelines, 
which worsens the impacts of flooding.  
18. In the 14 counties, smallholders face high levels of exposure and sensitivity to climate variability and climate 
change . This is due to limited access to assets and services,  such as water  (on average less than 30% have 
access to clean water and sanitation), food (on average, 16% of children suffer  from malnutrition and 22% exhibit 
stunting), and energy ( only 33% have access to electricity)26. Gender and cross -sectional inequalities p ersist , 
with women, youth, and persons living with disabilities being more significantly affected by vulnerability.  In general, 
women and youth earn less income from agriculture and often go unpaid for their labor.  They are more likely to 
engage in informal economic activities, have less secure access to land, and have limited access to capital. Both 
in farmer organizations and  in households, women and youth often have less influence on economically significant 
decision -making.   
19. Crop and livestock production systems and related value chains in the LREB are vulnerable to climate change .  
Extreme weather impacts go beyond primary production and affect the entire value chain. Farmers, in particular, 
experience financial and income losses due to price disruptions and the loss of crops. Observed and projected 
climate hazards and their impacts are summarized in Error! Reference source not found. a,b. below and further 
detailed in the Feasibility study (Tables  8 to 15, Annex 2, Feasibility Study, Part A).

B1.3 CAPACITY TO ANTICIPATE AND RESPOND TO CLIMATE  CHANGE  
22. Farmers’ adaptive capacity to climate change is limited due to inconsistent  access to water, limited  income  
diversification , and l ack of access to appropriate technologies and climate information.  Further, the majority 
of smallholders lack risk -sharing reducing mechanisms,  such as social safety nets, financ ial resources , and 
alternative coping strategies,  and to make more informed decisions about climate- responsive production choices.  
23. Despite previous interventions , the local adaptive capacity remains low, as most smallholder farmers 
continue to cultivate  small  plots  of land with limited means, and without necessary the risk- reducing 
mechanisms to make climate- responsive production choices . A survey conducted in Kakamega and Vihiga 
counties in the LREB, revealed that  smallholder farmers heavily  rely on rainfed, seasonal,  and informal agricultural 
activities49. Further more, the limited size of their landholdings also hampers  their access to agricultural credit. 
Lessons learned from previous development programs implemented by various partners emphasize the importance 
of supplementing traditional extension -based interventions with risk -sharing mechanisms such as cooperatives . 
These cooperatives can help alleviate producer isolation, enhance adaptive capacity and improve access to 
adaptive assets (Refer to Annex 2, FS, Part B )50. 
24. There is limit ed integration of climate  and low carbon strategies into  land use strategies and management 
plans . Since the 1970s, land use change in the LREB has been driven by population pressure and low productivity, 
resulting in the expansion of agriculture into wetlands, grassland, and deforestation of hill slopes51. The risk of land 
degradation fueled by land use and land cover changes is considered high in many parts of LREB.  These 
anthropogenic drivers lead to erosion, biodiversity  losses  and ecosystem diversification, land use changes, 
urbanization, and weak water management systems and pollution52. These changes are accelerating the 
degradation of ecosystems that are vital to  supporting agricultural productivity.  Deforestation hotspots have been 
identified in the southern areas of the LREB where tree cover has been reduced to make space for cropland 
(Figure  26, Annex 2, Feasibility Study, Part A).  
25. Impacts from inter related climate hazards are exacerbated by anthropogenic pressure on key socio -
economic activities in the region, such as the expansion of the production land caus ing deforestation.  
Flooding impacts for example are exacerbated by land quarrying, sand harvesting, and wetland drainage to support agricultural development. These activities frequently increase soil erosion along riverbanks and farmlands
53.  
26. The destruction of catchment forests and wetlands is linked to the disruption of rainfall patterns, droughts , 
and floods.54  Prolonged droughts have negatively affected water availability and vegetation in the basin. Loss of

Extreme flooding in parts of Busia, Homa Bay , and Kisumu counties submerges productive lands , destroying crops 
and properties, and causing erosion.  
27. Climate change is partly responsible for crop failures, water shortages, reduced water quality , and decreased 
hydropower generation capacity in the basin due to low water levels57. Scientific reports have confirmed that 
eutrophication and climate change are the leading causes of ecological degradation in LREB.  
28. For more information on environmental conditions in the LREB, please refer to FS Part B, Section 1.4. 
29. The public services offered to farmers and cooperatives are insufficient to enable value chain 
transformation.  National and county -level climate policies, regulatory framework s and strategies have been 
established in Kenya, reflecting a commitment to addressing climate challenges. However, the insufficient climate 
services provided to smallholders remain a concern. The technical capacity among county government staff is  yet 
to be strengthened, resulting in a gap in translating these objectives into actionable plans that directly support 
farmers. Developing specific tools and methodologies tailored to the local context could be instrumental in bridging this gap.   
30. There is a lack of support from  buyers and other actors in the value chain to help  smallholders adopt 
climate actions and transit ion to low carbon pathways in their production . Additionally, there is a need  to 
invest in sustainable supply chains and  provide  smallholders with access to market s and fair prices. Market 
incentives may not currently align with climate action in agriculture. For example, there may be a lack of price 
premiums for sustainably produced agricultural products, making it challenging for farmers to adopt climate- smart 
practices. Additionally, the costs of environmental externalities associated with conventional agricultural practices, 
such as greenhouse gas emissions or soil degradation, are often not reflected in market prices. Limited access to 
credit, technical assistance, and market information can hinder  efforts to adopt sustainable agricultural practices.  
B1.4 GREENHOUSE GAS (GHG) EMISSION PROFILE  
31. In 2015, the agriculture sector was the leading source of GHG emissions in Kenya. According to Kenya’s Updated 
NDC (2020), GHG emissions are projected to reach 143 MtCO2e by 2030, which is a  152% increase from 93.7 
MtCO2e in 201558. Kenya’s Second National Communication59 states that agriculture emissions , the largest source 
of GHG emissions in the country,  are expected to increase from 30 MtCO2e in 2010 to 39 MtCO2e in 2030, primarily 
driven by livestock methane emissions (18.8 MtCO2e in 2030) and emissions from  agricultural soils (16.2 MtCO2e 
by 2030).  
32. Smallholder farming also utilizes woody biomass for charcoal production, resulting in deforestation, land 
degradation, biodiversity loss, and loss of soil organic matter. According to the  findings from the cooperative census 
undertaken in 2022 for the development of this proposal, farmers appear to use a semi -diversified energy basket 
that varies according to costs, accessibility, and the needs of each value chain. Initial findings of the cooperative 
census indicate that only 41% of cooperatives exclusively  use hydro- electricity, while the rest use a combination of 
hydroelectricity and other sources such as solar, firewood, LPG and kerosene. This is  not a comprehensive census 
of energy uses, but it does provide a representative snapshot of the current situation in the LREB region. There is evidence in the area that hydro- electricity supply varies when rainfall is lower or there is a drought, highlighting the 
need to ensure adequate access to energy under projected climate conditions.  
Finally, the region is also experiencing increasing emissions from deforestation, land degradation, and the expansion 
of agricultural land into forests. The annual rate of forest loss has been  estimated at 6% since 200060. According 
to Global Forest Watch, “between 2001 and 2021, forests in Kenya emitted 8.56MtCO ₂e/year and removed -
13.7MtCO ₂e/year. This re sults in  a net carbon flux of  -5.09MtCO ₂e/year”, with a significant proportion of forest 
emissions originations  from the western region. While  Kenya is not a net emitter, it is necessary to separate

B1.5. Alignment with county priorities  
33. The project aligns  with relevant national laws, policies, and plans, and contributes to the development and climate 
change goals of the Government of Kenya. This includes the National Climate Change Act and Strategy, as well 
as laws and policies in the agriculture, land, water , and environment sectors, existing institutional frameworks for 
climate risk and disaster risk management as well as their equivalent at county level. The project also supports the implementation of Kenya’s National Adaptation Plan (N
AP) and Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC). 
Relevant policies, laws and institutional frameworks include:  
• Kenya’s Vision  2030 development  plan 
• The National Climate Change Act (2016)  61 
• Kenya’s Second National Communication62 
• Kenya’s National Adaptation Plan 2015 – 203063 
• National Climate Change Action Plan 2018 - 202264 
• National Climate Change Response Strategy (NCCRS), 201065 
• Nationally Determined Contribution (updated, 2020)  
• The Cooperative Societies Act, Chapter  490, Revised 2005  
• The Water Act, 2016  
• The Energy Act, 201966 
• The Kenya Climate Smart Agriculture Strategy (CSAS) 67, 2017- 2026  
• The Forest Conservation and Management Act (FCMA), 201668 
• The Environmental management and Coordination Act (1999)  
• The National Disaster Risk Management Policy (NDRMP), 2017  
• The National Environment Policy (NEP)69, 2013  
34. The country has put in place several other key policies to support implementation of climate change adaptation and mitigation actions. Some of these policies include The National Policy on Climate Finance, which emphasizes  how 
climate investment can support 
measures like reducing deforestation, diversifying livelihoods, and promoting 
research and innovation. Furthermore, the Climate Risk Management Framework  aims to harmonize it climate 
change and disaster risk policies242.  
35. Regarding key  agricultural policies , the National Livestock Policy of 2015 recognizes the increased frequency and 
severity of droughts as a result  of climate change. Th is policy outlines  measures to enhanc e the livestock 
subsector 's contribution to food and nutritional security, provide raw materials for agro- based industries , and 
contribute t o improved livelihoods . The Agricultural Sector Transformation and Growth Strategy (ASTGS) (2019-
2029) and the Kenya Climate Smart Agriculture Strategy (KCSAS) (2017- 2028) further prioritize addressing the 
impact of climate change on all agricultural  sub-sectors ( crops and livestock). The KCSAS highlights adaptation 
and resilience as one of four broad strategic areas244.  
36. At the national level, coordination and implementation of climate change adaptation policy and action plans are 
overseen by the National Climate Change Council (NCCC), headed by the President, the Cabinet Secretary of Climate Affairs, and the Climate Change Directorate
70. County level coordination and implementation of climate 
change adaptation policy and action plans are overseen by the county governor and the climate change unit (CCU). 
Most county governments have enacted climate change laws, Climate Change policies, cr eated climate change

B1.6. Related projects/interventions  
38. There are several ongoing initiatives in Kenya and in the LREB that support climate adaptation and mitigation in 
the agriculture sector. This project builds on these initiative s as well as on the devolution and decentralized climate 
governance systems set up by the Government of Kenya.  
39. The list includes two GCF Readiness initiatives that ended in 2022. The FAO -supported NAP initiative
71 delivered 
studies, information and institutional capacity that is being directly used as a contribution to this project. This NAP 
Readiness grant was implemented from 2018 to 2022 with FAO as a Delivery Partner. The project enhanced 
technical and institutional capacities for adaptation planning, built the NAP knowledge base, improved evidence-
case for climate change adaptation, and promoted private sector investment in the adaptation process. T he 
Readiness project conducted a ten- cohort training that reached 338 climate change units across all departments 
and ministries in the agricultural sector. The NAP readiness also delivered training to build the capacity of technical 
staff at the county level to integrate climate change risks and opportunities in planning and budgeting, and delivered 
data analysis and information management systems in six national institutions that were linked across all the 47 
counties and with national planning processes. The activities under Outcome  1 are therefore designed to take these 
processes and capacities to the next stage, for example by broadening the trainings and technical assistance to 
the departments of cooperatives in each county, and refining the data collection processes, as well as collecting decentralized data, that  will feed into the systems established under the NAP project.

B.2 (a). Theory of change narrative and diagram  (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagram)  
43. The climate analysis shows that the anticipated changes in climate would make smallholder farmers more 
vulnerable by reducing production and productivity, and that these farmers are already experiencing vulnerability 
and climate risks from current variabil ity. The climate analysis also shows that lack of adaptive capacity and 
sensitivity to climate variability and risks, are high among smallholders in LREB. LREB has one of the highest


---
*Page 18*
---

B B B B 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0  | PAGE 14 
 
  
73 The 8 Principles of LLA are: 1.devolution of decision- making to the lowest appropriate level; 2. addressing structural inequalities; 3. 
providing predictable and accessible funding;  4. Investing in local institutional capacity; 5. building an understanding of climate risk; 6. 
flexible programming and learning; 7. transparency and accountability; 8. collaborative action and investment.  Although the project makes 
an indirect linkage to LLA, the principles are upheld in all outputs and activities.   
74 FAO supported NAP Readiness, 2022, final report.  population densities in Kenya. Together with the unsustainable natural resource management practices used by 
smallholders, climate change could have dramatic effects on the entire economy of the LREB. Furthermore, the 
drive to increase production in response to climate risks could, under the business -as-usual scenario, lead to 
increased emissions from extensification and land use change. These trends would culminate in less access to food and a visible impoverishment of local farmers.  
44. There have been few climate finance initiatives in the LREB. Previous development programmes and investments also did not fully achieve maximum benefits for resilience and decarbonization of the agriculture sector because they missed a few crucial ingredients: to foster the emergence of locally -driven adaptation
73 solutions, building on 
the strengths and potential of local organizations such as cooperatives is key. Creating flexible demand driven 
technology transfer mechanisms that are based on peer support, and ensuring the financial viability – and even 
profitabi lity – of any climate solution proposed, are key to ensuring continuous endogenous adaptation even after 
project execution.  
45. The project proposes a set of adaptation practices designed to improve adaptive capacity, access to adaptive 
assets and social safety nets, prevent losses and sustainably increase productivity as a means of improving 
resilience to climate change. To ensure that any gains in resilience are not made at the cost of increasing emissions 
and the degradation of the ecosystems, the project proposes adaptation solutions focused on intensification and 
solutions focused on mitigation of emissions from the AFOLU sector.  
46. To disseminate these adaptation and mitigation practices at scale and to promote rapid transformation, the project sets up a long-
term, lasting mechanism to support technology transfer that leverages the strengths of the 
cooperative movement in Kenya as well as the devolution process. The project will increase the climate resilience 
of producers, facilitate both public and private finance investment in technology transfer and up- scale, and generate 
climate resilient, low -carbon business models.   
47. Several mutually -reinforcing barriers have, however, prevented the deployment of adaptation solutions, which this 
project must now address  in order to facilitate the paradigm shift. 
Barrier  1: The public services offered to farmers and cooperatives are insufficient to enable value chain 
transformation.  
48. At national level, there are adequate climate change and sector related policies and regulatory frameworks to guide 
climate action in all sectors. At county level, there is also notable progress in mainstreaming of climate change in relevant laws and regulations: over 33 counties out of 47 have enacted a climate change Act and a significant 
number of counties have developed their own county climate change funds. All counties have set up Climate 
Change Units. However, there is evidence that the County Integrated Development Plans (CIDPs,) which are the principal county policy and development plans, are only beginning to mainstream climate change issues
74.  
49. Moreover, counties have yet to develop specific tools and methodologies that would enable operationalization of their CIDP’s climate objectives into concrete action in support of farmers. A key example is the delivery of agro-meteorological advisory, climate information and extension, which still follows traditional, cost -heavy methods that 
have proven unsuccessful in reaching the full scope of last mile users. Given the dynamic nature of the agriculture sector in the LREB, there is scope for introducing innovation in the way that county governments support farmers as private sector agents.  
50. The delivery of such improved, enabling services from government to private sector farmers would, however, 
require much improved information systems. To enable a local understanding of climate risks and vulnerabilities, facilitate rapid climate response and support the socio-
economic performance of LREB farmers, the data and 
information basis at county level needs to be improved. This includes the nature of data (e.g., climate- related crop 
data, or accurate price data) and information services, the efficien cy and effectiveness of collection and 
dissemination methods, and the way in which the information and data is used and leveraged for climate resilient, 
low-carbon growth.  
51. Among county government staff, technical capacity to deal with climate variability and climate change is nascent but growing. Many training programs have taken place that provide a baseline of understanding, knowledge, and capacity. However, the departments of extension and cooperatives typically have not yet been fully included in 
capacity development for climate change. As a result, they are not yet fully able to deliver support programs, 
extension or to develop innovative guidelines that would facilitate adaptation and mitigation at the farm level.


---
*Page 19*
---

B B B B 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0  | PAGE 15 
 
  
75 The Climate Change Survey and Cooperative Census  were conducted by FAO and Agriterra in 2022.  Barrier  2: Limited integrat ion of  climate resilien t and low carbon strategie s into land use planning and 
landscape management at county level .   
52. Current environmental degradation trends show that cropland will continue to expand at the expense of the natural 
ecosystems unless critical changes occur to halt it. This situation is complex to address due to several interrelated 
factors:  
53. While much is currently being done to strengthen county government capacity in all aspects of climate change, there remain key policy gaps in terms of managing land use, preventing further land use change, and conserving ecosystem services. There do not exist any int
egrated approaches tying together adaptation and mitigation 
priorities, and the territorial approach to managing natural resources is un- coordinated. As a result, natural parks 
and reserves remain disconnected from the broader landscape; and because enforcement is difficult, there are serious encroachment threats onto fragile areas, including wetlands. There is a need for a more integrated territorial approach that would bring together all sectors under common goals of agricultural productivity, environment al 
management, natural resources conservation, climate adaptation and emissions reductions.  
54. There are, however, missing data and information at local level that would help inform the development of integrated landscape management strategies. While there have been major efforts at mapping key environmental resources 
(including for example the Kenya atlas of biodiversity, the Atlas of wetlands), these are not regularly updated. These 
reports do not always inform county policies on land use, and counties may not be entirely capacitated to integrate all the information into coherent, climate- informed,  landscape management strategies and plans.  
Barrier  3: Smallholders have limited capacity to  access productive assets and face risks in adopting climate 
resilient, low -carbon technologies.  
55. Farmers must first acquire the technical knowledge, know -how as well as the material assets required to be able 
to change their habitual patterns of production towards climate resilient, low -carbon methods. While many of the 
farmers, as evidenced in the Climate Change Survey and Cooperative Census75, are aware of the impacts of 
climate variability and potential impacts of climate change on their livelihoods, few are adopting the full suite of available options for adaptation or mitigation. Without proper support from governments and without a mechani sm 
to support the dissemination and broader adoption of climate resilient, low -
carbon technologies, farmers will 
continuously be left to use maladapted extractive practices and think in terms of short -term results. Yet traditional 
climate extension methods  have not proven entirely effective in getting the knowledge to farmers, and in 
accelerating adoption of best practices. A public -private alliance is needed that will foster farmer innovation and 
creativity, while supporting appropriate climate action through better rules, regulations, knowledge, and norms. Traditional “top-
down” extension methods often perpetuate barriers to accessing technologies, because the 
technologies are not well suited to farmer priorities and needs. Peer - to peer networks have in t he past, and 
elsewhere, proven effective means through which farmers can self -identify climate challenges and select solutions 
that make economic, social as well as climate sense.  
56. However, the adoption of climate resilient, low -carbon technologies is also hindered by the sub- optimal organization 
of value chains and farmers in the LREB region, particularly for the non- traditional cash value chains. Too many 
smallholders work on very small, fragmented plots, in isolation from one another, or in informal groupings that bring little added value in terms of risk reduction. Existing cooperatives exhibit a higher level of organization that enables 
them to act as vehicles for risk sharing when adopting climate resilient, low -carbon technologies, save costs and 
reduce transaction costs for farmers (in the acquisition of knowledge and assets). Yet even formed cooperatives 
face organizational challenges that prevent them from fully realizing the
ir potential for members, in terms of 
economic and climate gains: it is organizational gaps (such as lack of adequate climate- oriented business planning) 
that prevent cooperatives from leveraging finance that would enable scaling of climate resilient, low -carbon 
practices.  
57. The low access to formal rural finance for adoption of climate resilient, low -carbon technologies prevents 
smallholders from accessing knowledge and assets they require. This low access is due not only to the 
organizational gaps listed above, but also the risk aversion of financial institutions who, themselves, lack the 
technical and operational capacity to deliver suitable products to their potential clients. In this regard, cooperatives also play a key role as intermediary, as a cooperative will always be  in a better solvency position than individual 
farmers. In addition, access to financial services that would enable climate resilient, low -carbon practices is unequal 
among men, women, and vulnerable groups. As a result of these gaps, commodities produced 
using climate 
resilient, low -carbon technologies are not yet reaching markets to their full potential.


---
*Page 20*
---

B B B B 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0  | PAGE 16 
 
  
76 ASDS,2010- 2020, p.47  
77 JoAnn Jaffe and Terra Brockett, 2016  Barrier  4: There is a mismatch between supply and demand of finance to support smallholders and their 
organizations  in the transition towards climate resilience/low carbon pathways.  
58. There are clear information gaps that prevent climate resilient, low -carbon commodities from reaching markets and 
being profitable. In the more organized value chains such as coffee, tea and dairy, market exchanges and buyers 
are not yet aware of the potential value added of such products. Financial institutions are also not fully informed of the potential profitability of climate resilient, low -carbon value chains. There are no labelling schemes for climate 
resilient, low -carbon products, and as such, because they are not identified, they do not necessarily fetch a better 
price unless their comparatively better quality can be demonstrated. This then feeds into the risk aversion of 
farmers, who face costs for adoption of technologies, but no guarantee of inc reased income – and of financial 
institutions as well. The comparison has not yet been attempted, mainly because the awareness and knowledge among buyers is not yet sufficient – and in turn this lack of awareness is also partly driven by the policy gaps ab ove 
(national and county level), that are not yet driving markets towards climate resilient, low -carbon products.  
59. In the less organized value chains, because farmers operate in a less aggregated manner, links to markets are also not fully developed. Individual farmers or small groups must then contend with prices, supply, and quality on their own, and this creates risks and costs, which are compounded by the inefficiencies in public service delivery 
mentioned above. Cooperatives, big and small alike, must be empowered to reach buyers and customers and to 
accurately market their climate resilient, low -carbon products for the transition to be financially viable. Furthermore, 
financial institutions also face barriers in reaching potential smallholder clients: high transaction costs and the 
difficulties in identifying suitable business cases also compound accessibility barriers (such as the requirements for collateral and high interest rates). Financial institutions need to be strengthened so they can identify and cater to 
potentially successful business organizations and farmers and support them with accessible finance to s atisfy 
market demand.  
60. Taken together, these barriers and gaps require a coordinated approach where governments and private sector 
learn to work towards a shared purpose, each according to their comparative advantage, towards climate resilient, low-carbon value chains and ultimately, a transformation of the local economy.  
Theory of Change  
This project is premised on the following theory of change:  
61. Smallholder farmers in the LREB are currently and will be vulnerable to the impacts of climate variability and climate change, regardless of the climate scenario that materializes. This vulnerability is due to the over -
reliance on rain fed agriculture, the land and ecosystem degradation that undermines agricultural productivity, 
limited access to climate -appropriate production and processing technologies, practices, information and services, 
and underlying multidimensional poverty.  
62. However, the same vulnerable farmers have at their disposal a set of adaptive mechanisms that, if used properly and disseminated at scale, could deliver significant resilience- building benefits . Among these adaptive 
mechanisms, cooperatives serve as risk sharing and risk reduction mechanism for members, and access to growing 
county government capacity in dealing with climate risks.  The cooperative movement is quite vibrant in Kenya. 
Agricultural cooperatives play a significant role in Kenya’s agricultural d
evelopment, accounting for 46% of all 
registered cooperatives with approximately 7 million members.76 Playing various roles, agricultural cooperatives 
help access inputs,  improve members’ position in the market chain, help farmers achieve economies of scale, increase production and productivity of members, provide extension support services, and market access for small -
scale farmers,  help households diversify livelihoods and crops, all of which contribute to boosting farmer 
resilience.77 (please refer to FS, Part B, section 6.1.3 for more about cooperatives).  
63. In addition to this, agricultural development in the region runs the risk of becoming a significant source of 
emissions given the current pressures towards the expansion of cropland at the expense of forests, the 
degradation of existing agricultural landscapes and marginal areas due to unsustainable land use practices, and 
inefficient practices in key emitting sectors such as livestock.  
64. To adequately steer the agriculture sector and development pathway towards a climate resilient, low carbon 
trajectory, taking advantage of the existing strengths of the cooperative movement will be key – while filling the 
remaining capacity gaps and barriers, at individual, institutional and ecosystem levels.  
65. Therefore, IF vulnerable smallholders and value chain actors have access to technologies, markets and finance to support production, processing and marketing of targeted VCs, and are supported by improved climate information, 
extension, and climate -resilie nt landscapes, THEN they will become more resilient to the impacts of climate change


---
*Page 21*
---

B B B B 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0  | PAGE 17 
 
 and emissions from the AFOLU sector will be reduced BECAUSE the priority value chains will be reoriented towards 
climate -resilient and low -carbon pathways, and farmer -led adaptation and mitigation actions will be supported by 
adequate gender -responsive public agro- climate services and public and private investment.  
66. The objective  of the project is to foster the emergence of climate- resilient, low -carbon, environmentally sustainable, 
and financially viable agriculture value chains by accelerating the transfer of technology, knowledge, assets, and 
services with a focus on agri -food cooperatives as key agents to leverage rural change.

empowering farmers’ cooperatives and groups as the service centers. Further, the project will facilitate the 
integration and coordination of the value chain actors by providing market access to the producers and financial incentives to the farmers and other value chain actors . The project will ensure the climate solutions promoted

68. This project applies the following key principles and concepts:  
69. Locally -driven approach: This project will invest in vulnerable smallholder farmers through involving local 
communities in a participatory seasonal  planning process and in identifying effective climate solutions and 
enhancing their access to technologies, market and finance. The project will enhance the local government’s 
capacities to serve the target ed communities including in providing weather information and agro- climate services. 
The project aims at increasing adaptive capacity and strengthening public agro -climate services, w hich would 
support locally -led climate action.   
70. Financial viability for sustainability : This project considers that farmers, be they smallholders, will seek visible 
benefit from their activities, be they food security or monetary. The aim is to ensure that smallholders are resilient, 
that agriculture does not generate emissions increases, and that farming becomes financially profitable. Financial 
viability and sustainability will be key to ensuring the continuation of locally -driven  climate action, and to accelerating 
the pathway to broader adoption and upscaling.  
71. Technology transfer : A key pillar of this project is the opportunity to accelerate the transition towards CRLCSA 
through technology transfer, leveraging the knowledge, know -how, tools, and expertise of farmers in Kenya and 
abroad and deploying farmer to farmer peer support s ystems. The project will include a mechanism through which 
farmers located in other parts of Kenya, Cooperative Partnership countries or elsewhere, will be able to transfer their knowledge and technology on adaptation and mitigation to l ocal farmers in the targeted value chains.  
72. Smallholder aggregation : Leveraging cooperatives, Farmer Organizations (FO), and their members, will help 
accelerate the diffusion of suitable practices and creates a strong resilience- building social network that will outlast 
the project. This can be accomplished by building on  existing networks and using data -driven, evidence -based


---
*Page 23*
---

B B B B 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0  | PAGE 19 
 
 tools and approaches. This project aims to create the conditions for complete transformation of key value chains. 
This includes working with value chain actors and stakeholders such as processors and buyers in addressing the 
barriers to adaptation and emis sions reductions. The project will implement an inclusive and participatory approach 
to all activities.  
73. Sustainable Intensification : At the heart of the climate problem in the LREB is the need to ensure a minimum level 
of ecological integrity and resilience, to allow for continued ecological service provision to the agriculture sector and beyond. This project will pay particular attention to trade- offs between the various dimensions of agriculture. 
The project will promote sustainable intensification, balancing the demands of a growing agri -food sector in terms 
of water, energy, and land, with the need to sus
tain ecological services for future generations throughout the 
broader landscape. This project will promote practices that increase production and productivity without further land 
expansion, while restoring or rehabilitating ecosystem services that suppor t agricultural production.  
74. Gender and Social inclusion: The project emphasizes empowering those who are most vulnerable in participating 
meaningfully in the transformation of agriculture, to ensure that they also derive resilience benefits.  For women, youth, persons with disabilities, and social minorities, co operatives offer inclusion that otherwise may not have been 
possible. The project will not only integrate Gender and Social inclusion perspectives in all its processes, activities 
and outputs (mainstreaming), it will also dedicate specific activities and budgets towards addressing gender -based 
or socially -based inequities, in the particular context of addressing climate risks.

B.3. Project/programme description (max. 2500 words, approximately 5 pages)  
75. The project is structured around four components and 5 connected outcomes . Component 1 ( Outcome  1) 
improves the institutional context that would support climate resilient, low carbon, and environmentally sustainable 
value chains. The purpose of this outcome is to support local stakeholders, including particularly county 
administrations, in becoming fac ilitators for the upscaling of CRLCSA value chains. This will require strengthening 
the capacity of county government stakeholders in the extension and cooperatives departments, adding to climate 
information flows and knowledge systems, and filling the remaining capacity gaps in technical capacity in line with devolution and the climate change priorities. Activities under Outcome 
1 support the upscaling and broader 
adoption of activities under Outcome 2 .1, 2.2 and Outcome 3 by ensuring that local communities, farmers, and 
other value chain actors have sufficient knowledge and adoption capacities climate resilient practices and 
awareness the needs for the climate change actions.  
76. Component 2 ( Outcomes 2 .1 and 2.2)  achieves direct emission reductions and support the conservation of 
ecosystem services in the face of multiple threats, including climate variability and climate change impacts, 
population pressure and agricultural land expansion. Under this Component , which is mainly implemented by the 
Government of Kenya through the county administrations themselves, a vision for resilient, low carbon agriculture landscapes will be formulated to inform future land use planning and land allocation based on climate change impact and risk assessments. This strategy will then be implemented and monitored in targeted areas.  
77. Component  3 (Outcome 3)  delivers adaptation and emissions benefits from the agri -food sector through the 
dissemination and upscale of technologies, assets, knowledge, practices, and services related to climate- resilient, 
low-carbon, environmentally sustainable agriculture practices in the 6 value chains. This is reaching smallholder 
farmers through community -based associations and organizations, and cooperatives.  
78. Finally, Component 4 ( Outcome 4) builds on the realization that financial viability of interventions is key to ensuring 
long-term adoption, scaling and replication, something that had been missing in previous interventions.  Activities 
support the reorientation of business and market practices towards the adoption of climate resilient and low carbon models, and creates conditions for long- term sustainability of the project by supporting cooperatives and farmer 
organizations in becoming financially sound, au
tonomous, and profitable climate resilient and low carbon 
businesses.  
79. Locally, although the project covers a large territory, project activities will be deployed in a coordinated manner. 
Vulnerability hotspots will be identified through Outcome 2, which will guide the prioritization of landscape 
management strategies and the selection of participating smallholders and farmer organizations. Smallholders will 
be reached through existing aggregations, such as informal groups, farmer field schools or cooperatives.  Linkages between 
cooperatives working in complementary value chains will also be pursued. This targeting approach 
ensures linkages between farmers, cooperatives and the overall agricultural landscapes in which they are nested, 
for mutually reinforcing adaptation and mitigation benefits.  Please refer to the FS, Part C , section  6 for more 
information on targeting.  
80. Further detail on each activity and sub- activity, and a detailed description of the baseline for each output, is found 
in the Feasibility Study, Part C, Section 5.  
Component 1 – Enabling local government support for adaptation and mitigation  
Outcome  1 - Enhanced public agro- climate services support  farmer -led proactive adaptation and mitigation 
actions.  
Output  1.1 Local administrations deploy improved climate knowledge, extension, and methodologies to support 
producers and value chain actors.

*Page 26*
---

B B B B 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0  | PAGE 22 
 
 81. The purpose of activities under this output is to help county governments serve farmers, farmer organizations and 
cooperatives in a manner that contributes to the transformation of the agriculture sector from current pathways to 
climate resilient, low -carbon trajectories.  
82. All activities in this output build on a significant baseline of existing capacity, resources, information, and networks, 
and they are therefore carefully targeted to filling identified gaps. The six activities proposed here are based on existing county ca pacities, opportunities such as the availability of digital extension channels, and lessons learned 
from other projects with similar objectives (Refer to section 2.3 of the FS Part B). The expectation is that the 
approaches deployed here for the 6 targeted value chains will be scalable to all other value chains, regions, and 
sectors as well, transforming the way in which county governments interact with private sector to address climate change in Kenya.  
Activity 1.1.1 Develop and deploy innovative and efficient extension methods for disseminating and 
demonstrating CRLCSA knowledge, technologies, and practices in gender -responsive and socially inclusive 
ways.  
83. The first step (sub- activity 1.1.1.1) will be to examine and analyze the state of extension services in the agriculture 
sector and in the six value chains. This stocktaking exercise will analyze both public and private extension services. 
The purpose of this assessment is to determine gaps and challenges within and between counties and to develop 
more efficient and cost -effective solutions to respond to climate change to support transition towards climate 
resilient, low -carbon production and value chains. T his assessment will be conducted in close collaboration with 
the agricultural extension departments in each county and in the national government, engaging input by farmers, FO, and other value chain actors. The assessment will also consider gender gaps in delivering extension services.  
 
84. Based on this assessment, counties will come together to participate in the participatory design of new, innovative, and more efficient CRLCSA extension methods (sub -activity 1.1.1.2). Following the selection of methods or of 
improvements they wish to impl
ement, each county will receive technical assistance for the development of 
operating procedures, manuals, and guidance (sub- activity 1.1.1.3). Each county will receive training and capacity 
building, including material support, for the delivery and effect ive deployment of extension methods for the 
dissemination of climate resilient, low carbon, sustainable agricultural knowledge, technologies, and practices (sub-activity 1.1.1.4).  
85. The project will also ensure that gender and social inclusion issues are integrated within extension programs and 
within any support provided to cooperatives. This will ensure that existing inequalities are not exacerbated 
(including inequalities related t o decision -making, labour burden, and access to agricultural information, finance, 
inputs), so that no one is left behind from efforts to address climate change. This will include designing and 
delivering specific modules within and in addition to agronomi c training, which will include guidance on service 
provision to marginalized groups and the adaptation of services to persons living with disabilities.  
86. The deployment of selected methods of extension will be made in parallel with, and complementary to, the 
dissemination of climate resilient, low -carbon technologies to farmers under Outputs  3.1. In the final year of the 
project, an assessment of effectiveness and efficiency will inform the modalities for continuation by the county government based on both independent evaluation and feedback mechanisms between recipients and providers (sub- activity 1.1.1.5).  
87. Activity 1.1.1 will be executed by FAO . The project team will coordinate  with county departments of agriculture, 
extension, climate change, environment, and cooperatives, with support from the project in the form of technical assistance and training.  
Activity 1.1.2. Strengthen the dissemination of climate information services to last- mile users including 
women, youth and P ersons living with disabilities ( PLWD) through cooperatives and F armer Organizations . 
88. A similar process will be followed under activity 1.1.2, focused on the delivery of climate information services. The 
baseline assessment undertaken through the Cooperative Survey and Climate Change Survey in 2022 illustrated 
that men and women farmers are not yet receiving the full scope of necessary climate information services to 
enable adequate climate risk management and timely choices at both short (e.g., intra- seasonal) and long planning 
horizons (e.g., multi -year business planning).  
89. Therefore, the project will, in line with improvements to extension services, seek to improve the quality, reach, 
usefulness and timeliness of climate information transmission to last mile- users. This will include building the 
capacity of devolved climate change units and decentralized meteorological offices to develop and disseminate 
locally tailored, user -friendly and value chain specific climate information services.  
90. Based on a detailed stock -take and gap analysis in each county for the 6 value chains (sub- activity 1.1.2.1), the 
project will provide assistance towards the development or revision of existing climate information services ( CIS)

 including  short - and long -term projections,  seasonal forecast , agro-weather based forecast  and nowcasting . and 
for improvements in the quality and availability of real -time weather and climate information, in cooperation with 
national and county meteorological departments (sub- activity 1.1.2.2). The stock -taking report will also identify the 
most effective and cost-efficient method for dissemination in the 6 value chains, and the project will (under 
Outcome 3) ensure that last -mile users are enabled to access the CIS and to understand the information. Training 
will be provided to agro- meteorological department staff on the development and delivery of climate information 
services to FOs and cooperatives using the extension methods developed under activity 1.1.1 (sub- activity 1.1.2.3).  
91. To increase the use of the climate and weather information, the project will support seasonal planning at the ward 
level to develop locally relevant and tailored seasonal advisories in the targeted value chains and natural resources 
management (sub- activit y 1.1.2.4). Climate information will be communicated through FOs and cooperatives, and 
by making use of digital agriculture solutions such as cell -phone based groups (e.g., WhatsApp distribution lists) or 
any extension services designed under activity 1.1. 1 (sub- activity 1.1.2.5).  
92. This dissemination will require the development of a list of intermediaries for transmission of climate information to 
groups including women, elderly and illiterate or low literate farmers (sub- activity 1.1.2.6), which will also be linked 
to the update of  the agricultural databases under activity 1.1.4. This activity will include regular updates of farmers 
and farmer organizations distribution lists and maintenance of the cooperative census. Monitoring of the 
dissemination of climate information services w ill be conducted by county meteorological departments and will 
include surveillance of indicators such as type of product disseminated, method of dissemination, number of people reached with particular emphasis on traditionally marginalized groups (women, youth, PLWD and elderly), and frequency of use (sub- activity 1.1.2.7).  
93. The gender equality and social inclusion objective for Activity 1.1.2 is to strengthen the dissemination of climate 
services and information to women, youth, and PLWD through cooperatives and farmer organizations. Detailed 
actions for achieving this objective are to liaise with county -level and cooperative gender specialists and leadership 
in selecting the climate information service dissemination strategies that are accessible to women, youth, and 
PLWD (i.e., radio, videos, public campaigns, in- person demonstrations). This process will be based on 
consultations with women, youth, and PLWD on the types of climate services and information needed during 
scoping. Key among this will be to consider the dig ital gender divide in implementation, and whether women 
(especially elder women and women living with disabilities) have additional barriers to CIS that require additional support - for example, direct access to finance. Consultations with cooperative members from marginalized groups 
will ensure their differentiated needs, priorities, and preferences are accounted for in strengthening the appropriate methods for dissemination.  
94. Activity 1.1.2 will be executed by FAO . 
Activity 1.1.3. Develop and test methodologies for decentralized carbon accounting.  
95. In support of future upscaling and in direct relation to the implementation of the integrated landscape management 
strategies (Activities 2.1.1 and 2.2.2), the project will mobilize technical assistance to conduct training and 
demonstrations of carbon accounting methodologies at county level. It is expected that this activity will lead to the better integration of emissions reductions practices in future land use planning activities, green accounting, and may also support the creation of linkages between co unties and carbon markets. As noted in the previous sections, 
locally specific data on emissions and emissions sources in the AFOLU sector are not readily available, and 
therefore cannot inform suitable mitigation strategies.  
96. Among the methodologies being explored, the project will draw on IPCC guidance and any previous work 
undertaken in Kenya such as the System for Land based Emissions Estimation (SLEEK) platform under the 
National Communications processes, to downscale these at county level, with the supporting capacity building 
required. This will enable counties to undertake their own carbon balance accounting in line with the agricultural 
landscape management strategy.  
97. A first step in this activity will be to conduct a participatory design workshop with the 14 counties (Meteorological, 
Environment, Agriculture, Forests, and Land departments and CCU) to select and develop a regionally specific and 
well adapted AFOLU carbo n accounting methodology (sub- activity 1.1.3.1). A process will then be established for 
accessing and collecting local data, including remote sensing data, and computation of calculations (sub- activity 
1.1.3.2). This will leverage existing capacity in the climate change units, including such that was provided by the 
Readiness Project and G -FLLOCA. Operating manuals and guidance documents will also be produced to support 
county administrations and to ensure harmonized comparable reports across the 14 counties (sub -activity 1.1.3.3). 
The project will then support the deployment of two decentralized carbon accounting exercises, using a learning-
by-doing approach (sub -activity 1.1.3.4): at year  2, which will establish a baseline, and another at year  5, which

98. The gender equality and social inclusion objective for Activity 1.1.3 is to develop and test methodologies for 
decentralized carbon accounting in ways that are gender -responsive and socially inclusive. The participatory 
workshop with the 14 counties mentioned above will aim to have equal gender representation (50% women and 
50% men) and youth representation and PLWD representation equivalent to their populations at national level (20% 
and 2%, respectively). In addition, the workshop will include a presentat ion and facilitated discussion session on 
the importance of integrating a gender equality and social inclusion lens to carbon accounting and project 
programming (e.g., enables inequalities in access to climate services, responses to climate change to be reduced).  
99. Activity 1.1.3 will be executed by FAO.  
Activity 1.1.4  Upgrade and update agricultural databases, crop and productivity datasets, cooperative census.  
100. As noted during the development of this project, reliable data on production, prices, sales, cooperatives, and 
climate, are scattered across departments and vary among value chains and counties. To further enable county governments to better serve the transition to climate resilient, low -carbon pathways within the targeted value chains 
and beyond, data systems need to be improved and made more efficient. Complete and updated price and sales 
data informs development of climate resilient business plans for cooperatives and FOs, investment at both farmer 
and private sector levels and county plans in infrastructure and market development; crop and livestock production data that can be effectively related to climate data on a regular basis can also inform local c limate change risk 
assessment and management in activity 1.1.5 and help steer farmers decisions away from maladapted practices
78. 
Finally, accurate and up to date data on farmers, land users, cooperatives and cooperative unions in the target 
value chains can help strengthen governance, government outreach to end users, and facilitate investment in the region.  
101. Therefore, the project will first conduct a detailed data systems needs assessment (sub- activity 1.1.4.1). This 
will review the type of available data, channels for sharing the dataset, usage for decision making and barriers to 
access, and determine data gaps (coverage, accuracy, storage). The project will make recommendations to county 
governments on the improvement of these data systems in support of climate resilient, low -carbon agriculture. The 
next step will be to ensure that the database infrastructur e is adequate to support its upgrading – this may require 
the purchase of additional servers or computers, as well as the development of technical guidelines and the 
deployment of training for relevant staff (sub- activity 1.1.4.2). The project will make sure that sufficient resources 
for the operation and maintenance of servers/platforms, as well as internet access, have been allocated.  
102. Starting in the second year and up to year  5, the project will support counties in collecting, compiling, 
synthesizing, and analysing various datasets for the 6 value chains (sub- activity 1.1.4.3), such as crop production 
(loses and productivity) in relation to seasonal climate, land use per crop/l ivestock, processing and value addition 
data, and market data. This initial demonstration will serve as an illustration of how better data services can 
contribute to the reorientation of value chains. It can be ex tended to other value chains and sectors after the project 
is over. At the end of the 5th year, the counties will undertake an effectiveness review of these datasets through 
consultation with stakeholders and will continue supporting the priority data products using national budgets after the end of the project.  
103. In addition to this, because the cooperative movement is crucial to this project and to the transformation of 
value chains, the project will support the county governments to update the cooperative census on a biennial basis by recruiting consultants to revise the interview protocols, sharing available data, and creating data infrastructure, 
including lists of contacts (sub- activity 1.1.4.4). The first time, the project will assist counties operationally to conduct 
the census to ensure accuracy of baseline and create linkages between counties and cooperatives; following this, 
the project will support the counties in developing an automated process for updating the census through an online 
surveying of cooperatives that is tied to the annual registration proc
ess. The cooperative census included a 
thorough list of indicators that covered membership and participation, social inclusion, sales and income, 
environmental and climate challenges and resilience, and access to assets and inputs (land, water, energy).  
104. The gender equality and social inclusion objective for Activity 1.1.4 is to upgrade and update agricultural 
databases, crop and productivity datasets and cooperative census in ways that are gender -responsive and socially 
inclusive. The project will update databases with sex - and gender -disaggregated data and ensure that data on 
marginalized and invisible groups (i.e., women respondents living in male- headed households, widows) are 
included in updating datasets. Furthermore, women, youth, and PLWD will be tr ained on the technical aspects of
