105. This activity will be executed by FAO.  
Activity 1.1.5. Assess eligible climate solutions for the agriculture sector in relation to climate impacts.  
106. The proposed climate solutions included in this project are based on current knowledge of climate trends and 
projections, and on currently available technologies for the 6 targeted value chains. However, there is some 
uncertainty in the way climate impacts  will materialize in the long term, and there also will be evolution in locally -
specific vulnerability to the impacts of climate change, given the dynamic and diverse nature of the LREB economy. 
Technologies are also expected to evolve, as new approaches a nd techniques become available. The project 
creates, under Outcome  3, a continuous mechanism for technology transfer that facilitates adoption of adaptation 
and mitigation technologies by farmers and that will last beyond the project. However, this mechanism requires 
sound knowledge on climate impacts and suitable climate  responses, else farmers may be led into maladaptive 
practices, or to make ill -informed short -term choices. Therefore, the purpose of this activity is to enable county 
governments to provid e services related to the screening, assessment and participatory selection of suitable climate 
technologies that respond to the way in which climate impacts and risks are materializing locally.  
107. As a first step, the project will build on the results of the GCF NAP Readiness project and the G -FLLOCA 
program79 and deliver training for county climate change units, meteorological and agriculture departments on the 
selection, screening, and prioritization of climate solutions (sub- activity 1.1.5.1) considering available climate risk 
and vulnerability assessments ( CRA), such as those conducted under G -FLLOCA, and the screenings that were 
conducted during this project’s feasibility assessment. The project will support training in the identification of 
adaptation and mitigation climate solutions for the 6 value chains  at county level, though it is expected that the 
process will be expanded to other value chains after the project.  
108. Available Climate Risk Assessments (CRA), such as those conducted under G -FLLOCA, will then be used to 
inform a participatory screening of acceptable climate solutions for the targeted value chains, which will be 
conducted with the participation  of the Departments of Agriculture and Extension, Kenya Agriculture, Livestock and 
Research Organization (KALRO), and other research partners (e.g., CGIAR), as well as  farmer organizations and 
cooperatives. The result of this will be a government -validated list of cl imate technologies (Climate Technology 
Green List) that either promote resilience or help reduce emissions in the agriculture sector (sub- activity 1.1.5.2). 
The list will include technologies, approaches, practices, processes, and knowledge, with costs, documentation of benefits and links to potential providers. The list may be maintained and updated regularly; in the 5
th year of the 
project, the list will be updated based on any new incoming climate impacts, scenarios, and projections for the region.  
109. These “green lists” will be used under activities 3.1.1 and 3.1.2 by cooperatives and farmers to independently 
identify, and source climate solutions adapted to their own challenges, and (under activity  4.2) to guide decisions 
by financial institutions on lending towards climate resilient, low -carbon practices (reducing the risk of lending). 
Thus, the project helps build local capacity for autonomous adaptation, leveraging government technical capacity 
and private sector innovation and rapid pace of adaptat ion. These lists will also be shared across counties, and 
with the Council of Governors for broader uptake and replication (through activity 1.1.6)  
110. The gender equality and social inclusion objective for Activity 1.1.5 is to assess eligible climate solutions for 
the agriculture sector in relation to climate impacts in ways that are gender -responsive and socially inclusive. The 
project will include wome n, youth, and PLWD in climate solution workshops for futures planning and mobilize 
women’s and youth groups in generating climate solutions and county -level advocacy. Additional detailed actions 
for achieving this objective are listed in the Gender Action Plan.  
111. This activity will be executed by FAO.

In particular, the project will work with counties, cooperatives and smallholder farmers and other value chain actors to document and identify upscaling opportunities of lessons learned and factors of success that would enable the 
transfer of project approaches to other value chains, sectors, and regions.  
113. To do this the project will leverage the already established Climate Change Multi -Sectoral Knowledge Platform, 
the Maarifa center and the Council of Governors, based on a Knowledge and Learning strategy that will be

Component 2 – Sustainable Resilient Agricultural Landscapes  
Outcome  2.1 - Reduced emissions from and increased adaptive capacity of the AFOLU sector   
Outcome 2.2 – Increased ecosystem resilience to climate change  
Output 2.1 Agricultural landscapes are managed under strategies that conserve, restore, and sustainably 
manage community forest and agriculture land, and reduce emissions . 
Activity 2.1.1- Develop county and regional climate -resilient and low -carbon agricultural landscape 
management strategies, including improved watershed management, land use planning, reforestation, and natural regeneration.  
117. Under this activity, FAO will provide technical assistance support to each county government towards the 
development of a climate resilient, low -carbon landscape management strategy and action plan, which will then be 
brought together in a LREB -wide integrated landscape management strategy.  
118. The project will begin by conducting an assessment and mapping of agricultural landscape degradation in 
agricultural landscapes in each county, to obtain the latest portrait of the land in targeted areas and to determine possible vulnerability hotspots (sub- activity 2.1.1.1). Existing data will be correlated with physical observation and 
ground- truthing with communities will occur. The methods used for characterizing landscapes will be the Land 
Degradation Surveillance Framework (LDSF)
81 method and/or the Restoration Opportunities Assessment Model 
(ROAM), which provide a biophysical baseline at landscape level and assesses processes that contribute to land degradation neutrality
, including changes in land use and land cover, as well as help prioritize restoration 
opportunities and activities. This will be used to assist counties in prioritizing sites and types of interventions, to be implemented under activity 2.2.2.  
119. In addition, the project will develop and deliver a stakeholder -tailored training on climate resilient, low -carbon 
landscape management to ensure harmonization of approaches (sub- activity 2.1.1.2). The training program will 
include aspects related to monit oring of landscape and ecosystem health, related to the carbon accounting 
methodologies developed under activity 1.1.3. This training will emphasize the benefits of integrated landscape management or integrated watershed management as both an ecosystem- based adaptation approach and as a 
method that helps reduce AFOLU emissions locally.  
120. Based on the training and as a learning- by-doing undertaking, the project will support each county 
administration in the development of its own climate resilient, low -carbon landscape management strategy (LMS) 
(sub- activity 2.1.1.3).


---
*Page 31*
---

B B B B 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0  | PAGE 27 
 
 121. Participatory mechanisms will be built -in to the development and subsequent revisions of the LMS to make 
sure local populations, particularly smallholders who are currently using land for agriculture, have an opportunity to 
voice concerns. Attention will also be paid to the identification of tenure rights and holders, and any land conflicts 
that may hinder the achievement of project objectives. The LMS will include interventions on public or communal 
land (executed by county administrations) or interventions on private land (executed by communities with funding 
from counties and FAO) and will focus on areas where such interventions can facilitate or leverage improved 
productivity through ecosystem services. LMS will be submitted to County Governing Councils for adoption, which 
will create a further opportunity to raise awareness among county administrations and elected officials on the 
benefits of improved ecosystem management for economic outlook in each county, as well as integration into the 
CIDPs and climate change policies. The project will also support community awareness raising of the benefits of 
ensuring restoration, conservation, and sustainable management of their agriculture landscape (sub- activity 
2.1.1.4), which will feed into the capacity of communities to formulate proposals for financing under activity 2.1.2.  
122. Counties will then come together to develop a LREB -wide landscape management strategy (sub- activity 
2.1.1.5). This strategy will give particular attention on upstream- downstream linkages, fragile ecosystems that 
straddle county borders, population movement s and ecological trends nested within the broader Lake Victoria 
Basin. The purpose of this LREB strategy will be to harmonize action under a common objective and to pool 
resources for implementation later on. Counties will then be brought together at regul ar intervals for lessons 
sharing. The Council of Governors will participate in these events to make sure the benefits from integrated planning are integrated into the best practices for devolution. During the last year, the project will support an evaluation and lessons learned initiative that will inform the development of the next iteration of the LMS.  
123. The project will ensure women, youth, and PLWD are consulted in the development of the landscape 
management strategy and implementation plan. The project will also train women, youth, and PLWD to implement and monitor landscape management plans. Additional  detailed actions for achieving this objective are listed in the 
Gender Action Plan.  
124. This activity will be executed by FAO in consultation  with the Government of Kenya, including the National 
Treasur y and the agriculture, environment, and forestry ministries at national and county levels.  
Activity 2.1.2. Implement and monitor climate -resilient and low -carbon landscape management plans.  
125. This activity is financed through co- financing from the Government of Kenya (through National Treasury  
governments, leveraging resources from the G -FLLOCA program) and FAO (through FAO -GEF7 Mt. Elgon 
Restoration Project) . The identification  of sites in which to implement priority activities will be undertaken by 
counties based on the results of the assessments of landscape degradation undertaken in activity 2,1,1. Methods 
used for these assessments include  the Restoration opportunities Assessment Methodology (ROAM) which is 
participatory.  
126. Selection and prioritization of the sub- projects is conducted using pre- established inclusion and exclusion 
criteria as detailed in the G -FLLOCA operations manuals , and requests for finance are submitted to the National 
Treasury  G-FLLOCA unit  for approval . Reporting on implementation also occurs through the National Treasury . 
The interventions that address climate change challenges and are eligible for inclusion in a LMS (GCF) or a Climate Change Action Plan (G -FLLOCA)  include: 
 
• Reforestation at spring heads or in degraded sensitive areas including gazetted forests.   
• Reforestation and creation of woodlots  
• Restoration of water catchments and watershed management   
• Community -Based Sustainable Land Management  
• Forest Landscape Restoration   
• Community -Based sustainable forestry  
• Conservation and assisted natural regeneration.   
• Conversion to agro- forestry or permaculture  
• Construction or upgrade of anti- erosive structures   
• Afforestation, erosion control, gully healing in extremely degraded areas   
• Construction or upgrade of water conservation, irrigation efficiency infrastructure  
• Wetland conservation, reconstruction, or rehabilitation   
• Restoration of riparian areas under cultivation (private land)  
• Construction/repair of community water retention ponds, weirs, dikes   
• Setting aside land (public community) as micro- reserves e.g., smaller papyrus wetlands around LVB and 
other water bodies   
• Removal of wastes, reduction of effluents and pollution

127. The project will support the development of implementation plans and monitoring and evaluation plans for each 
landscape management strategy (sub- activity 2.1.2.1) while ensuring local community buy -in and participation 
through consultations (sub- activity 2 .1.2.2).  
128. Using GCF funding, e ach county will then implement selected reforestation, conservation, restoration, 
afforestation, watershed management, forest landscape restoration and/or improved land management actions in 
degraded hotspots of 200 ha per county around the agricultural landscapes targeted by this project (sub- activity 
2.1.2.3), and in gazetted forests and reserves (10,000 ha) (sub- activity 2.1.2.4) over the 6 years of project 
implementation for a total of 12,800 ha82. Environmental and Social Safeguards and risk analyses will be conducted 
before implementation and environmental management plans will be developed as needed. Project proceeds will only be used to support the implementation of activities that fall within the Category B of risk assessment.  
129. Modalities for implementation will include community -based delivery and direct county -government execution. 
Counties will also report  annual ly on the implementation of their LMS (sub- activity 2.1.2.5)  to the  National Treasury , 
including on the number and type of projects under implementation and funds disbursed, social and environmental 
indicators such as number of participating women and youth, area set aside for conservation, area under 
restoration, type of ecological system under improved management (e.g., wetland, forest). After 6 years, counties 
will undertake a new survey and assessment of the landscape to fully capture the results and inform the next iteration of the strategy.  
130. The Government of Kenya acting through National Treasury will execute th e sub-activities  co-financed by the 
Government of Kenya given its mandate to mobilize resources to counties, determine procedures and requirements for accessing resources, eligibility and disbursement, and ensure fiduciary standards.   The counties will develop 
proposals to apply for the finance through the National Treasury. The N ational Treasury  will review the proposal 
and decide if the requested finance will be disbursed to the count ies to support activities in the selected sites .  To 
access G- FLLoCA finance from the National Treasury, County Governments need to meet several criteria
83 and 
demonstrate Minimum Performance Conditions (MPC).  
131. FAO will execute the  FAO co-financed sub- activity  2.1.2.4  while the rest of sub- activities under Activity 2.1.2 
will be founded and executed by the Government of Kenya.  
Component 3 – Resilient livelihoods  
Outcome  3 - Increased climate resilience of smallholders' livelihoods using climate -resilient, low carbon 
technologies.  
Output 3.1 Vulnerable smallholders adopt gender -responsive and socially- inclusive climate resilient and low 
carbon production and processing practices, technologies, assets, and risk reduction mechanisms.  
132. The project has pre- identified a suite of adaptation and mitigation technologies that will be promoted for uptake 
by smallholder farmers in the 6 value chains (Refer to FS section 5.3). These technologies will be included in the 
curriculum of farmer field schools under activity 3.1.1 and transferred through cooperatives under activity 3.1.2. 
This initial “Green -List” of technologies, which is based on best available knowledge, consultations, and research, 
is to be updated by counties regularly throughout the project, also leveraging consultations with smallholder farmers to ensure buy -in and increase adoption rates.  
Activity 3.1.1 . Deploy CRLCSA production/ processing assets and training to smallholder farmers, farmer 
organizations and associations . 
133. Through the FFS approach, the project will allow farmers to experiment with and uptake climate- resilient 
practices, technologies, and farming systems. Farmers will receive training through FFS for 2 seasons (2 years) 
followed by monthly visits from the FFS  facilitators throughout the project to ensure that knowledge the farmers


---
*Page 33*
---

B B B B 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0  | PAGE 29 
 
 learned during the FFS is reinforced, and that practices and technologies the farmers adopt are suitable to address 
climate variability and climate risks as well as the other community specific conditions, such as availability of inputs. 
All of the aspects  of the FFS will be climate oriented, meaning that the farmers will be called upon to make use of 
climate information services to select climate resilient and low carbon interventions, and to evaluate their 
productivity results against assumptions related to climate conditions, as well as against productivity and income 
results.  
134. A key aspect of Farmer Field Schools is to ensure the sustainability of the approaches promoted in current as 
well as future conditions. The FFS interventions are anticipated to enhance farmers’ adaptive capacity and 
contribute towards the development of more resilient farming systems. In this regard, the services provided by the 
government through Outcome 1 will be crucial in helping define what technologies each FFS group will adopt at a 
given time.  
135. In the first year, FAO will undertake, along with activity 1.1.5 and the Resilience Survey (see FS Section  6.3), 
a survey of participating smallholder farmers to provide a detailed understanding of climate resilient, low -carbon 
practices currently being implemented, assess technology and capacity needs (sub- activity 3.1.1.1). At the same 
time, a targeting and g roup formation exercise will be initiated, along with awareness raising and Free, Prior 
Informed Consent processes to ensure participating farmers do so willingly and in full understanding of their rights 
and responsibilities under the project (sub- activity 3.1.1.2).  
136. Participating farmers will be selected from the areas in each county that are considered as “vulnerability 
hotspots,” following activity 1.1.1 and the rapid CRA, which will be completed with the collaboration of counties. For 
criteria and methods for selec ting participating farmers, please refer to FS Part C Section 5.3.  
137. During Year  1, FAO will complete the development of the FFS curriculum and programs, manuals, and 
procedures of the FFS for the selected value chains (sub- activity 3.1.1.3), in close collaboration with the county 
extension department. This will include detailed information and guidance on climate risks and climate change 
scenarios, information on the recommended technologies (linked to activities 1.1.1 to 1.1.5) and how to implement 
them in the field, guidance on how to train smallholders, particularly thos e more vulnerable to exclusion such as 
women, PLWD and people with low levels of literacy. Documentary support, visual illustrations, posters, video clips and other teaching aides will also be produced in local language to support adoption and disseminatio n.  
138. FAO will train 3 Master Trainers (sub- activity 3.1.1.4) on the climate resilient, low -carbon sustainable 
agricultural practices promoted in this project; local climate change risks, impacts, and vulnerability; as well as Farm Business School Modules including leadership skills and entrepreneurship such as book -ke
eping or contract 
farming. Gender modules aiming at achieving gender -transformative outcomes will also be included, to ensure that 
all trainings build the skills and improve the position of women sm allholders (and other marginalized groups) within 
the value chain (not only at production stage). FAO ’s subject matter experts  will then deliver a training of FFS 
facilitators in each county together with Master Trainers. FFS deployment will be organized with local non-
governmental  organizations (NGO) and community -based organizations (CBO), whose tasks will be to schedule 
FFS training  sessions and demonstration activities, managing the FFS facilitators, and reporting on a daily basis in 
the FFS sites.  For roles and responsibilities of and selection criteria of the Master Trainers and Facilitators, please 
refer to section 5.3 of the FS.  
139. FFS roll -out will begin at year  2 (sub- activity 3.1.1.5). In the first year of the FFS training, farmers participate in 
field observation, analysis of the climate impacts in the communities, and identify solutions through learning and discussions led by the facilitators. In the second ye ar, the facilitators conduct supervisory checks and visits, and 
organizes field days and farm visits among members to accelerate learning and dissemination. The FFS will use a participatory action- research approach for the adoption of climate resilient and low -carbon practices. 
The FFS 
participants will also have opportunities to join exchange and learning visits  (at least twice for each FFS during the 
implementation period) . The project will support FFS for the value chains selected by each county over 5 years. 
Each FFS will comprise of 25 men and women, though in some VC it may be possible to find groups made up of 
only men or only women. FFS will be sequenced in three cohorts ( 2520 FFS). Each FFS group (25 people)  will 
receive around 35 trainings a year for two years.  FFS groups  will also receive targeted material support (sub- activity 
3.1.1.6) such as inputs, tools and materials for applying the various  climate resilient, low -carbon technologies. For 
sequencing and roll out of the FFS, please refer to section 5.3 of the FS. At the end of a cohort, farmer s who are 
in a position to do so (e.g those who can pay coop membership dues)  will then be supported to become members 
of existing cooperatives  (see activity 3.1.3).  
140. Activity 3.1.1 will be executed by FAO using GCF funding.  
Activity 3.1.2 . Disseminate CRLCSA technology, knowledge, and assets to cooperative members through peer -
to-peer networks and exchanges.


---
*Page 34*

B B B B 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0  | PAGE 30 
 
 141. Activity 3.1.2 is designed to transfer technology to farmers through cooperatives and will be executed by 
Agriterra using GCF and Danish MFA funding (sub- activities 3.1.2.1 and 3.1.2.3). The process envisaged for this 
activity follows a tried and tested process of technology transfer already piloted in Kenya by Agriterra, in which 
cooperatives receive multi- year support according to their needs and business plans, through a milestone- based 
action plan.  
142. The project will begin by identifying and on- boarding participating cooperatives. For selection criteria please 
refer to section 5.3 of the FS. Following the initial screening and selection, each cooperative will undergo a 
comprehensive Cooperative Assessment and Climate Clever Check (CA -CCC) which includes the following 
elements (sub- activity 3.1.2.1):  
143. Cooperative Assessment (CA):  The assessment includes an analysis of financial data, production, sales, and 
employment data, governance and transparency, benefit sharing, and will also include an analysis of processes 
used to transfer knowledge, services, and technology to individual members, with a focus on vulnerable groups. The assessment will also consider practices and barriers to social inclusion and gender sensitivity.  
144. C
limate Clever Check (CCC):   The CCC provides an assessment of climate risks on production and processing 
as experienced by each cooperative (current and future) and helps evaluate the soundness of any climate response 
plans. It includes an analysis of the climate change challenges from both adaptation and mitigation perspectives 
along the entire value chain, from input supply to consumption. Please refer to section 5.3 of the FS for additional detail on screening procedures.  
145. Agriterra will benefit from the climate risk assessments conducted by counties under Activity 1.1.5. Challenges 
and the solutions applied by the cooperative are ranked and compared according to a point system. The impact of 
challenges and solutions on the business case of the cooperative is also reviewed (intensity of challenge and 
appropriateness of response). Each cooperative receives a “mark” or scoring against the different criteria at the end of the initial assessment; the assessment is then repeated once the cooperative is exited from project support to measure progress objectively and in a participatory manner.  
146. The CA/CCC serves as a basis for recommending solutions to the members across the full spectrum of 
cooperative activities, focused primarily on climate interventions, extension services, governance, management, 
and finance interventions if they help to sustainably implement the climate interventions. The solutions will be taken 
from the list of adaptation and climate solutions proposed in each county under Activity 1.1.5.  
147. Support received by cooperatives takes many forms:  
• Support in the identification and implementation of selected climate resilient, low -carbon technology, including: 
hiring and training cooperative extension officers, consultants and agri -poolers, training, and technical 
backstopping (sub- activity 3.1.2.2)  
• Mobilization of inputs, materials, equipment, supplies (sub- activity 3.1.2.3). For example, the project may assist 
a cooperative in acquiring renewable energy technology or processing or packaging material, provided it will benefit the entire membership of  the cooperative. The project may also support the full cost of the rehabilitation 
or upgrade of cooperative infrastructure such as water reservoirs, storage facilities, shelters, barns, and 
hatcheries, particularly when those are assessed as maladapted to the impacts of climate variability and climate 
change.  
• Support for the development of climate resilient, low -carbon business plans and business cases towards and 
mobilization of capital and finance (under output  4.1) 
• Support for the improvement of governance and management particularly for meeting climate- related 
requirements, onboarding new members, disseminating information and benefit sharing, social and gender inclusion. (Activity 3.1.3)  
148. The targeted number of cooperatives is 130, onboarded in the first 4 years of the project. To support the 
implementation of multi -year support workplans under sub- activity 3.1.2.2, the project will deploy service providers 
or holders of technologies, knowledge, processes and goods or services that respond to specific needs identified. 
These partners will be identified using the Agri -Pool roster of experts or through open procurement. Screening 
criteria for inclusion in the Agripool are listed in section 5.3 of the FS.  
149. Because the demand for support is likely to exceed the available resources of the project, and to ensure 
equitable access to project support among beneficiaries, the support workplans will include a measure of 
prioritization. In this regard, a key part of the project’s upscaling and exit strategy will also rely on the provision of 
loans and finance services by participating financial institutions (as leveraged financing) to the more mature 
cooperatives under output  4.2.

Activity 3.1.3. Support smallholder farmer aggregation into cooperatives and other business units as climate 
risk reduction and risk sharing mechanisms.  
152. Activity 3.1.3 will be cofinanced by the Danish Ministry of Foreign Affairs. The purpose of this activity is to 
nourish the cooperative movement as a key mechanism to share and reduce risks among farmers, particularly climate risks and the financial risks farmers shoulder when adopting climate resilient, low -carbon technologies.  
153. Training and capacity development will be provided to farmer organizations with a focus on the management 
and governance of FO and cooperatives (sub- activity 3.1.3.1). This will help increase smallholders’ business 
capacity, a key requirement to ensure market access regardless of the form the FO takes.  
154. The project will encourage farmers to become members of existing cooperatives or to create new cooperatives 
(sub- activity 3.1.3.2). At the end of an FFS cohort training, smallholders who are able to meet the minimum 
requirements for cooperative membership (e.g. payment of dues, or coop- specific requirements) will be invited to 
join. The project aims to support 6,300 farmers to join existing cooperatives, and the project will also provide 
technical assistance and material support to cooperatives to facilitate onboarding of new members (sub- activity 
3.1.3.3).  
155. Field visits and awareness raising events will be organized to highlight the benefits of cooperative membership, 
by documenting specific advantages participating farmers have gained It is expected that the better performance of production and increased income seen among members of cooperatives will serve as a powerful incentive for other individual farmers who may be working in isolation. Knowledge sharing mechanisms such as those leveraged under Output  1.1 (Maarifa Centers, multistakeholder platforms, Lake Region Economic Bloc, Lake Victoria Basin 
Authorities) and other awareness raising mechanisms will be used to raise awareness of documented best 
practices within and outside cooperatives.  
156. To support integration of new members and the efficient management of cooperatives (sub- activity 3.1.3.3), 
Agriterra will deploy the My.Coop84 tool among beneficiary cooperatives. It was developed using International 
Labour Organisation’s (ILO) Materials and Techniques for Cooperative Management (MATCOM) Program.  
157. This activity will be executed by Agriterra.  
Activity 3.1.4. Support improvements in social inclusion and women's meaningful participation in climate resilient, low -carbon value chains  
158. This activity is designed to ensure that project activities, technology transfer and support to cooperatives also 
contribute to the achievement of improvements in social inclusion and in the participation of women, youth and PLWD in the 6 value chains. As noted in the Gender Assessment and Gender action plan, ensuring equitable participation and inclusion requires a more subtle approach that goes beyond fixing “quotas” and considers barriers 
to participation at various stages. The activity therefore aims to influence change in the way in which women, youth 
and PLWD participate in agriculture (beyond the primary production stage), benefit from their work (ensuring they receive appropriate remuneration and socio- economic benefits), and influence decision-
making in households, 
farmer organizations, cooperatives and community or county policies, where gend er relations and women’s 
positions are improved, and women’s entrepreneurship is boosted, contributing to SDG 5.1.  
159. Gender focal points will be identified in counties, and in each EE, as well as within farmer organizations. At the 
start of the project, staff in the executing entities, project coordination unit, and counties will receive mandatory training in the prevention and management of Sexual Exploitation, Abuse, and harassment (SEAH), Gender -Based 
Violence (GBV) and in the application of the FAO Grievance and Redress Mechanism (GRM) to handle such 
incidents and ensure safe working conditions for women, PLWD, and vulnerable groups (sub- activity 3.1.4.1). 
Trainings will be delivered to men and women on gender and social inclusion- related barriers and on ways to 
identify and address them. The sub- activity also includes delivering awareness raising and training for women, 
PLWD and other vulnerable groups on their rights in the context of the project.


---
*Page 36*

 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0  | PAGE 32 
 
 160. The project will also deliver targeted trainings through FFS and cooperatives in the context of the dissemination 
of agricultural technologies, following the tested Agriterra and FAO approaches to develop leadership, participation 
and meaningful inclusion of women, youth and persons living with disabilities (sub activity 3.1.4.2). The approaches 
are the Women's Leadership- Youth leadership trainings developed by Agriterra as well as the Gender Action 
Learning System (GALS). These trainings will focus on acce lerating the inclusion of vulnerable groups in 
production, processing and value addition, and trade. For example, during Women’s leadership workshops (which are also attended by men), the role of women as cooperative members, entrepreneurs and leaders are discussed 
and action plans are developed to improve women’s meaningful participation in the cooperative’s activities. 
Solutions proposed in such action plans could include, among others: changing bylaws in cooperatives to facilitate 
women’s accession to le adership roles, offering solutions to issues related to transferring ownership of trees and 
land to women, equipping women leaders with more knowledge, resources and support, ensuring recruitment of 
women extension workers and in non- traditional positions.   
161. At the end of the project, the expected result of these efforts is that more women, youth and PLWD can more 
easily accede to roles of meaningful participation in the targeted value chains. The project also intends to monitor gender objectives through all other activities, including through a gender impact assessment at end of project and 
the collection of gender -and social -inclusion related data throughout implementation (sub- activity 3.1.4.3) . Please 
refer to the Gender Action Plan (Annex  8) for detail.  
162. This activity will be delivered by FAO using GCF funds , in coordination  with Agriterra and the Government of 
Kenya at national and county levels, and in particular the beneficiary cooperatives and farmer organizations.  
Component 4 – Scaling through CRLCSA market and finance  
Outcome  4 - Climate resilient, low carbon value chains are sustained financially and upscaled by the 
adoption of new business models and practices among farmers and value chain actors.  
Output 4.1 Increased access to markets and profitability of climate smart, low carbon sustainable agricultural 
products  
163. Many smallholder farmers are producing using practices that are not well adapted to climate change, or that 
generate undue emissions, in particular in cases where agricultural land encroaches onto forests and marginal lands. Under Outcome 3, the project supports smallholders in producing more (and better) without resorting to land 
expansion, and to assist them in developing coping and risk reduction mechanisms in response to climate 
variability, climate change and climate shocks. However, at this stage the market incentives to sustain climate 
resilient, low -carbon production are not in place – that is, farmers are not aware they can obtain price premiums for 
climate resilient, low -carbon commodities.  
Activity 4.1.1 Work with buyers and aggregators to increase demand and market opportunities for CRLCSA 
commodities.  
164. The project will work on the demand side of the value chain to increase uptake of CRLCSA commodities 
produced by project beneficiaries. Profitability of adopting climate resilient, low -carbon technologies will be key to 
encouraging continued sustainability , replication, and upscaling. Therefore, the project will work with county 
governments and cooperatives, as well as other actors along the value chain, to develop market opportunities for climate resilient, low -carbon products.  
165. Demand for the products included in the 6 value chains has long been established, including through the 
targeting exercise undertaken at the start of project design (demand was a criteria for selection) and through the market study and value chain analyses  (refer to Annexes 23a- f).  Initial data shows that fair trade, organic coffee 
and tea, and vegetables fetch a higher price on both local and international markets.  In all other value chains, prices are currently set based on product quality and grading (e.g. avocado size, nutritional content).  However, 
more information is needed to establish rational grading and price structures for producers to negotiate fair prices 
for their climate resilient, low carbon products.  Therefore, the project will begin by carefully analyzing the demand, quality standards, grading norms and prices for climate resilient, low -carbon products, to inform quality -based 
equitable price scales and premiums for products produced with climate resilient, low -carbon technologies (with or 
without certification or labeling) (sub- activity 4.1.1.1). This will feed into a set of recommendations in each value 
chain on how to increase the market share of climate resilient, low -carbon products. The analysis will also help 
identify buyers of products supported by the project during project execution (examples for each value chain are listed in the Market Study and Value chain analyses under Annex 23a- f). It is expected that at least 2 buyers will 
be identified for each value chain.  
166. This process will also be supported by the development of information products, knowledge events, and 
awareness campaigns to increase aggregators,’ market intermediaries,’ and buyers’ awareness of the existence of 
climate resilient, low -carbon and sustaina ble commodities. Research and comparative analysis of quality, grading


---
*Page 37*
---

B B B B 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0  | PAGE 33 
 
 and pricing of products supported under the project will be conducted, to benchmark climate resilient, low -carbon 
produce against existing quality standards (e.g., the coffee grading system) (sub- activity 4.1.1.2). This analysis will 
feed into the developm ent of information products and into the development of the marketing campaigns for each 
value chain.  
167. To create further market opportunities for targeted commodities, starting year  3, the project will team up with 
relevant value chain actors (aggregators, intermediaries, and farmer organizations) as well as county governments, 
to develop and deliver a targeted marketing campaign for each of the 6 value chains, highlighting the benefi ts and 
attractiveness of the products under climate resilient, low -carbon technologies (sub- activity 4.1.1.3). This will 
include developing technical briefs for corporate buyers  and market exchange (e.g., The Coffee Exchange), but 
also consumer -oriented marketing products, video and print profiles of producers and cooperatives. The project will 
also work with county governments to develop their own ongoing marketing services in s upport of climate resilient, 
low-carbon value chains. Participation in trade fairs, trade missions, and funding to support buyer’s visits to 
beneficiary farms and facilities will also be supported.  
168. Finally, throughout its duration, the project will provide technical assistance and business mentoring, to the 
most mature cooperatives to negotiate sales agreements and contracts with identified buyers (sub- activity 4.1.1.4). 
This will include negotiation  training, advice on marketing selected products, legal agreement review, price setting 
advice and branding. This service will be on- demand only once the cooperative has sufficiently demonstrated that 
it has adopted the climate resilient, low -carbon practi ces promoted in the project, and just prior to being exited from 
Agriterra support. The development and incubation of small agribusinesses among less mature farmer 
organizations (sub- activity 4.1.1.5) will also be supported through co- financing from FAO by  leveraging the GEF -7 
Project, which deploys innovative business hubs to promote market access and services, including business 
incubators for smallholder farmers pursuing nature- based livelihoods.  
169. The Counties will then use the updated agricultural databases (activity 1.1.5) to identify and invite participants 
to marketing events, which will include market fairs, awareness campaigns, special trade shows, and showcases (sub- activity 4.1.1.6). Using t he data collected in the agricultural databases, the counties will develop seasonal 
market insights that will include sales trends, production trends (including qualitative and quantitative trends), and 
price evolution for each of the 6 value chains (sub- activity 4.1.1.7). After the end of the project, this service may be 
extended to other value chains. This sub- activity creates a bridge between the county government and Kenyan 
governmental institutions and the work of the project, ensuring that county serv ices to cooperatives also encompass 
market -oriented activities.  
170. This activity will be delivered by FAO using GCF funds.  
Activity 4.1.2. Increase access to various third -party certification and labeling schemes.  
171. The purpose of this activity is to remove market access barriers and create a distinct market pathway for 
commodities produced or processed under climate resilient, low -carbon processes. Certification creates added 
value and ensures better prices are fetched for goods produced at a higher cost for the farmers and processors. 
This activity, which will be delivered jointly with the above, will provide technical assistance to farmer organizations 
and cooperatives in accessing and complying with relevant certif ication schemes on a voluntary basis. Third party 
certification schemes that are being considered include Encert Organic, Kenya Organic Agriculture Network  or 
IFOAM, EcoCert, Global GAP, Rainforest Alliance, Fair Trade, and others, depending on the commodity and its market.  First, the farmer organizations will be identified based on current market access, maturity, inclusiveness, and performance across the CA and CCC assessments, and of course the rate of adoption of CRLCSA practices 
among members. A selec tion of 30 cooperatives and FO is expected during the project, starting at year  3.  
172. These organizations will be supported in identifying the most suitable certification scheme –  i.e., the one that 
would bring the most added value both economically and from a climate resilience or decarbonization point of view. It is understood that some s uch certification schemes may take years to achieve, therefore only the cases where 
certification is realistically achievable within 3 years of project implementation will be taken on board. (sub- activity 
4.1.2.1).  
173. Each organization will then receive targeted technical assistance, training, and expert advisory services (sub-
activity 4.1.2.2) to understand and apply the requirements, to strengthen reporting and transparency mechanisms, 
and to meet and document any legal or institutional requirements. The annual costs of maintaining certification will 
be borne by the farmer organization or cooperative. As part of the support provided under activity  4.2, the project 
will assist in ensuring that these costs are also included in the price- setting and business planning processes of 
each cooperative to ensure sustainability and continued uptake.  
174.  This activity will be delivered by Agriterra using GCF funds.

85 For example, the project will explore opportunities for FI to link with agriculture lending incentive providers such as ACELI. ACELI Africa 
Trust is currently offering risk -reduction incentives to member financial institutions in Kenya. These can reach 10% of the lending portfolio,

Activity 4.2.1.  Develop gender -responsive and socially inclusive private finance tools, procedures, and 
products to promote the upscale of CRLCSA value chains.  
175. Under this activity, which will be implemented by FAO in cooperation with Kenya Commercial Bank  (KCB) , 
Cooperative Bank and Equity Bank, the project will support the development of capacity within financial institutions 
to support climate resilient, low -carbon value chains. This activity will help strengthen and increase the supply and 
accessibility of fi nancial products available to support climate resilient, low -carbon agriculture in the region.  
176. Working with the three  FI, the project will support financial institutions, through consultancies and expert advice, 
in the development and roll out of new financial products or in the revision of existing products and services (sub-
activity 4.2.1.1) that are more tailored to cl imate resilient, low -carbon practices and local stakeholders across the 6 
value chains.  
177. Each FI will benefit from support for the development of procedures, guidance material and other 
documentation, as well as for the development of data collection processes to enable disaggregated reporting and tracking of benefits and beneficiaries. Traini ng will be developed for FI at headquarter as well as in decentralized 
branches in the LREB region, to ensure rapid operationalization of the products and services (sub- activity 4.2.1.2). 
Other technical assistance will include support for improving risk analysis methodologies for loans in the 6 value chains climate resilient, low -
carbon pathways to ensure that climate risks do not compound financial risks; 
improving accessibility conditions such as collateral requirements, repayment terms, interest rates; and analyzing social barriers to access to any of the various products and developing internal ESG mitigation procedures.  
178. The features of the new or revised financial products will include the following:  
• Targets application of climate resilient, low -carbon technologies as identified in the project and the county green 
lists. 
• Ease of access or revision of eligibility conditions, including size of lender, collateral requirements, to ensure 
FO and cooperatives, vulnerable groups have equitable access.  
• Value chain specificity  
• Increasing concessionality, including through the mobilization of guarantees 85, interest rate buy -downs, first 
loss guarantees, modified repayment schedules, etc. These mechanisms will be identified during the project’s 
first year and the project will support Banks in their negotiations or in leveraging agreement.  
• Contain or propose risk reduction mechanisms for farmers and farmer organizations (e.g., insurance)  
• Leverage digital technologies (e.g., digital payment; remote credit appraisal based on data analytics)  
• Be complementary to the current Bank offer in support of climate resilient, low -carbon practices in the 
agriculture sector.  
179. This technical assistance will be executed by FAO with funding from GCF.  
Activity 4.2.2. Support smallholders and their business units in the development of bankable business plans, 
with particular focus on social inclusion and gender -based access  
180. In parallel to activity 4.2.1, the project will also support farmer organizations, value chain actors (e.g processors) 
and cooperatives in accessing the financial services offered by the partner financial institutions. This will take on 
two forms: first, the project will deliver training on financial literacy and financial management to farmers, farmer 
organizations and cooperatives (sub- activity 4.2.2.1).  
181. Second, cooperatives who operate on the more “mature” end of the spectrum will also benefit from assistance 
in the development of bankable business plans and finance requests that will be submitted to participating financial institutions (sub- activity 4.2. 2.2). Kenya Commercial Bank  (KCB) , Co-
operative Bank and Equity Bank have 
expressed their strong interest in providing up to USD 10 million in loans as leveraged financing. The purpose of 
this assistance is to ensure that cooperatives have, in the long- term, the autonomous capacity to mobilize their own 
financing. This will bridge the gap or the mismatch between supply and demand of climate resilient, low -carbon 
finance in the 6 value chains.

 182. Activity 4.2.2 will be executed by Agriterra using Danish MFA grant funding, in continuity with activities under 
Output  3.2.   
Activity 4.2.3 Facilitate smallholders access to financial incentives schemes for agroforestry  
183. As part of the innovations introduced in this project, and to support continuous mobilization of finance and 
investment into CRLCSA value chains, the project will assist select cooperatives in the 6 value chains in accessing 
cooperatives in the 6 value chains in accessing carbon markets, payments for ecosystem services, biodiversity offsets or conservation finance.   Cooperatives selected for this activity will be those who show the most success in implementing CRLCSA practices at farm level.  In year 3, the project will begin by undertaking an analysis of the most promising avenues based on the rate of adoption and success of the technologies promoted in the project 
(sub- activity 4.2.3.1).  Options considered may include, inter alia: the ACORN facility, Net  Zero Adaptation Facility, 
Conservation Finance, Payments for Ecosystem Services or Biodiversity Offsets, or private sector impact 
investment funds.   
184. Support provided to cooperatives and their members will include raising awareness on the availability and 
opportunity for the various financial mechanisms (sub- activity 4.2.3.2) and developing training for participating 
cooperatives on the requirements of each selected mechanism (sub- activity 4.2.3.3).  This may include training on 
conducting farm- level carbon accounting, implementing traceability requirements, monitoring of carbon stocks in 
plantations and forests and monitoring biodiversity at various scales, and managing reporting requirements for 
finance.  
185. This activity will be executed by Agriterra using GCF grant for sub- activity 4.2.3.1 and 4.2.3.3 and Danish MFA 
grant for sub- activity 4.2.3.2.  
B.4. Implementation arrangements (max.  1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagrams)  
Accredited Entity (AE)  
186. FAO will serve as the Accredited Entity (AE) for this project. As such, FAO will be responsible for the overall 
management of the project, including: (i) all aspects of project appraisal; (ii) administrative, financial and technical 
oversight and supervision throughout project implementation; (iii) ensuring funds are effectively managed to deliver 
results and achieve objectives; (iv) ensuring the quality of project monitoring, as well as the timeliness and quality 
of reporting to the GCF; and (v) project closure and evaluation. FAO will ensure these responsibilities in accordance 
with the detailed provisions outlined in the Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA) between FAO and GCF.  
187. FAO role as AE will be attributed to the relevant offices and divisions in FAO Headquarters located in Rome, 
Italy (HQ), Sub -Regional Office for Eastern Africa located in Addis Ababa, Ethiopia, and the Country Representation 
Office for Kenya (FAO - Kenya).  
188. To fulfil the AE functions, FAO will set up a dedicated Project Task Force (PTF) in line with FAO project cycle 
guidelines. The PTF will be composed by the Budget Holder (BH), the Lead Technical Officer (LTO), Funding 
Liaison Officer (FLO), HQ Technical Officer and other officers, as appropriate.  
189. The PTF will remain independent from the Executing Entity functions also performed by FAO (see Project 
execution section below). In line with the GCF policy on fees adopted through GCF Board Decision B.19/09, the 
above- mentioned segregation of responsibilities within FAO will ensure that the Organization can independently 
and effectively perform the AE functions listed in the GCF  General principles and indicative list of eligible costs 
covered under GCF fees and project management costs.

197. Agriterra is a global farmer advisory organization that takes its roots in the agricultural sector and farmer 
associations in the Netherlands. It focuses on strengthening the position of cooperatives and farmers' organizations  
in counties including Kenya. As a member of the Cooperative Partnership for Climate Smart Food and Forestry, a 
consortium of Danish, Dutch, and Finnish agriculture development agencies and experts, Agriterra has been 
implementing technology transfer programs in Kenya leveraging expertise of Dutch agriculture experts on climate 
resilient agriculture. Decades of work with cooperatives has led to evolving a comprehensive model of technology 
transfer that leverages the experience of farmers to benefit other farmers.  This enables learning by doing, creating 
rich relationships and business partnerships, and developing locally adapted solutions creatively.

authority in selecting the sites and interventions and the implementation of the activities in accordance with G -
FLLoCA procedures . The Government of Kenya will not administer GCF proceeds as EE.

200. A dedicated Project Management Unit (PMU) will be established and hosted by FAO in Nairobi, Kenya with a 
regional antenna located in Kisumu. The PMU will be functional for the entire duration of the project. The PMU will 
coordinate directly with all relevant government departments, county administrations, external partners, service 
provides, NGOs and communities, and will be responsible for providing support to the execution of day -to-day 
activities.  
201. The PMU will be led by the assistant FAO Representative (program) and a National Project Coordinator (NPC) 
who will be recruited by FAO. The NPC will be responsible for overall project management and coordination with 
project stakeholders. The PMU will als o include finance, procurement and administrative officers, Human 
resources, legal and contracting agents; Regional project coordinators, program assistants, a gender and social 
inclusion specialist and ES S specialist, as well as M&E officers.  
202. Please refer to Annex 2, FS, Part C, Section  8 for more detail.  
Project Governance  
203. A Project Steering Committee (PSC) will be established to provide strategic guidance for the project. The PSC 
will be co -chaired by the PS of the Treasury (NDA) and the Principal Secretary of the MoA.  As indicated in section 
B.4, FAO is part of the PSC and as such is a party to the final decision- making process . Members will also 
include:   
• The Principal Secretary (Representative) from the Ministry o f Cooperative Development & Marketing 
(Ministry of Industry, Trade And Cooperatives ),  
• The Principal Secretary (Representative) of Ministry of Environment  
• The Chair of Agriculture Committee Council of Governors  
• The Chair of Climate Change Committee Chair of the CoG  
• The Chief Executive of the Lake Region Economic Bloc and  
• The Representative of FAO in Kenya  
204. The role of the PSC will be to: (i) provide overall guidance and direction to the project, ensuring it remains 
within any specified constraints; (ii) address project issues as raised by the national project coordinator; (iii) monitor 
project risks and the effectiveness of mitigation measures, and provide guidance on new project risks, and agree 
on possible countermeasures and management actions to address specific risks; (iv) review the project progress, 
and provide direction and recommendations to ensure that the agreed deliverables are produced satisfactorily 
according to plans; (v) review and agree with annual work plan and provide necessary strategic guidance for its 
implementation; (vi) appraise the annual project implementation report, including the quality assessment rating 
report; (vii) make recommendations for subsequent work plans to build on achievements and address any 
shortcomings; and (viii) provide ad hoc direction and advice for exceptional situations when the project coordinator’s 
tolerances are exceeded.  
205. The PSC will be expected to meet formally at least once every 12 months. Formal meetings will be scheduled 
and arranged by the National Project Coordinator in consultation with, and at the request of PSC members (with 
tentative dates for the following meeting being agreed under Any Other Business). Extraordinary meetings of the 
PSC can be requested by any of its members.  
206. The project will also be supported by a Project Technical Coordinating Committee (PTCC) . The PTCC will be 
chaired by FAO Kenya, and supported by representatives from the Ministry of Environment, Ministry of Agriculture, 
and the Ministry of Cooperatives, Agriterra,  the Cooperative Partnership, and representatives from private sector 
and civil society. A representative of I ndigenous Peoples will also be invited to participate in the PTCC, and attention 
will be paid to ensuring equal gender participation in the Committee. The PTCC will meet every semi -annually  using 
in-person and online platforms and its function will be to conduct overall Monitoring, learning, and reporting on 
project delivery, conduct regular risk management, and address any implementation issue prior to and in between 
high-level PSC meetings. The PTCC will provide technical guidance to PMU and to the PSC.

B.5. Justification for GCF  funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)  
209. From 2015 to 2019, Kenya’s economic growth  averaged  5.7%,  making it  one of the fastest growing economies 
in Sub- Saharan Africa.   The public debt to GDP ratio has risen considerably over the past few years to 62% at the 
end of 2020. Half of public debt is external. This limits the GoK’s ability to finance projects and activities to deal 
with the climate change crisis. Without GCF sup port for ongoing efforts to address climate change resilience 
barriers, the GoK cannot take adequate steps on its own. In addit ion, GCF investments enable existing efforts and 
transformative projects to scale- up by enabling private sector, cooperatives, and financial institutions to set the 
stage for large scale autonomous adaptation. The GoK has explored alternative modes of financing for this project but has limited the taking of large loans to investment programs for infrastructure programs, such as those delivered by the World Bank under the National irrigation program. These provide a solid baseline on using GCF resources to support this project.


---
*Page 44*
---

B B B B 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0  | PAGE 40 
 
 210. In 2018, the Kenyan government was able to disburse only 752.4 million USD in climate -related development 
expenditures. The same report indicates that international partners would need to sustain 87% of the NDC costs 
and that development partners were prov iding less than 1/3 of all finance tracked, concluding that “there is an 
urgent need for international investors to scale up their investments in adaptation sectors, which requires more 
innovative financing models”. GCF support can help meet this financing gap and foster the emergence of a healthy 
agriculture private sector, while taking pressure off the National Treasury in the face of competing priorities. The 
GCF resources will enhance the implementation of actions that bring positive impacts to the most  vulnerable 
communities and livelihoods in the LREB. Thus, GCF financing will contribute to faster economic growth and 
development in the targeted counties leading to the realization of the aspirations of national climate change policies 
as detailed in the NDC.  
211. Smallholders are, by definition, highly vulnerable on their own. Currently there are limited mechanisms whereby 
farmers can access financing to transform their agricultural practices into climate resilient low carbon modalities. 
Access to assets is low, ad aptive capacity is low, and aggregation among farmers is seen here as a way of sharing 
risk, pooling resources, and accelerating access to technology. All direct beneficiaries in the project are climate vulnerable smallholders. Some are reached through inf ormal groupings that either exist or will need to be formed 
for the project (e.g., associations, self -
help groups, FFS). Some are reached through existing groupings, like 
cooperatives. In terms of exposure and sensitivity to climate hazards, all are the same.   
212. All smallholders (regardless of their groupings) are vulnerable to climate variability and climate change: small 
farms in size and number of animals, generally subsistence production or limited surpluses, low productivity, little or no diversification. The main difference between coop members and non- members is that coop members have 
access to additional adaptive/risk reduction mechanisms (slightly higher incomes, the ability to access knowledge and markets more effectively through the cooperative social network). While they are a little more resilient than 
others, coop members are still vulnerable. That is why we want to encourage non -members become members at 
the end of the project (activity 3.1.3).  
213. Cooperatives vary in size, climate capacity and maturity. The number of members in a coop (coop size) is not 
linearly related to coop maturity, to climate capacity, or to the climate resilience of individual members - a large 
cooperative does not necessari ly provide more climate services to its members; a small coop can be more mature 
than a large one. Each cooperative has its own challenges and in particular challenges related to climate change adaptation and mitigation.   
214. We defined a resilience index based on the Resilience Index Measurement and Assessment Framework 
(RIMA) and the IPCC6 definitions of climate risk, adaptive capacity, sensitivity etc. This index is measured at 
household or individual level. It will allow us  to differentiate between members and non- members. By our simulation, 
using high- level aggregated data, coop members appear to be between 10 and 15% more resilient than non-
members. Non- members rank in the 40% range, whereas members rank in the less than 55% range. Because our 
target is a % improvement on the initial resilience score, we will also be able to measure increases for all types of vulnerable farmers, and also understand attribution to various variables on which the project has acted.   
215. GCF grants will be used to procure technical assistance, goods, and services to support smallholders directly. 
One group of beneficiaries will receive support through FFS and semi -formal organizations while the other will 
receive their support through thei r cooperative, building on the existing adaptive mechanisms and social networks. 
GCF grants are not used to develop the commercial capacity of coops but to increase their capacity to change the behaviour of individual members -  through knowledge/training, processes, institutional climate servicing capacity, 
access to markets, acquisition of inputs, etc. All according to the specific barriers each coop faces in addressing climate risks.   
216. While this project mobilizes grants from GCF and other partners, the concessionality provided to different 
beneficiaries will vary: individual farmer beneficiaries on the lower end of the resilience spectrum are expected to benefit from technical support and training, assets and other investments designed to reduce their vulnerability, funded entirely from grants with no financial counterpart contribution required. Cooperatives on the relatively higher 
end of the resilience spectrum will be required to cont ribute counterpart funding, reducing the use of GCF grants 
and leveraging private finance from Banks and other donors. The exit strategy for this project entails “graduating” 
beneficiaries from one level to another on the resilience spectrum –  at the final  stage, cooperatives who can access 
loans from financial institutions will be enabled to independently and continuously acquire and transfer climate 
resilient, low -carbon technologies to their members.  
217. No other initiative targets cooperatives or integrates business models aimed at making the economic case for 
climate smart farming in the targeted area of the project -- the Lake Region Economic Bloc. Coffee, tea, and dairy 
value chains are critical for Kenya’s agricultural economy and farmers’ livelihood, while there is a severe shortage 
of investment from both public and private sectors to assist the key players, especially the smallholders, to transit


---
*Page 45*
---

B B B B 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0  | PAGE 41 
 
 to a low carbon and climate resilient pathway. The investment in fruit trees, poultry, and indigenous vegetable value 
chains have been long neglected in the targeted region. Although there are increasing public investment in these 
value chains in the past decades, partially because of these value chains’ climate resilience potential, the majority 
of the producers and producer groups are in a small scale, and the organization of the value chains is fragmented. 
Furthermore, the GCF financing would also serve to create a model for technology transfer that is not currently present in Kenya, without which individual farmers would be left to make ill -
informed choices. Increasing and 
accelerating access to technology that can unlock significant climate and economic  benefits is one aspect of the 
project that requires upfront support, but whose benefits are also expected to outlast project execution.  
218. Given the difficult socio- economic context, and the introduction of fiscal adjustment measures to control debt 
(exacerbated by climate change, socio- economic, and environmental challenges), the GoK and the participating 
financial institutions seek the highest level of concessionality from the GCF that will unlock non- grant financing, 
including from direct access entities in the near future.  
B.6. Exit strategy (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)  
219. The exit strategy of this project has multiple aspects: the first part is an “internal” exit strategy that develops the 
capacity of smallholders to practice climate resilient, low -carbon agriculture and that, at the end of the project, bring 
them in to existing cooperatives so that they too can benefit from the added resilience and risk sharing mechanisms that a cooperative provides to their members. In this part of the project, vulnerable smallholders will access climate 
technologies and practices that wil
l enable them to increase their resilience, increase their productivity and 
production and increase their income. These farmers can then join cooperatives as a means of continually 
accessing climate extension services and also increasing their market acces s.  
220. The second part of the project’s exit strategy is making sure that cooperatives themselves become self -
sufficient in the financing of climate solutions. This is achieved by building their capacity to integrate the cost of implementing climate resilient, lo w-
carbon practices in the business plan and financial management of the 
cooperative itself. Ensuring that the costs of implementing new practices are reflected in the price of commodities 
sold by the cooperative will ensure that farmers do not face excessive risks in adapting to climate change. The project also helps cooperatives develop their own climate extension services, making them more cost -effective and 
ensuring that all members meet the new climate standards. These new services would be covered by existing membership fees and dues, as an added benefit of membership. Because the project also supports the creation of 
market linkages for climate resilient, low -carbon commodities (including labeling and certification), it ensures that 
farmers receive fai r prices, and that market demand is met.  
221. A third part of the project’s exit strategy lies in the link established with financial institutions who are ready to 
assist. By bridging the gap between demand and supply of finance for climate resilient, low -carbon agriculture, the 
project contributes to creating a lasting, replicable, and easily scalable mechanism that can be applied to any 
cooperative, farmer organization, value chain or region. This part of the exit strategy builds on the capacity building provided to cooperatives to articulate better business plans and funding requests and on the institutional capacity 
strengthened within FI for developing and rolling out more appropriate, accessible financial products for climate resilient, low -carbon agriculture (under outcome  4).  
Operations & Maintenance 
222. The project strengthens capacity, disseminates knowledge and assets to smallholder farmers and their 
organizations, county governments and financial institutions. Some of the project activities create requirements for in-project and post -project operations  and maintenance which are detailed as follows:  
223. Under Component 1 , the county governments will continue to deliver and disseminate climate information 
services, extension, data and other support to farmers and cooperatives on the basis of improved capacity built by the project.  
224. Under  Component 2 , the counties implement their Landscape Management Strategies using their own 
budgets. The cost of maintenance of rehabilitated, restored, sustainably managed agricultural landscapes are 
estimated at a maximum of 5% of the costs of initial implementation,  annually ( USD 225 per hectare per year). 
These costs include monitoring of land degradation and ecological trends, but also continued maintenance such as clearing paths, creating fire buffets, ensuring drainage and other regular land maintenance activities. Total costs of maintaining all project -supported lands (i.e., not counting any possible upscaling) are USD 45,000 per county 
per year.  
225. Under Component 3 , the project supports the delivery of technologies, knowledge, and assets to smallholder 
farmers through Farmer Field Schools and Cooperatives. Some maintenance costs may be incurred by farmer 
groups and farmer organizations for the project -supported assets  that may include greenhouses, irrigation kits,


---
*Page 46*
---

B B B B 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0  | PAGE 42 
 
 earth ponds and water cisterns, post -harvest storage, or processing machinery and equipment. Costs of Operations 
and maintenance will be factored into the business plans developed under Component 4, facilitating the integration 
of all costs into the pricing of commodities. Selection of technologies to be implemented by cooperatives and 
farmers will also be based on a careful consideration and integration of ongoing O&M costs. Costs have been 
calcula ted based on 3% of project implementation costs, equivalent  to USD 2.66 per participating farmer per year 
(total of USD 35,508 per year). These costs are to be met from cooperatives and/or farmers own resources after 
the project. Further details are available in Annex  3 (Economic and Financial Analysis) and the Annex  2 FS, Part 
C, Section  7.3.

 D. EXPECTED  PERFORMANCE  AGAINST  INVESTMENT  CRITERIA   
This section refers to the performance of the project/programme against the investment criteria as set out in 
the GCF’s  Initial Investment Framework .  
D.1. Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)  
226. Direct beneficiaries:  The project reaches 572,000 vulnerable smallholder farmers  whose 
livelihoods depend on any of the 6 VC in the targeted region. Of these, the project expects to reach at least 
80,000 individual cooperative members (project participants), with direct benefits accruing to their 
households (a total of 320,000 people based on the conservative estimate of 4 people per household). 
Other beneficiaries are smallholder farmers who are not part of cooperatives (63,000 participating farmers 
with direct beneficiaries accruing to their households, 252,000 people).  
227. Additional beneficiaries not included in the figure above include government officials and county 
administration representatives, who would add a total of approximately 50 people per county (700 
persons); buyers, who will be targeted under outcome  3 (12 buyers); and other value chain actors.  
228. For indirect beneficiaries , we have assumed that benefits would accrue to members on non-
participating cooperatives in the 6 value chains (1,342,140.0088), and that participants in the FFS would 
also transfer indirect benefits to extended family members and households in surrounding villages (756,000 
people)89. This brings the total of immediate indirect beneficiaries to  2,098,140 people, 50% of which are 
women, and which represents nearly 15% of the Lake Victoria Economic Block population. However, our 
estimate is that, in the long- term, thanks to the upscaling strategy and the presence of  Kenyan Banks 
willing to support eligible cooperatives through loans, indirect benefits may be accrued by a much larger 
number of people in the Lake region.  
229. The Gender Action Plan details how the goal of intersectional equity will be mainstreamed in two ways: 
participation in activities and the content of activities. Women, female youth, female- headed households, 
and females living with disabilities all have specific quotas that are required for each activity. Mainstreaming 
in terms of participation will be ensured by setting the target proportion of participants from each of these 
intersections to be roughly equal to the relative population sizes at the national level: 50% women, 16.5%  
FHH, 1% for women LWD, and 25% female youth (aged 18- 34 years). The male youth (aged 18- 34) 
participation quota is 25% and 1% for men LWD. Mainstreaming in terms of content will be ensured by 
integrating Gender Action Learning Systems (GALS) and gender transformative concepts into 100% of 
training materials.  
230. Please refer to FS, Part C, section 6 for additional detail on beneficiaries and benefits. The benefits 
that are expected from this project are as follows:  
Improving the  Health, well -being, food and water security (ARA2) of the Most vulnerable people and 
communities (ARA1)  
1) Improved adaptive capacity through increased access to climate resilient and low carbon 
technologies, market, finance, social safety nets and risk sharing mechanisms by participating in 
organized farmer groups/cooperatives.  
2) Improved levels of food and nutrition security through increased production of key crops and 
commodities that can either be directly consumed or sold.  
3) Increased incomes through improved production, productivity, value addition, and access to markets  
4) Reduced exposure to climate hazards such as droughts and floods from improved watershed 
management and maintenance of soil cover, and from increased access to climate information 
services including Early warning.  
5) Improved access to water through measures designed to promote water conservation, harvesting 
and erosion control . 
6) Increased gender equality and social inclusion in access to/control over agricultural inputs, 
agricultural finance, and agro -climate  information and services and agricultural extension.  
7) Increased women’s, PLWD, and youth representation and participation in decision- making in 
cooperative societies and value chains.  
8) Increased access to gender -responsive and socially inclusive adaptation and mitigation technologies, 
markets, and labour practices.  
 
88 As per Cooperative census, FAO and Agriterra (2022)  
89 https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10457-006- 9007-8  and https://link.springer.com/article/10.1057/s41287-020- 00323-7
