 9) Increased access to gender and social safeguards to reduce climate risks for the most vulnerable, as 
well as risks to women’s health, wellbeing, and livelihoods from increased engagement in 
cooperative organizations and leadership.  
231. Resilience in this project is a direct result of adaptive capacity and sensitivity.  To measure progress in 
resilience and to ensure the inclusion of all project -influenced variables, the project has created a specific 
Resilience Index that draws heavily on the Resilience Index Measurement and Analysis Framework (RIMA -
II90) which was developed by FAO . Originally conceived as a tool to measure socio- economic resilience to 
food- related shocks, the RIMA -II contains sub- indicators that can also inform climate resilience.  The RIMA 
index classifies data according to 5 groups of elements of resilience. For this project, we have retained 
those indicators that are specifically impacted by climate change and that align with our theory of change 
and projected impact pathways . Furthermore, we cross -referenced the RIMA framework with the IPCC  6 
definitions of climate risk and resilience, to include measures of sensitivity and adaptive capacity 
specifically related to climate change (as opposed to food- related) . The sub- indicators  include:  
• Access to Basic Services (ABS):  This includes access to water/sanitation, energy, markets and 
roads, health services. The reason these indicators are included is because they facilitate or hinder 
access to agricultural assets ( e.g., inputs) and therefore determine the ability to generate income in 
the face of climate change; they also facilitate or hinder access to public services and aid in the 
case of severe climate events, along with the transmission of climate information services.  
• Access to Assets (AST):  This includes assets directly related to the production of income from 
agriculture (an indicator that the project will seek to influence) but also protective assets, meaning 
those that can serve as buffers to climate impacts. For this project we have reta ined the following 
production assets: access to land, access to crop and livestock for production, access to savings 
and finance and access to processing and value addition facilities, as well as the direct measure of 
income from the  main VC commodity - and the following non- productive assets: access to climate 
resilient, low -carbon technology, access to post harvest storage and access to shelter . These sub- 
are all sub- indicators that the project intends to directly influence . Change in the measurement of 
these is expected as a direct result of project intervention.  
• Adaptive Capacity  (AC): This category also includes sub- indicators that will be directly influenced 
by the project and are limited to adaptive capacity in relation to climate change. Though all the 
other indicators also contribute to adaptive capacity, we have retained the following three dedicated 
sub-indicators: Access to climate resilient, low -carbon technology, access to education and training, 
and access to climate information and early warning.  
• Access to Social Safety Nets (SSN):  In this category we included only one indicator which is related 
to membership in a farming group, business unit or cooperative (to be disaggregated). In conformity 
with the project theory of change, membership in a farming organization serves as a mechanism to 
reduce risk and accelerate knowledge and technology acquisition. This sub- indicator is not included 
in the RIMA framework but  are added in for the purpose of this project as means of sharing climate 
risk or reducing climate risk through social networks, public or private. Furthermore, the sub-
indicator will help us understand differences in resilience scores among the different types of 
beneficiaries (members of FFS vs members of COOPs).  
• Sensitivity (S):  In the context of the project sensitivity relates to exposure to climate risk as well as 
to the ability to resist climate shocks, which may be facilitated or undermined by some underlying 
socio -economic factors . As noted earlier, overall “geographic” sensitivity and the climate risks and 
hazards to which beneficiaries are exposed, is established in section 2 . In this category we have 
included socio- economic drivers of sensitivity, such as gender and age, disability, current level of 
food security or malnutrition, and the dependence on rainfed agriculture, given that precipitation is 
one of the key factors to be  influenced by climate variability and change.  
232. The measurement will be made at household level and the measure will be made through a Resilience 
Survey of a representative sample of project participants at inception, mid- term, and final evaluation). To 
facilitate tracking of project indicators and deli verables, the survey will be designed at inception, and will 
 
90 The RIMA framework was created using the following definition of resilience: “The capacity of a household to bounce back to a  previous 
level of well -being (for instance food security) after a shock.”  While this definition (and the indicators that support it) do not exclude climate 
shocks, they do not consider slow -onset climate trends and indidual sensitivity/exposure to those, as a “shock.” Furthermore, the RIMA II 
resilience index contains two parts:  one direct, descriptive measure and the other indirect and inferential that looks at determinants of 
resilience.For the purposes of this project, it was felt that the second part would be superfluous since we are only consider ing the climate 
drivers of re silience and not the full spectrum of underlying causes. Please refer to https://www.fao.org/3/i5665e/I5665E.pdf  for further 
reading on RIMA II.


---
*Page 52*

 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 48 
   
 also include additional questions in order to minimize the reporting burden on farmers and the project. 
Please refer to Part C, section 6 of the FS for further detail on indicators and sub- indicators of the resilience 
index.  
Reducing Emissions from Forest and Land use (MR A4) 
233. The calculation of mitigation benefits for this project was made using two different methodologies. The 
FAO Ex -ACT methodology and model was used to calculate emissions from AFOLU activities in all value 
chains except dairy and poultry, and the GLEAM model  was used to calculate emissions in the livestock 
value chains (dairy/poultry). Overall, while the dairy and poultry value chains remain net emitters, the 
project achieves significant reductions in emissions intensity per unit of output. Globally, the proj ect 
achieves a net emissions reduction of 4,268,492.04 tCo2 eq by 2044.  
234. Mitigation benefits are expected from the following activities and technologies:  
• Reforestation, regeneration, rehabilitation,  and conservation of land under the Landscape 
Management Strategies (Output  2.1) 
• Integration of agro- forestry in existing value chains (Output  3.1) 
• Improvements in land preparation, tillage, soil cover (Output  3.1) 
• Improvements in water and energy use efficiency (Output  3.1) 
• Increased use of renewable energy, waste- to-energy,  and reduction of deforestation (Output  3.1) 
• Improvements in emissions  reduction per liter of milk, Kg of meat and kg of egg through improved 
productivity and feed/management changes (Output  3.1) 
• Reductions in emissions from food loss and wastage (Output  3.1) 
235. Key assumptions for the calculation of mitigation for each model are included in FS, Part C, section 6. 4 
and Annex 22.  
D.2. Paradigm shift potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)  
236. The fundamental paradigm shift  that this project fosters is the transformation of value chains from low 
productivity and low resilience to high performing, financially profitable, climate resilient, low -carbon 
pathways  through locally -led approach. The project leverages cooperatives as a climate risk reduction 
mechanism – not only a business strategy that enables farmers to pool resources and leverage better 
negotiating power on the market. This is done by strengthening the ability of farmer organization and 
cooperatives to self -identify climate problems and solutions and to easily access relevant adaptation and 
mitigation technologies to enhance proactively climate resilience in production and business. Ensuring that 
the technologies and practices promoted fit into the business plans and models of cooperatives means 
that farmers will implement climate solutions that make both climate and financial sense to them.  
237. Having cooperatives include climate technologies and extension as part of their business plans will 
ensure that climate costs are integrated as part of the regular cost of farming. Prices of commodities will 
therefore reflect the full cost of doing business under a climate change scenario, reducing the risk borne 
by individual farmers in adapting to climate change. The increased market opportunities fostered under 
Outcome 4 will further facilitate transformational changes to the value chains as farmers will  be able to 
fetch better prices, stronger bargaining positions and increased market shares, because both production 
and quality of produce under climate resilient, low -carbon practices will be increased.  
238. This project dovetails with local governance and devolution processes, recognizing that while climate 
capacity is stronger at national level, it is imperative to strengthen it at local level. The project generates a 
paradigm shift in the way in which county governments relate and provide services to farmers and agri -
businesses. Recognizing the limits and constraints faced by conventional extension services, the project 
builds the capacity of county governments to exercise normative influence on markets and producers by 
setting norms, providing enabling climate services, and assuring the maintenance of public goods, 
including ecological services that underpin agriculture. Conventional extension services will therefore be 
complemented by farmer -to-farmer climate extension that will be integrated into the business practices of 
any cooperative – as climate related services that cooperatives offer their members to sustain climate 
resilience, decarbonization and profitability at the same time.  
Potential for scaling up and replication.  
239. Climate change adaptation measures introduced in the project will support resilient, low carbon, 
sustainable, and competitive agri- food value chains in the long- term that can create a model for further 
upscaling to other beneficiaries in the same value chains or extended to other value chains and other 
sectors and regions in the country. Activity 1.1.6 is specifically designed to foster such upscaling, replication 
and knowledge exchanges. Under Outcome  1, innovative extension methods and climate information


---
*Page 53*
---

D D D D 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 49 
   
 services developed and delivered by county governments can readily be made accessible to other counties 
through the action of the Council of Governors or the Maarifa Center. The decentralized carbon accounting 
methods designed under outcome 1 as well as the rapid climate risk assessment processes delivered 
under Outcome  2 can also be extended to other counties. Under Outcome 2, the integrated climate 
resilient, low -carbon landscape management strategies can also be replicated in other counties, especially 
once the lessons are drawn regarding the impact of such on agricultural landscapes and agricultural 
production. All climate technologies promoted under Outcome 3 – whether through FFS or cooperatives – 
are replicable and can be scaled up. All climate technologies promoted under Outcome 3 – whether 
through FFS or cooperatives – are replicable and can be scaled up. The premise in this project is that 
participating farmers will see and document the economic benefits of applying these technologies as well 
as the climate benefits – in fact, the economic benefits may accrue earlier than the climate benefits. 
Farmers in the same value chains who are not participating in the project will be able to see – through the 
awareness raising and research activities of the pr oject – the benefits of climate resilient, low -carbon 
technologies; because the technology transfer mechanism created under outcome 3 will continue to be 
available after the project, cooperatives and farmer organizations will be able to access the technologies 
and knowledge independently (albeit for a price).  
240. All climate technologies promoted under Outcome 3 – whether through FFS or cooperatives – are 
replicable and can be scaled up. The premise in this project is that participating farmers will see and 
document the economic benefits of applying these technologies as well as the climate benefits – in fact, 
the economic benefits may accrue earlier than the climate benefits. Farmers in the same or different value 
chains who are not participating in the project will be able to see – through the awareness raising and 
research activities of the project – the benefits of climate resilient, low -carbon technologies; because the 
technology transfer mechanism created under outcome 3 will continue to be available after the project, 
cooperatives and farmer organizations will be able to access the technologies and knowledge 
independently (albeit for a price).  
241. The project also creates an internal scaling process whereby smallholder farmers can aggregate into 
more mature farmer organizations, culminating in high- performing climate resilient, low carbon 
cooperatives. Visible economic and climate benefits experienced by cooperative members are expected 
to create an attraction effect which combined with project efforts to remove barriers to aggregation, will 
support the scalability of project outputs. The project also creates a push effect, increasing the supply of 
in-demand climate smart commodities.  
242. Under outcome  4, all activities are naturally scalable and replicable. The project creates capacity in 
financial institutions to develop and deliver concessional finance to farmers designed specifically for climate 
resilient, low -carbon agriculture. This capacity and these products, which can immediately be deployed by 
the financial institutions to eligible beneficiary cooperatives (whose capacity to develop bankable proposals 
will also be strengthened), can be upscaled and replicated by financial institutions to other areas and other 
value chains easily and at very low cost.  
Potential for knowledge sharing and learning.  
243. Knowledge sharing and learning are fundamental elements of this project’s strategy. Under 
Outcome 1, county administrations, particularly those departments concerned with climate change, 
agriculture, extension and cooperatives, will be capacitated to develop new information and knowledge 
services with the participation of farmers and farmer’s organizations  – including climate information 
services – that will be deployed to last mile users. In particular, the project leverages farmers’ own 
organizations as vehicles of climate knowledge dissemination vs the traditional model of on- demand, 
government -to-farmer extension. Under Outcome 2, the project also generates and share knowledge and 
learning around the benefits of climate resilient, low -carbon landscape management in support of 
agriculture. In this regard, the project leverages the LREB Multi -stakeholder climate change knowledge 
platform and other mechanisms to promote knowledge sharing and learning.  
244. The most important part of the knowledge sharing and learning strategy lies in the development and 
acquisition of knowledge by farmers themselves, by leveraging cooperatives and providers of technology 
in Kenya and elsewhere under Outcome 3. This includes generating knowledge about specific climate 
issues and solutions, empowering farmers to undertake their own diagnosis and prioritization of climate 
problems in the context of agriculture as a business. Methods used for knowledge sharing and learning 
includ e trainings, demonstrations, exchange visits, community meetings, market fairs and county level 
forums as well as LREB -wide meetings and workshops, the Multi- sectoral Knowledge platforms, Council 
of Governors and Maarifa center.


---
*Page 54*
---

D D D D 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 50 
   
 245. The project also generates knowledge and learning for other value chain actors, and particularly 
buyers, aggregators and finance institutions. New knowledge products such as research and comparative 
analysis of quality, grading and pricing of climate resilient, low -carbon products, market insights, 
agricultural databases, will also be generated by this project to support future similar endeavours.  
246. Finally, the project includes sub- activities related to the evaluation and documentation of the 
effectiveness of major strategies promoted, generating significant evidence and lessons learned that will 
be disseminated and shared to all stakeholders through  activity 1.1.6.  Lessons learned, case studies, 
information products and other reports will be disseminated at county and national level and at international 
levels through the project website, print and mass media.  
247. Further details are found in the Monitoring, Evaluation and Learning plan in Annex 11.  
Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment 
248. The project supports the improvement of the enabling environment for farmers and cooperatives. First, 
by strengthening the capacity of county administration to provide climate- related services and market -
access services, farmers will be enabled to produce more and better -quality products that will reach the 
market more readily. The services provided that support this enabling environment include improved 
climate information services and warnings; agrometeorological advisories; council -approved lists of clim ate 
resilient, low -carbon technologies , improved extension services; market studies, market information and 
sales data.  
249. The approaches taken for transferring technology under Outcome  3 are intended to reduce the costs 
and risks faced by farmers when attempting to adapt or to adopt low carbon practices. First, all practices 
are tested and approved by county administrations in relation to local vulnerabilities (outcome 1). Farmer 
to farmer extension and networking reduce the costs of disseminating knowledge and practices, while 
ensuring upscaling to other areas and value chains. Because the costs of adaptation and mitigation w ill be 
fully considered in farmers business cases when proposed, farmers will not face any surprises or untold 
risks. Demonstrated profitability of climate resilient, low -carbon methods and improved access to markets 
further reduce costs and risks.  
250. The project also supports the improvement of the enabling environment for framers and their 
organizations to access finance that supports climate resilient, low -carbon value chains, by improving the 
capacity of lenders to develop suitable products, and by improving the capacity of business entities to 
develop bankable business cases. This portion of the project, under Outcomes  3 and 4, is a significant 
pillar of the project’s exit strategy that – together with documenting the profitability of climate resili ent, low -
carbon technologies – will ensure long- term sustainability of project outcomes. By also working with buyers, 
the project ensures that increased market space is made for the commodities produced under this project.  
Contribution to the regulatory framework and policies  
251. The project contributes to improving regulatory frameworks and policies at county level in each county 
where it will operate. Specific sub- activities are included to support the linkages of the project’s activities 
with the county’s CIDP and investment plans (e.g., the landscape management strategies) and Council 
Assemblies (e.g. approval of technology green- lists). The project also supports the implementation of each 
county’s climate change acts and action plans (refer to FS, Part B, section 3.3). Through the Council of 
Governors and the Climate Multi- stakeholder Platforms, it is expected that lessons learned with policy or 
regulatory application will also be extended to other counties and escalated to national level policy making. 
The project also makes an indirect contrition to the implementation of existing national and county policies 
and regulations related to biodiversity, water, energy and pollution.  
252. For more details on how this project contributes to national policies, strategies  and plans, please refer 
to section D.6 and section 3 of the feasibility study.  
D.3. Sustainable development (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)  
253. Direct benefits accrued by project beneficiaries include improved climate resilience through improved 
food security, increased levels of income, improved access to assets, improved access to food, increased 
access to markets, social networks, and social safety nets. Combined, the improved access to nutritious 
food locally, the increased access to markets and increased incomes, will result in improved food security 
outcomes for project beneficiaries. Resilience will be measured though a composite index speci fically 
composed for this project, comprised of many sub- indicators that directly relate to the SDGs  including food 
and nutrition security, among others . Please refer to FS, Part C, section  6 for details on how the Resilience

 Index is composed and measured. Project activities will have direct linkage to SDGs  1, 2, 3, 5, 8, 13 and 
15.  
254. The measurable co- benefit of this project is job creation in the 6 value chains, while indirect benefits 
include increased economic activity in the supply chains for agricultural inputs and feed value chains, and 
increased employment in the transporting and bulking aspects of the value chain. In particular, the project 
expects to create meaningful employment for women and youth within cooperatives, farming groups and 
processing industries.   
255. Additionally, the project expects to generate benefits to the broader population of the LREB through 
the implementation of climate- sensitive landscape management strategies, and the overall improvement 
of the economic outlook in the region.  
256. Other indirect socio- economic benefits include:   
• Improved access to education for children and especially girls (whose working tasks may be 
reduced, or through improved ability to pay school fees)  
• Improved health through access to increased, more diverse and better quality food (either produced 
or purchased)  
• Improved access to health services (increased ability to pay)  
• Increased gender equality  
• Reduced rates of gender -based violence and child labour  
• Increased access to meaningful employment for youth, women, persons with disabilities  
Environmental Benefits  
257. At the farm level, environmental benefits arise from the implementation of sustainable land 
management techniques, anti -erosion work, increase in soil cover and the combination of crops or crop 
rotation, as well as the judicious application of fertilizer, reductions in use of noxious agro- chemicals and 
herbicides. Benefits include:  
• improved land productivity and increased biomass production,  
• improved soil fertility,  
• soil moisture retention and micro- organism content,  
• increases in agro- biodiversity at farm and landscape levels.  
258. At the landscape level the project also expects to deliver, through the implementation of the landscape 
management strategies (output  2.1) combined with other, non- project interventions, improved forest cover 
and forest density, reductions in rates of run- off and erosion, improvements in biodiversity status, and 
improved water conservation and aquifer recharge.  
D.4. Needs of recipient  (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)  
259. The vulnerability in LREB is due to agriculture primarily being rainfed  and small -scale 
production. Therefore, crop production is extremely vulnerable to precipitation changes, causing crop 
failures in the short -term and production declines in the long- term91. Most smallholder farmers are 
particularly vulnerable to interannual rainfall variability and oscillating crop yield and livestock production 
trends, resulting in unstable and unreliable incomes. For example, a survey of smallholder farmers in 
Kakamega and Vihiga counties found that smallholder farmers strongly rely on rainwater to feed crops and 
do not have access to stable financial resources and credits from public or private supporting institutions92. 
Overall, increasing rainfall variability has led 10% of the population in the Lake Victoria Basin to suffer 
chronic food insecurity, requiring short -term emergency food relief and long- term development programs93.  
260. Economic and social development level of the smallholders and the value chain actors hinder 
their actions to responds to climate hazard and require the project supports to reduce costs of 
climate actions and increase  efficiency and coordination  between governments and private actors 
in delivering climate services and easing access to market.  Smallholders working small plots (0.2 ha 
on average), and/or owning 3 -4 cows or approximately 10 heads of poultry. They are characterized by low 
 
91 World Bank Group. WBG. 2021. Climate Risk Profile: Kenya (2021): The World Bank Group. 
https://climateknowledgeportal.worldbank.org/sites/default/files/2021- 05/15724- WB_Kenya%20Country%20Profile- WEB.pdf  
92 Karugia et al. 2005. The Role of Rural Factor Markets in Reducing Poverty, Risks and Vulnerability in Rural Kenya: The Case of 
Kakamega and Vihiga Districts. Strategies and Analysis for Growth and Access.  
93 USAID. 2018. Lake Victoria Basin: Climate Change Adaptation Strategy and Action Plan, 2018 - 2023. Retrieved from: 
https://www.climatelinks.org/sites/default/files/asset/document/2018_USAID -PREPARED_Lake -Victoria -Basin- Adaptation- Strategy -Action -
Plan.pdf

*Page 56*

D D D D 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 52 
   
 access to basic services (e.g., sanitation, education, health, markets). They also have limited access to 
climate -proof inputs (e.g., seeds, varieties, breeds, etc.). Approximately 75% of their income depends on 
a single commodity which is usually rainfall dependent or climate sensitive.  
261. In the LREB, yields are symptomatic of low productivity aligned to climate variability, high levels of loss 
and wastage in the post -harvest and processing cycles. Many farmers are producing with limited technical 
means, low levels of mechanization, with limited access to inputs. Most practices can be characterized as 
unsustainable mono- cropping land and water use, although many farmers are becoming aware of land 
degradation issues and its impacts on their productivity.  
262. Smallholders who produce surpluses (i.e. above household consumption) can pool production and 
access processing and value addition through business groups (e.g., Business units, coops). However, 
individual farmers have very little access to financing on their own. Cooperatives and farmers organizations 
generally also have limited access due to high requirements, high costs of lending, and the general low 
capacity in articulating bankable business propositions for financial institutions. Women, youth and per sons 
living with disabilities face increased barriers to access to inputs, land, income, finance and technical 
support.  Please refer to the Gender Assessment for further detail.  
263. This project therefore creates a system where governments and private sector come together to 
remove the barriers to autonomous financing of value chains through cooperatives. At one end of the 
spectrum, vulnerable smallholders are trained and capacitated in the best available climate resilient, low -
carbon practices for their value chain. They derive short and long- term adaptation benefits from these, and 
also have the opportunity to further reduce their costs and risks by joining cooperatives. At the other  end 
of the spectrum, mature cooperatives exit the project with members who are all practicing climate resilient, 
low-carbon agriculture, have access to improved assets, increase their sales and market presence, and 
are able to include in the regular costs  of doing agri -business the provision of climate services to their 
members. This gives them the leading edge and additional capacity to access private finance, and thus 
perpetuate the model autonomously.  
264. County budgets are limited and even though counties are legally mandated to dedicate 2% of their 
budgets to climate action, priorities are many, and the linkages to private agri -businesses are not enough 
to direct public resources to cooperatives. This is why the project designed to strengthen the capacity of 
county governments to serve farmers and farmer organizations in a supportive, enabling, normative 
manner, by providing guidelines, regulations, climate information, and advisory.  
D.5. Country ownership  (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)  
265. The project supports the implementation of Kenya’s national climate change policies, strategies and 
plans as follows:  
1. The National Climate Change Act of 201694: Paragraph 2 of Section 395 (which lays out specifically 
how the Act will affect Kenya’s climate change response), mandates the formulation of programs and 
projects to enhance resilience that are intergenerationally , gender equity mainstreamed and 
community led, while also contributing to disaster risk reduction. This paragraph also stipulates the 
need to promote low carbon technologies. All points are supported by the project’s activities. The Act 
of 2016 also follows the decentralization framework. The Act establishes the Climate Change 
Directorate, which coordinates and facilitates climate change adaptation policies and action plans 
government agencies between counties, and county level climate change units, which will be 
supported by the project  
2. Kenya’s Updated NDC96: Kenya’s 2020 Updated Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC) sets an 
abatement target of 32% of the business -as-usual (BAU) scenario. Under the BAU scenario, Kenya’s 
projected emissions are projected to increase to approximately 143 MtCO2 by 2030, which the NDC 
wants to limit to 32% relative to the BAU. Over the course of the project lifespan, the project would 
tentatively mitigate a projected 4.2MtCO2.  
3. Kenya’s National Adaptation Plan  2015 – 203097: The National Action Plan 2015 – 2030, 
coordinates climate change adaptation at the national level while also contributing to economic 
development to boost resilience. Outcome  1 of the project supports the mainstreaming of climate 
 
94 Retrieved from: https://faolex.fao.org/docs/pdf/ken160982.pdf   
95 Section 3 of the Climate Change Act of 2016, Paragraph 1 states “This Act shall be applied for the development, management, 
implementation and regulation of mechanism to enhance climate change resilience and low carbon development for the sustainabl e 
development of Kenya.”  
96 To be confirmed.

 change adaptation into all public sector offices with the trainings provided. Land management 
reforms that will be carried out in Outcome 2 support the land management reforms of the NAP. 
Outcome 3 further develops the bullet point #5 via the farmer field schools, cooperatives 
development, and gender -, generation- streamed climate change adaptation policies and actions 
plans that the cooperatives will develop.  
4. National Climate Change Action Plan  2018 - 202298 : The National Climate Change Action Plan 
(NCCAP) 2018 -2022 sets out several priorities and actions that needs to be accomplished including 
link climate change adaptation with development , and i mprove water conservation by reversing main 
water towers degradation and rehabilitating all catchments . Activities the project will undertake in 
Outcomes  1, 2 and 3 will contribute to implementing the NCCAP. Specifically, the transfer of 
technologies to farmers, the development and implementation of landscape management strategies, 
the dissemination of climate information services (CIS), will support the NC CAP priorities.  
266. For an analysis of how the project contributes to sectoral policies (energy, water, environment, forests 
and others) please refer to section 3 of the FS, Part B.  
267. The National Designated Authority (NDA)  has been closely involved in the design of this proposal. The 
NDA has participated in all  major  meetings and consultations and provided input at regular intervals to 
ensure that the proposal meshed with the government’s priorities. And the NDA will play a key role in 
continuing coordination with county governments, linking with the Council of Governors.  
268. This project is the culmination of a long consultative process that involved the active participation of 
many partners at national and county levels. At national level, consultation involved all key sectoral 
ministries, central government agencies and development partners to ensure consistency of intervention 
with major policy orientation and synergies with other initiatives, in particular G -FLLOCA and the IFAD RK -
FINFA program.  The lessons from past projects and coordination with other ongoing or pipelined initiative 
have been integrated into this design.  Please refer to Annex 7 Stakeholder Engagement Plan for more 
details.  
269. The project is deigned based on the needs of targeted population. C ounty level consultations took 
place on multiple occasions, starting with the initial consultative meeting, which brought together over 90 
participants (men and women) from all 14 counties (cooperatives, environment, agriculture and climate 
departments) which enabled the prioritization of value chains and the initial design of the project’s theory 
of change and proposed interventions. Consultations continued with the County Capacity Needs 
Assessment, in which representatives (men and women) from all 14 counties were interviewed to 
determine climate related capacity gaps. Further consultations took place as the design advanced and 
county representatives provided input into the design of various activities. Several validation meetings were 
held after the finalization of the first draft of the proposal package.  
270. At local level consultations also took place with smallholder farmers, men and women. Individual 
farmers were contacted and interviewed regarding application of climate resilient, low -carbon technologies 
or potential barriers faced when adapting to climate change. A Climate Change Survey was distributed to 
famers who were members of cooperatives and others who were not (women included). Gender -specific 
questions were included in the survey as well. A comprehensive Cooperative Census was also undertaken. 
This included a detailed questionnaire designed to assess and screen potential beneficiary cooperatives 
in the 6 value chains, understand challenges regarding all aspects of climate resilient, low -carbon 
agriculture. This census allowed the project design team to access local data, understand beneficiaries 
and design the best possible mechanism to enable cooperatives to accede to higher levels of climate 
performance.  
D.6. Efficiency and effectiveness  (max`. 500 words, approximately 1 page)  
271. The financial structure proposed for this project uses grants to support technical assistance, technology 
transfer, capacity building for vulnerable smallholders, along with nationally -funded targeted investments 
in public service goods and services (e.g.,  ecosystem restoration and management, extension and 
information services). Considering the high level of needs for climate finance in Kenya (Refer to section 
B5) and the limited investment in adaptation to climate change and in AFOLU -related mitigation in  the 
LREB region, this proposed initiative makes optimal use of the GCF contribution to generate a public -
private partnership towards transformation of key value chains. The expected direct and indirect benefits 
of the project for the LREB and the national  economy justify the level of financing requested. The project 
design team has prepared the project cost.  Total project costs were estimated at US D 49.992 million. The 
total cost comprises of a GCF grant of US D 29.215 million (5 8% of total project cost); G overnment of Kenya

 through National Treasury of Kenya contributing US D 14.0 million (28%); a Grant of US D 5.791 million 
(11.6%) from the Ministry of Foreign Affairs of the Government of Denmark (DMFA); and USD 0.981  million  
(2%)  from Food and Agriculture Organization (FAO).  
272. The EFA has explored the financial and economic performance of the activities considered in the 
Funding Proposal of the project “with” and “without” GCF support. The project focuses on the top 7 priority 
value chains, three of which have more of a market orientation (dairy, coffee and tea) and four that have 
more of a household food security orientation (African leafy vegetables, poultry and fruit tree). The total 
project beneficiaries, 143,000 households, will be reached during the project period with various project 
assistance. Out of the total beneficiaries the project assumes that, 35,750 will have tea; another 35,750, 
coffee; 42,900 will have African leafy vegetables; 14,300, avocado, and another 14,300, banana. In 
addition, 42,900 and 28,600 beneficiaries will have dairy and poultry respectively. These beneficiaries will 
also have crops that are listed above.  
273. The incremental production of the targeted value chain products provided the benefits for the EFA. 
The increment was estimated by taking the difference between the baseline productivity (without project 
case) and estimated increased productivity under climate sensitive and low carbon technologies (with 
project case). The profitability indicators that were estimated for each value chain product at the beneficiary 
level include the Net Present Value (NPV, economic and financial), the Internal Rate of Return (IRR, 
economic and financial), the Benefit -costs ratio (B/C), the pay -back period and increase in returns to family 
labor (for the financial analysis). Using the aggregated cost and benefits flows of the VC product models 
and the project cost, the same profitability indicators were estimated for the project. The financial and 
economic viabilities were estimated for two climate change scenarios - RCP 2.6 and RCP 8.5 - for both 
the beneficiary level and the

276. As the bar chart shows, the farmers who participate in these VCs can generate enhanced profits 
starting with relatively low profitability and decreasing yield due to climate change under the WoP or 
baseline situation. The project intervention will allow them to increase their profitability from cultivating the 
same amount of land with new climate sensitive and low carbon technologies under the WP scenario, 
which also contributes to  reducing the carbon emission.  
277. Based on individual VC commodity model analyses, the project level financial and economic 
profitability indicators were analysed. The financial internal rate of return of the project is 23% with a benefit -
cost ratio of 1.11; and 25% and 1.13 respectively f or RCP  8.5 and RCP  2.6 scenarios for a 20- years period 
at 18% discount rate. The project will be able to earn US D 10.7 million as a net present value under the 
same discount rate for 20 years under RCP  8.5 (US D 12.9 for RCP  2.6). The project can breakeven the 
project expenditure plus the beneficiaries’ investments after 9 years (pay -back period) of project 
commencement for both climate change scenarios.  
278. The project level economic internal rate of return is 21% with a benefit -cost ratio of 1.24; and 22% and 
1.25 respectively for RCP  8.5 and RCP  2.6 scenarios under 10% economic discount rate. The project is 
also viable under many sensitivity scenarios. The EFA analyses indicate that the project is therefore 
adequately viable in financial, economic, and social (environmental) terms and also has the capacity to 
face many risk factors while being viable. The project also has the potential reduce GHG emission to  a 
significant level with social net present value of US D 113 million and USD 116 million for RCP  8.5 and 
RCP  2.6 scenarios. The project therefore is suitable for receiving public funds for investments.  
Further details on the economic and financial analyses undertaken for the project can be found in Annex  3.

 Replicability  This project builds on the 
replication and upscaling of proven, tested climate resilient, low-carbon practices in Kenya 
and abroad. However, not many of these practices are being implemented by local farmers due to the costs and risks involved.  
Medium  The project’s strategy for replication builds on various components:  
Building climate change responses into cooperatives’ business model and 
demonstrating their profitability will 
ensure that farmers joining cooperatives have permanent access to the best available climate information, extension and services.  
leveraging the county government’s 
participation in the project to identify and 
disseminate best practices to farmers beyond project beneficiaries, after project implementation.  
Deploying an active learning and knowledge sharing strategy that builds 
on institutions such as Council of 
Governors, Climate change Multisectoral platforms and national government.  All proposed technologies are applicable to similar crop or livestock value chains. Replication can be 
foreseen at various levels: to 
beneficiaries not targeted in the 
project in the same value chains; to other value chains in the region, and to other regions.  
 
The technology transfer mechanisms 
set up by the project can continue to 
be available to farmers after project completion.  
Sustainability  There is a good level of commitment among county governments to address climate 
change. However, the 
conventional approaches to delivering climate services to farmers (information, extension, inputs) has not been sustainable so far. Most of it is costly an d 
does not reach all last mile users and beneficiaries.  Medium  The project transforms the way in which government support farmers and their organization. By focusing on providing 
guidance, enabling investment, normative 
frameworks, and establishing rules, counties can partner with farmer organizations and businesses to create better conditions for agriculture in the region. As a result of the project, farmers will see both the economic 
and climate benefit of technologies proposed 
and will face less risk in adopting them.  The project will support the creation of technology transfer mechanisms, knowledge and capacity that will 
outlast the duration of the project. 
Linkages to CIDPs and other county level strategies and plans are established. Once climate resilient, low-carbon technology becomes 
mainstreamed in cooperatives 
business models, all farmers entering 
the market will have access to this knowledge and will benefit from climate related services provided directly by cooperatives.

E.6. Project/programme activities and deliverables  
 
Activities  Description Sub-activities  Deliverables  
Component 1 – Enabling local government support for adaptation and mitigation  
Output 1.1 Local a dministrations deploy improved climate knowledge, extension and methodologies to producers and value chain actors  
Activity 1.1.1 Develop and 
deploy innovative and efficient 
extension methods for disseminating and demonstrating CRLCSA 
knowledge, technologies and 
practices in gender -responsive 
and socially inclusive ways  The purpose of this activity is to assist 
county governments in developing and deploying improved agro- climate extension 
services, leveraging national and regional best practice, innovative and efficiency -
boosting technologies, and participatory 
processes . Key stakeholders involved in this 
activity are county governments extension, 
meteorology, agriculture and cooperative 
departments.   1.1.1.1 Stocktaking of extension 
methods and public and private extension service providers and actors and gaps/needs analysis  1.1.1.2 Participatory design of 
innovative and efficient CRLCSA extension methods including the feedback mechanisms between the services recipients and the providers  1.1.1.1  One stocktaking report on the 
state of CRLCSA extension in the LREB  
1.1.1.2 One regional extension design 
workshop ( 4-days)  
1.1.1.3. One Set of SOP for each new 
extension method adopted in each county  
1.1.1.4 . Cluster -level training on the 
new SOP, three cluster, one training

Activity 1.1.2  
Strengthen the dissemination of 
climate information services to 
last-mile users including women, 
youth and PLWD  through 
cooperatives and Farmer 
Organizations.  This activity supports county governments 
in deploying climate information services, 
advisories, early warnings and data to last 
mile users, with a particular focus on farmer organizations and cooperatives as key 
knowledge intermediaries, making sure 
vulnerable groups receive and can act upon 
climate information within the agricultural production context. Key stakeholders in this 
activity are the county governments meteorological, agriculture, extension, 
disaster risk management and cooperative 
departments . Methods for delivery of this 
activity include training, workshops, co-
planning, participatory scenario planning 
and co- design workshops, as well as the 
mobilization of technical advisory and 
expertise.  1.1.2 .1 Conduct a stock take and gap 
analysis of climate information services in each county for the 6 value 
chains.  
1.1.2 .2 Develop new climate 
information services or products for 
the 6 value chains in cooperation with 
National and County Meteorological 
Department  
1.1.2.3 Training for agro-meteorological departments (county 
level) in the development and delivery 
of climate information services to FO and cooperatives in the 6 value chains 
 
1.1.2.4 Seasonal planning at the ward 
level to develop locally relevant and 
tailored seasonal advisories in the 
targeted value chains and natural 
resources management   
1.1.2.5 Disseminate timely information 
on climate impacts and weather forecasts through selected community 
radio, social media, print channels and 
extension  
1.1.2. 6 Monitor reach and level of use 
for each new product.  
1.1.2. 7 Develop registry of 
intermediaries of climate information 1.1.2.1 One climate information 
services status report in each county  
1.1.2.2 One new climate information 
service or product in each county

for 140 people (in Year 1 and in Year 
3)

Activity 1.1.3  
Develop and test methodologies 
for decentralized carbon 
accounting  Under this activity, county governments will 
acquire technical capacity to conduct decentralized carbon accounting, through 
training as well as through learning by 
doing. The methodologies will be applied in 
linkages to the development of landscape management strategies (Outcome 2). The 
methods used for delivering this activity 
include training, development of processes 
and institutional capacity, acquisition of 
material capacity, and the mobilization of 
technical expertise to backstop emissions 
reporting i n each county.  1.1.3.1 Conduct a participatory design workshop for selecting and developing 
a LREB -specific decentralized carbon 
accounting methodology.  
1.1.3.2 Set up the process for data 
gathering and computation  
1.1.3.3 Develop operating manuals and guidance documents  
1.1.3.4. Conduct learning by doing 
accounting exercises in each county 
and prepare emissions reports.  1.1.3.1 Cluster -level workshop 
bringing together county -level 
Meteorological Department, 
agriculture/agro- meteorology and 
extension services, forest and 
environment departments and climate change units , three clusters, 5 days  
1.1.3.2 Software and data collection 
protocol  
1.1.3.3 One set of operating manuals 
and guidance documents  
1.1.3.4. One emissions report per 
county at year 2, and one at year 5.  
Activity 1.1.4  
Upgrade and update agricultural 
databases, crop and productivity 
datasets, cooperative census  Under this activity, the project supports  
county governments in developing more refined data and information services that can serve cooperatives, business and 
private sector development, while also 
supporting the documentation of climate 
benefits from application of  climate resilient, 
low-carbon technologies . This activity builds 
on ongoing efforts to digitize agricultural extension and services.  1.1.4.1 Conduct systems needs 
assessment and gap analy sis for 
database in each county  
1.1.4.2. Upgrade and update agricultural databases with technical 
support, draft technical and training 
guides and reporting schedule for 
dissemination purposes  
1.1.4.3 . Collect and analyse crop, 
climate, productivity, market and 
economic data annually through 
county extension services and 
integrate into databases.  
1.1.4.4. Deliver technical assistance to 
conduct biennial census of 
cooperatives  1.1.4.1. One stock -take and gap 
analysis report on agricultural 
databases  
1.1.4.2. 14 upgraded databases  
1.1.4.3. Value chain level report  
highlighting productivity vs climate 
trends  
1.1.4.4 Three Coop Census reports  
Activity 1.1.5  
Assess eligible climate solutions 
for the agriculture sector in 
relation to climate impacts  In this activity, the county governments build 
their capacity to understand local climate 
risk to agricultural production and to select 
the most suitable climate resilient, low -
carbon technologies in which to invest . The 
selection of technologies  forms a blueprint 
or a menu from which cooperatives and financial institutions can select when making 
decisions about investment and lending . 1.1.5.1 Training for county climate 
change units, meteorological departments and agriculture 
departments on selection of climate 
solutions.  
1.1.5.2 Conduct a participatory screening and pre- selection of climate 
resilient, low carbon solutions and 
technologies for the 6 Value Chains  1.1.5.1 Cluster based training in 
climate change impact assessment 
and climate solutions identification, 
three clusters, 3 days  
1.1.5.2 A county council -endorsed list 
of county -specific climate resilient, low 
carbon solutions and technologies in year 2 and updated in year 5.

low-carbon landscape management 
strategy

E.7. Monitoring, reporting and e valuation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)  
279. MEL will be undertaken in compliance with FAO and GCF policies. As AE, FAO will ensure that all EE are making use of a common  monitoring and 
measurement system to analyse and quantify the causal pathways and attributable changes, the contribution of project activities and the overall results of the 
project. This includes the implementation of: (i) a monitoring system to regularly measure progress and results; (ii) a Risk monitoring and management plan; 
(iii) an evaluation plan that helps understand efficacy, pathways to impact and to verify the assumptions that the program is making and (iv) a learning strategy  
through which the results of the project can feed back into project implementation and exit strategy.  
Monitoring System  
280. Project management, supervision and day to day monitoring will be carried out by the Project Management Unit (PMU), with the support of FAO -Kenya and 
information flows from each EE. Monitoring will ensure that: (i) project deliverables and outputs are produced in accordance with the project results framework 
and leading to the achievement of project outcomes; (ii) project outcomes are leading to the achievement of the project objective; (iii) risks are continuously 
identified and monitored and appropriate mitigation strategies are applied; and (iv) agreed project climate -related benefits are being delivered. The data 
collection tools will be made on the basis of the indicators from the project’s Results Framework (RF), and the Annual Work Plan and Budgets (A WPBs).  
281. Project outputs will be monitored separately as well as in relation to the achievement of higher level project results and overall GCF goals. Reporting on 
results will be required as follows:  
Project Inception Report  
282. A project inception workshop will be conducted in the first month of implementation in order to: a) orient project stakeholders  to the project strategy and 
discuss any change in the overall context that might influence implementation; b) discuss the roles and responsibilities of the project team, including reporting 
and communication lines; c) review the results framework and discussion, present in detail the Theory of Change of the projec t, reporting, monitoring and 
evaluation roles and responsibilities, and to finalize the M&E plans; d) review financial reporting requirements; and e) planning and scheduling of M&E meetings; 
and f) finalize the first year work plan. The workshop will generate an inception report that will be presented and approve by FAO withi n the 4 weeks following 
the workshop.  
Project Progress Reports  
283. Semi Annual Progress Reports. The PMU will prepare 6- months progress reports which will be sent to the Budget Holder. The progress reports will 
include an overall assessment of achievements against targets and deliverables. Executing Entities will also report on their financial and operational progress 
on a six -month basis.  
284. Annual Progress Reports . The M&E Officer and EE will provide inputs to the Annual Report for each year of implementation. The M&E Officer will ensur e 
that the indicators in the results framework are monitored annually through the online tools and other procedures. The annual  performance reports will be due


---
*Page 81*
---

E E E E 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 77 
  
  
 at GCF 60 days after the end of each calendar year. The final project annual report and the terminal evaluation report will s erve as the final project report 
package.  
285. Technical Reports will be prepared by consultants as part of project outputs (deliverables) and to document and share project outcomes and less ons 
learned. The drafts of any technical reports will be submitted by the PMU. The Lead Technical Officer (LTO) will be responsib le for ensuring appropriate technical 
review and clearance of the report. Copies of the technical reports will be distributed to project partners and the PTC C and PSC as appropriate.  
286. Annual Financial and Co –financing Reports: containing information compiled from all EEs on expenditures against targets, including expenditures from all 
sources of co- financing.  
287. Project Completion report (Final APR): A final APR report will be submitted by the AE, with input from al EE, to the GCF, no later than 6 months after 
technical completion of activities.  
288. For field -level activities, the project will make use of georeferenced monitoring. The project places particular attention on ensuring the monitoring of 
differential impacts by sex, age and vulnerability, especially monitoring regarding activities relevant  to women and more vulnerable populations.  
Monitoring of Core GCF Results - GHG emissions reductions and Adaptation Benefits  
289. The project will conduct EXACT and GLEAM simulation exercises in the final year of the project, leveraging updated data on project execution and output 
delivery regarding specifically:  
• Number of heads of cattle and productivity levels by end of project  
• Extent of land under improved management, improved cover, reforestation, afforestation and conservation  
• Using these simulations the project’s final evaluation will compare projected achieved emissions reductions with anticipated reductions at inception. 
(please refer to Annex 22 for details on the methodologies) . A report analysing changes and factors of success or challenges will be compiled at 
the end of the project.  
Resilience Survey  
290. As indicated in FS, Part C section 6.3, a resilience survey will be deployed at inception, mid- term and end point of the project. The survey will be designed 
at inception, to facilitate tracking of project indicators and deliverables as well as all sub- indices contained in the resilience index. It will be tailored to include 
additional questions to minimize the reporting burden on farmers and the project and to maximize data collection. The measurement will be made at household 
level with a representative sample of project participants.  
Cooperative Census  
291. The cooperative census, which was crafted with climate, gender equality and social inclusion intent will be carried out by counties on a biennial basis, 
assessing evolution in membership and participation, social inclusion, sales and income, environmental and climate challenges and access to assets and inputs 
(land, water, energy).  
Mid-Term  and Terminal Evaluations  
292. To provide an external  viewpoint  on the progress  of the project  and the achievement  of its objectives,  and in line with the AMA  signed with the GCF,  two 
independent  project  evaluations  will be conducted by FAO– mid-term and final evaluations.  In line with the FAO policy  on evaluations,  the interim  (midterm)  
and final evaluations  may be either  managed  by FAO Office  of Evaluation or decentralized (under  the responsibility  of decentralized  offices). In both cases,  
they will be carried out by a team  of independent  external  consultants.  The evaluations  will also be conducted  in line with the GCF  Evaluation  Policy,  and will 
follow  the GCF’s  Integrated  Results  Management  Framework,  namely  containing an assessment  of the paradigm  shift potential  and its enabling  environment.  
The project's  beneficiary  surveys will constitute  important  inputs  for the interim  and final evaluations . Please refer to Annex  11 for the detailed  M&E  plan.

 F. RISK  ASSESSMENT  AND  MANAGEMENT  
F.1.  Risk factors and mitigations measures (max. 3 pages)   
293. A risk assessment been conducted for this project, including an evaluation of financial, technical, 
operational and institutional risks that may prevent the project from achieving its objectives . An environmental 
and social risk assessment has also been conducted, the findings of which are presented in Annex X. FAO as 
AE has conducted due legal diligence assessment on all project partners and executing entities .  FAO confirms 
that none of the activities, counterparties, beneficiaries , executing entities, co- financiers or implementing 
partners are subject to or impacted by any United Nations Security Council Sanctions . No entity or individual 
in the project  is subject to or listed on any UN Security Council sanctions list, including the UN Security Council 
Consolidated Sanctions list. This will be reviewed annually on the basis of updated lists of sanctioned entities, 
during the Annual Project Performance Review.  
294. There is a low risk of reversal and no risk of leakage the carbon sink creating activities of this project. 
All activities designed to reduce GHG emissions from agricultural production and processing are also designed to ensure that activities are not displaced, or delegated to other value chain actors. There is strong will and 
potential for continued agricultural production in the area. Regarding the risk of reversal, this applies to the implementation of the Landscape Management Strategies executed by county governments under Outcome 2 
and to agroforestry or  any other increases in land cover achieved by the project. The risk of reversal arises 
from unlikely risks not controlled by the project, such as major natural disasters (earthquake, landslides, fires), sudden population changes (rapid influx of migrants or refugees, pandemic). Measures to prevent reversal are in-built into the project’s implementation strategy: by tying increases in land cover with economic benefits for 
farmers, by ensuring that the economic benefits of improved landscape management are documented, seen and integrated by communities, county governments and private sector, and by ensuring that commodities 
produced under climate resilient, low -carbon practices fetch fair prices and attract more financing than others. 
The decentralized carbon accounting methodologies and exercises deployed by counties under Outcome  1 
will also serve as a means to assess any risk of reversal or leakage during project implementation because it 
is repeated at 2 intervals and can inform a redress strategy.  
295. Main risks identified for this project are illustrated in the table below.  
Selected Risk Factor 1 Land tenure, land ownership or land conflict 
Category  Probability  Impact  
Governance  Medium  Medium  
Description 
Outdated land registries, limited access to land registries and information, poor dispute resolution, poor 
implementation of land reforms and overlapping mandates between governing institutions are some of the 
challenges associated with land governance in Kenya.104 Weak land use planning and insecure land tenure have 
proven to be a barrier to the adoption of practices and technologies ( e.g., agroforestry, irrigation infrastructure and 
soil conservation) that can reduce vulnerability to climate change. It also discourages long- term planning in favour 
of maximizing short –term profits and complicates the implementation of effective climate change adaptation and 
mitigation plans.  Regarding land use/ access issues for Indigenous Peoples, it is not foreseen that Indigenous 
Peoples will be directly targeted as Indigenous Peoples in the region (such as pastoralists and hunter -gathers) are 
not engaged in the project target VCs.  However , the project will develop and implement county’s landscape 
management strategies which could cause restrictions on land use and access to natural resources subject to traditional ownership or under customary use, if the proposed strategies and its implementation overlap with 
Indigenous Peoples ’s territories and their livelihood.  
Mitigation Measure(s)  
Identification of existing tenure rights and right holders, including the identification of customary use of land and 
resources, such as cultural, ceremonial or spiritual use, and any ad hoc, seasonal or cyclical use of land and natural 
resources ( Component  2). In case the project confirms the presence of Indigenous Peoples  in the project target 
areas, the Indigenous Peoples Planning Framework (IPPF) and related plans  will be applied to these Indigenous 
Peoples  who might be impacted by the project intervention.  
 
104 IGAD, 2018. Kenya - Land Governance Country Profile. Available at: https://land.igad.int/index.php/documents -
1/countries/kenya/profiles -3/785 -land -governance-in -igad-region-kenya -country -profile/file

 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 79 
   
 Selected Risk Factor 2  Market reorientation and supply chain constraints in selected value chains  
Category  Probability  Impact  
Technical and operational  Low Medium  
Description 
There is a very low risk that the national food market would see a change in demand in the commodities targeted 
by this project. For coffee and tea, demand projections at national and global levels have shown steady increasing 
trends, even despite market shocks and t he pandemic. However, supply chains may be constrained due to 
unforeseen international events, such as war, natural disasters, or sudden trade policy changes, leading to input and output barriers (barriers to acquisition of inputs, and barriers to delivery  of outputs). This is highly unlikely, as 
even during the pandemic, the agricultural sector in Kenya barely contracted, and trade continued unhindered.   
Mitigation Measure(s)  
Measures to mitigate this risk are embedded into Activity 4.1.1, where the project will work with and for buyers to 
demonstrate the quality and economic value of outputs produced using climate resilient, low -carbon technologies. 
Support to cooperatives to reach buyers, negotiate trade agreements, navigate supply chains, and conduct appropriate price- setting as part of activity 4.2.2 will also assist cooperatives in understanding and assessing the 
impacts of value chain risks to their own production. Furthermore, the project also works with counties to monitor 
trends for value chains as a service given to farmers and cooperatives under activity 1.1.4. This will serve the dual 
purpose of obtaining advance warning of any changing trends in demand or supply in the 6 value chains, but also of informing county -level trade and development  policy to better support farmers in their responses to market 
trends.   
Selected Risk Factor 3 Decreasing national budgets for management of natural resources  
Category  Probability  Impact  
Technical and operational  Low Medium  
Description 
There is a small risk, given the increasing demands on national budgets and given the fact that some of the sectors 
such as Forestry have not yet undergone full devolution in Kenya, that national and decentralized budgets for the improved management of natural resources and public lands could decrease. This would mean the national and 
county governments would progressively divest themselves from the conservation and management of natural 
reserves, parks, forests or public lands, leaving private sector interest to fill the vacuum in ways that might be contrary to the project’s objectives and the country’s best interest. This risk is very low – given the historical high 
level commitment of the Government of Kenya to the protection of natural resources (regardless of political interests and orientation), the presence of a solid corpus of national laws related to conservation and natural resources management, and the increasing trends of public investment in the management and conservation of the country’s 
natura l capital.  National and county budgets and priorities are set annually and enshrined in the Budget Policy 
Statement.” The BPS includes (a) an assessment of the current state of the economy and the financial outlook over 
the medium term, including macro- ec
onomic forecasts; (b) the financial outlook with respect to Government 
revenues, expenditures and borrowing for the next financial year and over the medium term;(c) the proposed expenditure limits for the national government, and indicative transfers to co
unty governments; (d) the fiscal 
responsibility principles and financial objectives over the medium term including limits on total annual debt; (e) 
fiscal risk statement”.   
Mitigation Measure(s)  
The project includes measures to build the county’s capacity to understand the risks to natural resources posed by 
climate change and unsustainable use, and to also understand the economic and development value of the 
maintenance of ecosystem services. This will be done through the development of the integr ated landscape 
management strategies, which are underpinned by rapid climate risk assessments (Output  2.2). Awareness raising 
of the ecological, social and economic benefits of healthy ecosystems and agricultural landscapes is also built into the project’s  strategy, further strengthening the local stewardship of ecosystems.   Measures to integrate activities 
related to the project (e.g., extension, climate services, etc.) into the County Integrated Development Plans (CIDP) 
are also in place. In addition, FAO will review, in collaboration with the National Treasury and the NDA, any impacts 
of the BPS on the project annually.  
Selected Risk Factor  4 Changes in financial policy hinder financial institutions’ ability to lend to 
beneficiaries  
Category  Probability  Impact


---
*Page 84*
---

F F F F 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 80 
   
 Technical and operational  Low Low 
Description  
There is a possibility that changes in monetary policy or rates will impact financial institutions’ ability to lend to 
project beneficiaries, which will make finance more difficult to access or could constrain the availability of supply 
despite project efforts to build FI and farmer organization capacity. Currently, Commercial banks in Kenya are 
required by law to keep a specified proportion of their total deposits at the Central Bank (5.25%). The Central Bank 
sets rates that determine other bank’s lending rates: currently the central bank rate is high, at 8.75%. Inflation rate in Kenya in March 2023 is also high, at 9.6%. Changes in these conditions will impact the participating banks’ lending appetite, ability and conditions. However, the risk of significant rapid change is low, as the central policy 
principle for the Central Bank is to maintain annual price stability. Monetary policy is guided by a monetary 
programme, whic h is premised on the economic growth and inflation targets provided by the National Treasury. 
Monetary policy decisions are made by the Monetary Policy Committee (MPC). The MPC meets at least once every two months and reviews data and analysis from various  sources including the Central Bank Departments enabling 
it to decide on any action to maintain or vary its stance. Information from the monetary policy and the general 
macro -economic indicators of the country are also integrated into the annual budget pol icy statement.   
Mitigation Measure(s)  
The project will monitor, together with the participating financial institutions, any changes in monetary policy and 
its potential impact on the ability of the institutions to deliver financing. Capacity development for financial 
institutions provided under outcome 4 will also include capacity to conserve lending appetite and increase 
concessionality within the parameters of monetary policy evolution. FAO will also monitor changes in the country’s 
monetary policy, macro -economic indicators and results of annual budge t policy statements.   
Selected Risk Factor 5: Political (collaboration between Government stakeholders ) 
Category  Probability  Impact  
Technical and operational  Medium  Medium  
Description  
Limited  collaboration between stakeholders from different ministries (e.g. Ministry of Agriculture - MoA, Ministry of 
Environment and the National Treasury  as well as the county governments) on land restoration activities  and the 
coordination of the project implementation. This low collaboration may negatively affect project implementation, resulting in limited information and knowledge- sharing on climate- resilience practices and technologies, and 
ultimately impair the overall long -term project impact.  
Mitigation Measure(s)  
All actors  will be engaged in the Project Steering Committee  (PSC)  and Project Technical  Coordination 
Committee (PTC C) to ensure that government organisations work hand- in hand. At local ( county ) level, all 
activities will be coordinated under the county level PSC and PTC C to ensure collaboration between all 
government stakeholders . These actions will lower the probability of risk to low.  
Selected Risk Factor 6: Resistance on women’s participation and empowerment  
Category  Probability  Impact  
Other Low Low 
Description  
The project may encounter passive or active resistance to women's participation as well as when working to 
strengthen women’s empowerment in communities.  
Mitigation Measure(s)  
The project targeting mechanisms including direct, community -based and self -targeting mechanisms will promote  
women's participation including vulnerable groups such as female- head of household, female youth and girls, and  
persons living with HIV/AIDS. The project will rely on participative community approach and on gender -
transformative approach: Women's Leadership- Youth leadership trainings developed by Agriterra as well as the 
Gender Action Learning System (GALS) . These trainings will focus on all aspects of the value chain, accelerating 
the inclusion of women/ vulnerable groups in production, processing and value addition, and trade. During the 
Women's Leadership Workshops , the role of women as members, entrepreneurs and leaders will be discussed 
and together with male and female farmers an action plan is developed to improve the participation of women in 
the cooperative.  All along the project lifecycle, the project will refer to the GAP developed during the design 
process to monitor the progress made on GEWE and take necessary corrective actions as appropriate.

 Mitigation Measure(s)  
FAO includes in the project agreement signed between FAO and the Government of Kenya  clauses related to  
AML/CFT, as follows:  
a) The Government shall comply, and shall require all persons and entities engaged in its activities under 
the Project  to comply, with all internal anti -money laundering, counter -terrorism financing laws, rules, and 
regulations;  
b) The Government confirms it has obtained sufficient undertakings from all persons and entities involved in 
its activities  under the Project that they shall not engage in any prohibited practices; the Government 
undertakes and confirm that  it shall comply with the substantive objectives of the GCF’s Policy on 
Prohibited Practices;  
c) Consistent with numerous United Nations Security Council resolutions adopted under Chapter VII of the UN Charter,  the Government and FAO are firmly committed to the international fight against terrorism 
and, in particular, against the financing of terrorism. It is the policy of the Government and FAO to seek to 
ensure that none of their funds are used,  directly or indirectly, to provide support to individuals or entities: 
i) associated with terrorism, as included in the list  maintained by the Security Council Committee 
established pursuant to its Resolutions 1267 (1999) , 1989 (2011)  and 2253 (2015) ; or ii) that are the 
subject of sanctions or other enforcement measures promulgated by the United Nations Security  Council. 
This provision must be included in all agreements that may be concluded with third parties for the implementation of activities under the Project.  
During project implementation FAO, as AE, will ensure close monitoring and supervision through its offices in the regional office and HQ in order to ensure that the activities are implemented in full compliance with the signed 
project .
