 G. GCF  POLICIES  AND  STANDARDS  
G.1.  Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)   
296. The project is aligned with the FAO Environmental and Social Standards  (2022), and GCF policies including 
the revised GCF Environmental and Social Policy  (2021), GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy  (IPP) and the GCF 
Information Disclosure Policy  (2016), GCF Policy on the Prevention and Protection from Sexual Exploitation, Sexual 
Abuse and Sexual Harassment ( GCF SEAH Policy ), among others. Stakeholder consultations process was carried 
planned at the community level in the project area through period 2021- 2023, considering GCF (2022) guidance 
on designing and ensuring meaningful stakeholder engagement . 
297. The project is expected to predominantly generate substantial positive environmental and social benefits, 
including, but not limited to enhance resilience of vulnerable smallholder and key value chains to the impacts of 
climate change, reduce GHG emissions  from Agriculture, Forestry and Land Use sectors (AFOLU), strengthen land 
use planning, among others. Nonetheless, the project has the potential to generate adverse social and 
environmental impacts that need to be carefully managed and monitored.  
298. The FAO Project Environmental and Social Screening Checklist was prepared, and the project was categorized 
as a Moderate Risk Project (Category B project), where:  
299. There are identified potential adverse environmental and social impacts requiring the need for environmental 
and social management plans, including: proximity to protected areas, potential generation of new environmental waste from agro- industrial activiti es, indirect trigger for increased pesticides use (even when IPM is being promoted) 
and construction of small scale of agro- facilities. It is expected that the project activities, as described in Chapter 
2, will trigger the following Environmental and Social Safeguard Policies: ESS1, ESS2, ESS4, ESS5, ESS6, ESS7, ESS8 (see Table below).  
300. Potential impacts are limited to the project´s footprint, not unprecedented in the project area, neither irreversible 
nor cumulative. Potential adverse impacts can be addressed using recognized good management or pollution 
abatement practices that include FAO guidelines as well as World Bank Group Environmental, Health and Safety Guidelines (known as EHS Guidelines
105) 
301. Project sub- activities will be subject to project screening process (using FAO Screening checklist) to identify 
potential E&S risks and impacts. Depending on the classification and level of risk identified, Environmental and Social Management Plans (ESMPs) will then be prepared. Given that the project is considered medium risk overall, it is expected that only some of the project activities will require ESMPs following the screening phase. Ad ditionally, 
all project activities will have to ensure compliance with Kenya’s environmental and social regulation and obtain necessary construction and or operation permits and licenses.  
302. The Government of Kenya has not yet adopted the United Nations Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous 
Peoples and has yet to ratify Convention 169 of the International Labor Organization, the country is yet to ratify the ILO convention on Indigenous and T ribal Peoples. Though, Kenya has no specific law on Indigenous Peoples, the 
country has ratified the: i) International Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Racial Discrimination (ICERD); ii) the Convention on the Elimination of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW); and iii) the Convention on the 
Rights of the Child (CRC). Kenya does not have specific legislation on Indigenous Peoples , and does not identify 
with the term indigenous, rather considers those segments of the population who have continued to live by their 
traditional customs and belief systems and way of life, as marginalized (these communities include mostly hunter -
gatherers and pastoralists).  
303. Participants to the project will be invited to self -identify as members of a vulnerable, indigenous, ethnic or 
religious minority and specific measures are included to support meaningful participation of I ndigenous People in 
the project, including in the Project Committees . Before initiating project activities  which could impact Indigenous 
Peoples , a Free Prior and Informe Consent (FPIC) process will be implemented following FAO guidelines, to ensure 
an effective and inclusive participation approach of potential affected indigenous communities. An Indigenous Peoples Planning Framework (IPPF) has been included within the project Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF - Annex 6) of the FP.
 
304. The overall ESMF budget (Annex 6) is USD 697,356  for a blend of human resources to respond to ad- hoc 
requirements for safeguards activities. Gender activities in the Gender Action Plan are included in the project 
activities budget. ESS compliance and GAP monitoring are also included in the baseline, mid  and end -line surveys, 
 
105 www.ifc.org/ehsguidelines

Stakeholder Engagement Plan (SEP) 
306. This project has adopted an active stakeholder engagement strategy since its inception and throughout 
development and design following a bottom- up approach that is aligned to the devolution process in Kenya. 
Particular attention has been paid to identifying key stakeholders and involving them meaningfu lly and at the right


---
*Page 88*
---

F F F F 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 84 
   
 time, to ensure their advice, knowledge and inputs are duly considered in all aspects of this project. Stakeholder 
engagement is fully mainstreamed in all project activities.  
307. The identification of stakeholders was done using a collaborative approach, mobilizing Kenyan government 
authorities at national and county level as well as FAO -Kenya’s experience and expertise. Analysis of each 
stakeholders’ potential influence on, benefi ts from, and contribution to the project was done combining desk review 
and interviews or discussions.  
308. The initial identification of stakeholders was done through exchanges between The National Treasury who is 
the NDA, and FAO on the basis of the project’s proposed regional and thematic scope. Subsequent discussions 
expanded to include relevant government departments, agencies and development partners at national and sub-
national levels who have helped with refining the concept note and ensured inclusivity of relevant actors. 
Stakeholders in this project fall in the following groups and categories, as detail ed in Annex  7. 
• Farmers : individual smallholder farmers involved in the six value chains namely coffee, tea, dairy, African 
leafy vegetables, fruit trees and poultry . Farmers are direct and indirect beneficiaries of the project. The 
project also identified vulnerable groups whose participation in project activities will be the object of particular 
attention: women, youth, persons living with disabilities, and persons from indigenous peoples or ethnic and 
religious minorities.  
• Farmer organizations : this includes informal self -help groups, business units, farming groups, community 
forest associations, farmer field schools and cooperatives in the six value chains, as well as value chain 
actors not involved in production, such as buyers, transporters,  wholesalers, input and service providers. 
Farmer organizations are also direct and indirect beneficiaries of the project.  
• County governments :  This includes county Council Assemblies, Governors, and public administration in all 
the devolved sectors . This also includes ward- level decentralized administration and services . Counties are 
direct beneficiaries of the project and implementing partners.  
• Regional (LREB) institutions : including the LREB secretariat and relevant caucuses such as agriculture, 
water and environment; LREB Centre of excellence in Agriculture and Water; the Lake Region Development 
Authority; Lake Victoria North and South Water Service Boards, as well as knowledge sharing platforms on 
various sectors. Regional institutions play a key role in upscaling and knowledge sharing.  
• National Government Departments : The National Treasury (NT); State department of Agriculture; State 
Department of Cooperatives; Ministry of Environment, Climate Change and Forestry; Kenya Meteorological 
Department, Kenya Agricultural and Livestock Research Organization (KALRO); Department of Resource 
Survey and Resource Survey, among others.  
• Non- state actors:  such as Non -Governmental Organizations, Faith Based Organizations, community 
leadership, associations, academic and research institutions, and media.  
• International organizations:  This includes development partners working or implementing projects in the 
LREB ( e.g.,. World Bank, IFAD, DANIDA, Netherlands, GEF, etc).  
• Financial institutions : This includes community based credit and savings cooperatives, savings groups, as 
well as formal lending and grant -making institutions ( e.g.,. Banks, micro -finance institutions).  
309. A participatory process for the design of this project was conducted starting in late 2021, culminating in April 
2023. This process involved gathering ideas, suggestions, inputs, and contributions from stakeholders identified 
above, including farmers, coun ty governments, development partners and others. Details of meetings, 
consultations and feedback received is included in Annex  7.  
310. A series of analytical consultations, designed to assess needs and gaps, understand barriers, and refine the 
technical components of the project were also deployed. These included in- person and virtual surveys, interviews 
and workshops with all categories of stakeholders.  
311. Throughout implementation, the project will make every effort to provide relevant information to stakeholders 
in appropriate and timely manner.  
312. Dissemination of information will be done using a variety of media such as print (newspapers, posters), radios, 
public meetings and workshops to ensure maximum reach to stakeholders. Particular attention will be paid to the adequate communication of projec
t criteria, targeting approaches, activities and benefits to vulnerable and 
traditionally excluded groups, including women, elderly people, indigenous/ethnic groups and persons living with 
disabilities. Provisions will be made for the translation in local language of key project documentary outputs, the 
Grievance Redress Mechanism and FPIC documents, and for the use of pictorial or image- based communication 
in order to ensure communication about the project is not limited by literacy rates.  
313. Details of how each stakeholder and stakeholder group is to be engaged are included in Annex  7.  
Grievance Redress Mechanism

 314. The project will establish one grievance mechanisms at the field level to file complaints, sensitive to the location 
wherein the project is being implemented. Both (i) contact information and (ii) information on the process one must 
follow to file a complaint will be disclosed in all meetings, workshops and other related events throughout the life of the project. It is also expected that all awareness raising material to be distributed will include the necessary information regarding the process for filing grievances and key contacts. The project will be responsible for documenting and reporting, as part of the safeguard’s performance monitoring, on any grievances received and how they were addressed.  
315. The Project Management Unit (PMU) will be responsible for addressing incoming grievances regarding 
environmental and social standards; as part of the safeguard’s performance monitoring, the Project Coordinator of the PMU will be responsible for documenting and reporting on any grievances received and how they were 
addressed. FAO as well as other executing entities (EEs) will inform communities about the GRM through culturally 
appropriate mechanisms, ensuring information on the mechanisms at different levels  
through preferred 
communication channels, which are to be agreed and discussed as part of the project FPIC.  
316. The GRM will include methods/process to (i) receive and register external communications from the public; (ii) 
screen and assess the issues raised and determine how to address them; (iii) provide, track, and document 
responses, if any; and (iv) adjust the management program, as appropriate. Please refer to Annex 7 for further 
detail.  
G.2.  Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)   
317. The Gender Assessment  documented the current state of women in Kenya compared to men, as well as how 
gender intersects with age, household headship status, and disability status to influence participation and benefit from engaging in agricultural value chains in the context of  a changing climate. The assessment revealed that 
female youth, female- headed households (including widows), and females living with disabilities, are the most 
under -represented and vulnerable groups in CSA value chains, based on how their gender interacts  with other 
axes of social differentiation and inequality. Thus, the project’s gender mainstreaming strategy makes a concerted effort to build in intersectional equity principles into its targeting metrics, to ensure that female youth, female- headed 
househ
olds, and females living with disabilities are equitably represented in all project activities. Given this 
intersectional gender approach, gender data is expected to be collected beyond sex -disaggregation (i.e., number 
and % of female farmers disaggregated  by youth status, household headship status, and disability status vs. 
number and % of male farmers disaggregated by youth status, household headship status, and disability status).  
318. The Gender Action Plan (GAP) tackles gender inequalities across several priority areas in climate resilient 
and low -carbon sustainable agriculture across the 6 priority value chains. These include closing gender gaps and 
existing inequalities in participation (at intra- household level and within producer organizations/cooperatives), 
workloads (prioritizing agricultural technologies and practices for adaptation and mitigation that reduce workloads 
and negative impacts on women), access and use of productive resources (such as agro- clima te information, 
technologies, livelihood incomes, credit), and collective action (working with women’s groups as platforms for 
enhancing access, agency, women’s leadership, and voice in climate- smart agriculture). In closing these gender 
gaps, the project will address the underlying socio- cultural norms that perpetuate gender inequality and constrain 
women’s capabilities. This requires a culturally sensitive, multi -level approach that includes women and men in all 
their diversity.  
319. The Gender Action Plan details how the goal of intersectional equity will be mainstreamed in two ways: 
participation in activities and the content of activities. Women, female youth, female- headed households, and 
females living with disabilities all have specific quotas that are required for each activity. Mainstreaming in terms of participation will be ensured by setting the target proportion of participants from each of these intersections to be roughly equal to the relative population sizes at the national level: 50% women, 16.5% FHH, 1% for women LWD, 
and 25% (12.5% of total project population) are female youth (aged 18- 35 years). The male youth participation 
quota is 25% (25% of total project population) and 1% for men LWD ( 0
.5% of total project population). 
Mainstreaming in terms of content will be ensured by integrating Gender Action Learning Systems (GALS) and 
gender transformative concepts into 100% of training materials. These training materials include the “gender - and 
youth- specific” trainings, such as the Specialized Agriterra Training Programmes in Women’s Leadership and 
Youth Leadership, as well as more “general” training materials on agricultural production, markets, finance, etc.  
Gender Action Plan claims half of the total project budget. The relationships among the project objectives, actions, indicators, targets, and baselines are as shown in Annex  8. 
G.3.  Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)


---
*Page 90*
---

F F F F 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 86 
   
 320. As Accredited Entity for this project, FAO will ensure that financial management and procurement of goods and 
services using GCF resources adheres to relevant FAO rules and regulations, as well as relevant provisions in the 
Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA) signed between FAO and GCF. These rules and regulations were reviewed 
and deemed satisfactory by the GCF Secretariat and Accreditation Panel as part of FAO’s accreditation process.  
321. FAO has deployed an Oracle- based Enterprise Resource Planning (ERP) system, the ‘Global Resources 
Management System’ (GRMS), which provides all FAO employees in all locations globally with travel, human 
resources, procurement, and finance functionalities. Using GRMS improves the flow of financial information, 
supports financial monitoring and reporting, increases transparency and visibility, and strengthens internal control. 
FAO maintains a Chart of Accounts which is used by the whole Organization and that allows for a separation of 
income and expenditure by donor and project, and it provides a standardized coding structure that enables data to 
be recorded, classified, and summarized to facilitate internal management and external reporting requirements.  
322. Direct procurement by FAO is done in accordance with its Manual Section  502 on “Procurement of Goods, 
Works, and Services”. To sub- contract the delivery of specific activities using Letters of Agreement, FAO operates 
in accordance with its Manual Section 507 on “Letters of Agreement”. Such services are managed by the FAO 
Procurement Service, which provides policy and operational support to ensure that the Organization procures 
goods, works, and services based on “Best Value for Money” principles.  
323. Financial management and procurement executed by Agriterra as Executing Entity of GCF proceeds  will be 
overseen and supervised by the FAO -GCF project supervision team. The FAO -GCF project supervision team will 
undertake regular supervision missions and recruit a qualified, internationally recognized auditing firm to perform 
frequent spot checks and audits to ensure financial management and procurement by the PMU and executing 
entities are conducted in line with agreed standards and practices. This will be governed by the agreements to be 
signed between FAO and Agriterra  before the project becomes operational.  Audits will be performed by a qualified, 
internationally recognized auditing firm, competitively selected by FAO.  
324. Controls against money laundering, terrorist financing, and prohibited practices will be duly exercised by FAO 
according to its   Policy Against Fraud and other Corrupt Practices . The FAO office of the inspector General handles 
complaint related to:  
• Unlawful acts related to FAO activities (for example theft, fraud, embezzlement, solicitation/acceptance 
of bribes or kickbacks, extortion);  
• Misrepresentation, forgery, or false certification in connection with any official business;  
• Fraud committed to obtain undue financial benefits or entitlements ( e.g., fraudulent claims for rental 
subsidy, education grant, travel, medical insurance);  
• Fraud, favoritism, disclosure of confidential bidding information, or misconduct related to contract bids, 
performance of contract obligations or evaluation;  
• Retaliation as defined under the Whistleblower Protection Policy;  
• Staff violations of the Standards of Conduct for International Civil Servants   
• Complaints of workplace harassment pursuant to the Policy on the Prevention of Harassment, Sexual 
Harassment and Abuse of Authority . 
325. The project will transfer the ownership of the procured item(s) to the beneficiary group immediately upon the 
attainment of technical installation clearance, the project will withhold ownership documents and prepare a full 
handover schedule to prevent any fraudulent activities or illegal disposition of the asset(s) not according to the 
business plan. The beneficiary group will assume the full duty of  care upon completion of ownership transfer. All 
steps of the asset management will be guided by FAO relevant rules.

Secretariat’s assessment of F P255 
Proposal name:  Transforming Livelihoods through Climate Resilient, Low 
Carbon, Sustainable Agricultural Value Chains in the Lake Region Economic Bloc, Kenya  
Accredited entity:  Food and Agriculture Organi zation of the United Nations  
Country:  Kenya  
Project/programme size: Small 
I. Overall assessment  of the Secretariat
1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following
remarks:
Strengths  Points of caution  
This cross -cutting project is building on 
demonstrated approaches of working with 
cooperatives as key agents of change in the 
Kenyan agriculture sector , while also 
supporting individual smallholder farmers to build resilience and organize  better .  
The project proposes a value -chain approach 
for multiple commodities – both staple crops 
and cash crops  – providing end -to-end 
support to  climate -affected producers and 
value -chain actors.  
2. The Board may wish to consider approving this funding  proposal  in accordance with the
term sheet agreed between the Secretariat and the accredited entity  (AE)  and, if considered
appropriate, subject to the conditions set out in a nnex II to  document GCF/B. 41/02.
II. Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment
2.1 Project  background 
3. This project , “Transforming Livelihoods through Climate Resilient, Low Carbon,
Sustainable Agricultural Value Chains in the Lake Region Economic Bloc, Kenya (CRLCSA) ”, aims
t
o transform agricultural value chains toward s low- carbon, climate -resilient pathways using  a
l
ocally- led approach. It leverages the private sector, smallholder farming networks, and th e
c
ooperative movement's strengths in Kenya to establish a framework for technology transfer
and scaling up, drawing on the expertise and knowledge within national and internation al
f
arming networks. With a focus on six priority value chains ( dairy, poultry, coffee, tea, fruit
trees, and African leafy vegetables ) the project seeks to introduce and expand both adaptation
and mitigation technologies at every stage, from production and harvesting to processing and
marketing.


---
*Page 99*
---

 
GCF/B.41/02/Add.03  
Page 99 
 
 
4. The project is organized around four main outcomes/components . Outcome 1 focuses 
on strengthening county- level government services to support the expansion of climate -
resilient, low -carbon and sustainable value chains, emphasizing how governments can assist 
cooperatives and vulnerable farmers. Outcome 2 collaborates  with county administrations to 
invest in community -led, sustainable agricultural landscape management and restoration, 
laying a foundation for climate resilience within the agricultural sector. Outcome 3 focuses on 
transferring production, harvest and processing technologies directly to farmers and their 
organizations to reduce emissions, enhance resilience and optimize natural resource 
management in response to climate variability and impacts.  
5. Under Outcome 4, the project aims to improve farmers' market access by partnering 
with value -chain actors, leveraging private sector financing to support scalability, and providing 
risk mitigation through Kenya Commercial Bank (a direct access entity in Kenya), Co -operative 
Bank of Kenya, and Equity Bank. Additional grant co -financing is provided by the Government of 
Denmark, Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO ), and the Government 
of Kenya. The project will be implemented through F AO as the AE , with FAO and Agriterra 
serving as co -executing entit ies (EEs) , while the Government of Kenya will execute the activities 
it co -finances. The project aims to directly benefit 572,000 individuals, primarily smallholder 
farmers, including both men and women , and will also indirectly benefit 2.1 million individuals.  
2.2 Component -by-component  analysis  
Component 1 : Enabling public support of a daptation and mitigation (t otal cost : USD 2.44 million ; 
GCF cost : USD 2.00 million ) 
6. Component 1 of this project focuses on enhancing climate -resilient and socially inclusive 
agricultural practices by developing innovative, gender- responsive extension methods to 
disseminate CRLCSA knowledge  (activity 1.1.1). It aims to strengthen climate information 
services for underserved communities, including women, youth and persons with disabilities, 
through cooperative networks  (activity 1.1.2) .  
7. Additionally, the project will develop decentralized carbon accounting methodologies, 
update agricultural databases, and assess climate solutions tailored for the agriculture sector  
(activit ies 1.1.3, 1.1.4 and 1.1.5). Knowledge -sharing through existing platforms will also 
support broader adaptation and learning  (activity 1.1.6) . 
Component 2: Sustainable resilient agricultural landscapes (t otal cost : USD 13.61 million ; GCF 
cost : 0.32 million)  
8. Under C omponent 2, the project aims to create county and regional strategies for 
climate -resilient, low -carbon agricultural landscape management, focusing on enhanced 
watershed management, sustainable land use, reforestation and natural regeneration  (activity 
2.1.1) . These strategies will be implemented and monitored to ensure effective climate 
adaptation and landscape sustainability  (activity 2.1.1) . 
Component 3: Resilient livelihoods  (total cost : USD 25.6 4 million ; GCF cost : USD  22.04  million ) 
9. Component 3 w ill provide CRLCSA production and processing assets, along with 
training, to smallholder farmers and farmer organizations, enhancing their climate resilience  
(activity 3.1.1). It aims to spread CRLCSA technology and knowledge through cooperative 
networks and peer exchanges, encouraging smallholder farmers to form cooperatives as a means to mitigate and share climate risks  (activit ies 3.1.2 and 3.1.3) . Additionally, the project 
emphasizes improving social inclusion and fostering women's active participation within climate -resilient value chains  (activity 3.1.4).  
Component 4 : Scaling through CRLCSA market and finance  (total cost : USD 4.16  million;  GCF cost : 
USD 1.97 million )


---
*Page 100*
---

 
GCF/B.41/02/Add.03  
Page 100 
 
 
10. The project aims to boost demand and market access for CRLCSA commodities by 
collaborating with buyers and aggregators while expanding access to third -party certification 
and labe lling schemes (a ctivit ies 4.1.1 and 4.1.2). It will also develop inclusive finance tools and 
products that promote CRLCSA value chains, emphasizing gender -responsiveness and social 
inclusion  (activit ies 4.2.1  and 4.2.2) . Additionally, support will be provided to smallholders and 
their business units in creating viable business plans, enhancing access to financial incentives 
for agroforestry, and fostering equitable opportunities across the sector  (activity  4.2.3).  
Additional allocation  in parallel provided by other commercial banks in the form of loan s (total 
cost : USD 10 million ) 
11. The project will secure up to USD 10 million in leveraged financing through loans from 
Kenya Commercial Bank, Equity Bank, and the Cooperative Bank of Kenya, which will be 
directed to farmers, cooperatives and value -chain actors based on bankable proposals and 
business plans developed under Component 4.  
Additional allocation: Project man agement ( total cost : USD  4.14  million ; GCF cost : USD 2.88 
million ) 
12. This project will be managed by the AE, FAO , who is also the main EE of the project. FAO 
will be responsible for the overall management of the project, including (i) all aspects of project 
appraisal; (ii) administrative, financial and technical oversight and supervision throughout 
project implementation; (iii) ensuring funds are effectivel y managed to deliver results and 
achieve objectives; (iv) ensuring the quality of project monitoring, as well as the timeliness and 
quality of reporting to GCF; and (v) project closure and evaluation.  
III. Assessment of performance against  investment criteria  
3.1 Impact potential                                                                 Scale: N/A  
13. The project will support 572,000 vulnerable smallholder farmers whose livelihoods rely 
on one of the six targeted value chains in the region. This includes an expected reach of at least 80,000 cooperative members (project participants), benefiting approximately 320,000 individuals based on a conservative estimate of 4 people per household.  Additional beneficiaries 
include 63,000 smallholder farmers outside the  cooperatives, reaching an estimated 252,000 
individuals within their households.  
14. The indirect beneficiaries include the  non-participating cooperatives within the six 
value chains (1,342,140 individuals), with additional benefits reaching extended family 
members and households in nearby villages through participants in the farmer field school 
program me (756,000 people). This brings the total immediate indirect beneficiaries to 
2,098,140, with women comprising 50  per cent  of this group  – nearly 15  per cent  of the Lake 
Region  Economic Block (LREB) population. In the long term, even broader  indirect benefits 
across the Lake region  are anticipated , supported by an upscaling strategy and the willingness 
of Kenyan banks to provide loans to eligible  cooperatives.  
15. The project also aims to enhance health, well -being, and food and water security for the 
most vulnerable communities by improving adaptive capacities through climate -resilient and 
low- carbon technologies, access to markets, finance, social safety nets and risk -sharing 
mech anisms within organized farmer groups and cooperatives. It will boost food and nutrition 
security by increasing the production of essential crops and commodities for both consumption 
and sale, thereby improving incomes through enhanced pro ductivity, value addition, and 
market access.  
16. The project also seeks to reduce exposure to climate risks like droughts and floods 
through watershed management, soil conservation and increased access to climate information


---
*Page 101*
---

 
GCF/B.41/02/Add.03  
Page 101 
 
 
services, including early warnings. Improved water access will be achieved by promoting water 
conservation, harvesting and erosion control measures. Gender equality and social inclusion are 
central to the project, with an emphasis on equitable access to ag ricultural inputs, finance, 
agroclimate information, and extension services, as well as increased representation of women, 
youth and persons living with disabilities in decision -making roles within cooperative societies 
and value chains. Additionally, access to gender -responsive and socially inclusive adaptation 
and mitigation technologies, markets and labo ur practices will be expanded.  
17. The project is estimated  to achieve a net emissions reduction of 4,268,492.04 tonnes of 
carbon di oxide equivalent ( t CO₂ eq ) by 2044 . The m itigation benefits for this project have been  
calculated using two distinct methodologies: the FAO Ex -ACT model assessed emissions from 
agriculture, forestry and other land use  activities across all value chains except dairy and 
poultry, while the Global Livestock Environmental Assessment Model  estimated emissions 
specifically for these livestock value chains.   
3.2 Paradigm shift potential                                                                     Scale: N/A  
18. The project promotes a fundamental paradigm shift by transforming value chains from 
low productivity and resilience to high -performing, financially profitable and climate -resilient 
low-carbon pathways through a locally -led approach. It utilizes cooperative s as a mechanism for 
reducing climate risk, functioning not only as a business strategy that allows farmers to pool 
resources but also to enhance their negotiating power in the market. This is achieved by 
empowering farmer organizations and cooperatives to  identify climate challenges and solutions 
while facilitating access to relevant adaptation and mitigation technologies, thereby proactively 
enhancing climate resilience in their production and business practices. By ensuring that the 
promoted technologies  and practices align with the business plans and models of cooperatives, 
farmers can adopt climate solutions that are both environmentally beneficial and financially 
viable.  
19. Overall, the project has significant potential for scaling up and replication, optimizing 
knowledge -sharing and learning through training sessions, demonstrations, community 
meetings and other methods. It also enhances the enabling environment for farmers and cooperatives by strengthening the capacity of county administrations to deliver climate -related 
and market access services. This support will em power farmers to produce more and higher -
quality products that can reach the market more efficiently. Key services that contribute to this 
enabling environment include improved climate information services and warnings, 
agrometeorological advisories, counc il-approved lists of climate -resilient, low -carbon 
technologies, enhanced extension services, as well as market studies, information and sales 
data.  
3.3 Sustainable development  potential                                                Scale: N/A  
20. The project activities will have direct linkages to the following Sustainable Development 
Goals (SDGs) : SDG  1: no poverty;  SDG 2: zero hunger ; SDG 3: good health and wel l-being ; SDG 5: 
gender equality ; SDG 8: decent work and economic gro wth ; SDG 13: climate act ion; and SDG 15: 
life on lan d. These goals will guide the project's implementation and ensure that it contributes 
effectively to addressing critical global challenges related to poverty reduction, food security, 
health, gender equity, sustainable economic growth, climate change and b iodiversity 
conservation.  
21. The project has a measurable co -benefit in job creation across the six value chains, while 
indirect benefits include increased economic activity in the supply chains for agricultural inputs and feed, as well as heightened employment in the transportation and bulking segments of the 
value chain. Notably, the project aims to provide meaningful employment opportunities for


---
*Page 102*
---

 
GCF/B.41/02/Add.03  
Page 102 
 
 
women and youth within cooperatives, farming groups and processing industries. Furthermore, 
it is anticipated to benefit the broader population of the LREB through the implementation of 
climate -sensitive landscape management strategies and overall enhancem ents in the region's 
economic outlook. 
22. Additional indirect socioeconomic benefits encompass improved access to education for 
children, particularly for girls, whose workloads may be reduced or whose families may have 
greater ability to pay school fees. Health improvements are expected due to in creased access to 
a more diverse and higher quality food supply, whether produced or purchased, along with 
better access to health services facilitated by greater financial capacity. The project also aims to 
promote greater gender equality, decrease rates of gender -based violence and child labo ur, and 
enhance access to meaningful employment for youth, women and persons with disabilities.  
23. At the farm level, environmental benefits arise from implementing sustainable land 
management practices, anti- erosion initiatives, increased soil cover, crop diversification or 
rotation, and the careful application of fertilizers, alongside reductions in h armful agro -
chemicals and herbicides. These efforts lead to improved land productivity, increased biomass production, enhanced soil fertility, better soil moisture retention and microorganism content, 
and increased agrobiodiversity at both farm and landsca pe levels.  
3.4 Needs of the recipient                                                                         Scale: N/A  
24. Smallholder farmers, in particular  in the LREB  faces  significant challenges due to inter -
annual rainfall variability and fluctuating trends in crop yields and livestock production, leading to unstable and unreliable incomes. The increasing variability in rainfall has exacerbated these 
issues, resulting in chronic food insecurity for 10  per cent  of the population in the  LREB. This  
situation necessitates both short -term emergency food relief efforts and comprehensive long -
term development program mes to address the underlying vulnerabilities in the agricultural 
sector and improve resilience among affected communities.  
25. Furthermore, i n the LREB , agricultural yields reflect low productivity due to climate 
variability and substantial post -harvest and processing losses. Many farmers operate with 
minimal technical support, low mechanization levels, and limited access to necessary inputs. 
Current prac tices largely consist of unsustainable monocropping with significant land and water 
use impacts.  
26. On the other hand,  smallholder farmers  have very limited financ ial capacity to invest in 
their crop production.  Cooperatives and farmers' organizations typically face limited access to 
financing due to high eligibility requirements, elevated lending costs and challenges in presenting viable business proposals to financial institutions.  
27. In order to overcome the challenges of climate vulnerability and limited financial 
availability , this project establishes a system where governments and the private sector work 
together to remove barriers to self -financing value chains through cooperatives. On one side, 
vulnerable smallholder s receive training and resources for adopting the best climate -resilient, 
low-carbon practices tailored to their value chain. This approach provides both immediate and 
long -term benefits for adapting to climate challeng es, while also allowing smallholders to 
further reduce costs and risks by joining cooperatives. On the other side, established cooperatives exit the project with members who all implement climate -resilient, low -carbon 
agriculture, have access to better ass ets, increase their market presence, and can integrate 
climate service provision into their regular business expenses. This enables them to remain 
competitive and gain additional capacity to access private financing, supporting the model’s 
long -term sustainability.


---
*Page 103*
---

 
GCF/B.41/02/Add.03  
Page 103 
 
 
28. Hence, the CRLCSA  project is needed to reduce climate action costs and to improve 
efficiency and coordination among governments and private actors in delivering climate 
services and facilitating market access.   
3.5 Country  ownership                                                                              Scale: N/A  
29. The country ownership of this project is very high for several reasons:  (i) it strongly 
supports the implementation of national climate change policie s and strategies such as the 
National C limate Change Act of 2016 , update d nationally determin ed con tribution  and the 
National Adaptation Plan (2015 –2030)  of Kenya;  (ii) the national designated authority  (NDA) is 
involved in the project from the inception and project design  stage . The NDA actively 
participated in all major meetings and consultations, providing regular input to align the project proposal with government priorities. Once the project is approved, the NDA will play a crucial 
role in coordinating with county governments and liaising with the Council of Governors;  (iii) 
the Government of Kenya is also one of the EEs and through the National Treasury of Kenya has 
co-financed USD 1 4 million in the project and will execute activities that a re co-financed  by the 
Treasury ; (iv) involvement of several partners at the  regional and national level throughout the 
project design ;. several discussion s were held  at the c ounty level where the consultations took 
place on multiple occasions, starting with the initial consultative meeting which brought together over 90 participants (men and women) including cooperatives, environment, 
agriculture and climate departments  from all 14 counties ; and (v) numerous consultation s were  
held at the  local level with smallholder  farmers , including climate change survey s, individual 
interviews with regard to application s for the climate resilient and low -carbon technologies.  
3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness                                                              Scale: N/A  
30. The proposal effectively demonstrates the project's efficiency and effectiveness through 
a comprehensive economic and financial analysis. Both analyses have been conducted for six 
priority value chains  – coffee, dairy, fruit trees, vegetables, poultry, and tea – under two climate 
scenarios, Representative Concentration Pathway ( RCP ) 2.6 and RCP 8.5.  
31. In the context of the macroeconomic environment  in Kenya , inflation has risen steadily, 
fluctuating moderately between 5 and 7 per  cent over the past five years —a relatively stable 
trend compared to broader African trends. However, food prices remain pressured by ongoing droughts impacting agricultural productivity. The AE identifies access to finance as a key barrier 
preventing local  farmers from further enhancing the agricultural value chains.  
32. Economic viability in the long run is substantiated by the positive results of economic 
indicators across the six value chains. Economic benefits were valued based on gross revenue 
for each value chain, adjusted to economic prices using calculated conversion factors, along with 
reduced and sequestered greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions. The model projects a positive 
economic net present value (NPV) of USD 120 million and an internal rate of return (IRR) of 40 per cent under RCP 8.5, and an NPV of USD 123 millio n with an IRR of 43 per  cent under RCP 
2.6, applying a social discount rate of 10 per  cent over a 20 -year period. Additional economic 
benefits, such as nutritional and health improvements and enhanced natural resources (e.g. soil 
quality), were not included in the model due to data limitations.  
33. Financial viability over the long term is supported by the financial indicators across the 
same value chains. At the project level, incremental financial NPV is USD 10.7 million with a 
financial IRR of 23.4 per  cent and a 12 -year payback period under RCP 8.5. Under RCP 2.6, the 
financial NPV is USD 13 million with an financial IRR of 24.9 per  cent and an 11 -year payback 
period, indicating the project’s financial feasibility using an 18 per  cent financial discount rate 
over 20 years.


---
*Page 104*
---

 
GCF/B.41/02/Add.03  
Page 104 
 
 
34. The financial model projects an average income increase of 20 per  cent for farmers, with 
a conservative target of 15 per  cent incorporated into the logical framework under Outcome 4. 
The model also indicates the financial viability of leveraged financing from Equity Bank and 
Cooperative Bank, amounting to USD 10 million. Even with an 18 per  cent interest rate, a 3 -year 
tenor, and no grace period for these agricultural loans, the investments are shown to be marginally viable, with a financial IRR slightly ab ove the financial discount rate and a debt 
service coverage ratio exceeding 3, ensuring sufficient cash flows for debt servicing post -project 
completion.   
35. The adaptation cost per direct beneficiary is USD 50.6, and USD 13.8 per total 
beneficiary, which are moderate within the GCF agricultural and adaptation portfolio. The 
mitigation cost is USD 0.07 per t  CO₂ eq, positioning it at the very lower end of the GCF portfolio. 
The co -financing ratio stands at 0.71. The proposal addresses the limited dissemination of best 
practices within the LREB and underscores the project’s potential to expand and reinforce best 
practices in low -emission, climate -resilient agricu lture among farmers in the region.  
IV. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies  
4.1 Environmental and social safeguards  
36. Environmental and social risk category. T he AE has assigned the project an 
environmental and social (E&S) risk category of Category B based on its screening of the proposed project activities. The Secretariat agrees with this categorization and confirms that 
the risk category is within the AE’s E&S  risk accreditation level.  
37.  The project is expected to predominantly generate substantial positive E&S  benefits 
through the transformation of key agricultural value chains by the adoption of climate resilient, 
low-carbon sustainable agricultural practices and technologies. Some of main benefits include 
strengthening the resilience of farmers (reducing thei r sensitivity to current and projected 
climate risks and simultaneously improving their adaptive and coping capacity); and reducing 
GHG emissions (promotion of renewable energy, imp rovements in land and water management , 
for example,  agroforestry, reforestation, watershed management). Besides all positive impacts, 
the project also has the potential to generate adverse social and environmental impacts that 
need to be carefully managed and monitored.  
38. Performance Standard (PS) 1: Assessment and management of environmental and 
social risks and impa cts. The potential impacts from project activities are envisaged not to be 
unprecedented in the project area but limited to the project footprint and are neither 
irreversible nor cumulative.   The management and mitigation of identified environmental and 
social risks/ impacts will require the development of Environmental and S ocial Management 
Plans (ESMP s). The AE has developed an environmental and social management framework 
(ESMF) , the umbrella instrument that will  guid e in the preparation of site -specific ESMPs. Final 
consultations with regard to the validation of the ESMF are still pending.  
39. PS2: Labour and working conditions . The AE has identified risk s related to labo ur and 
working conditions. Such risks are likely to include  the risk of employing child ren in project 
activities, discrimination in hiring and selection processes,  abusive working conditions, and a 
high degree of informality and vulnerability (e.g. lack of social protection). The project will work 
mostly with smallholders and family farmers characterized by family -focused motives such as 
favo uring the stability of the farm household system, using mainly family labour for production 
and using part of the produce for family consumption. Additionally, due to the country context, 
youth and children often assist with the farming work of their respec tive families, and there is a 
risk that the  work they undertake might be beyond what is age -appropriate (unless closely


---
*Page 105*
---

 
GCF/B.41/02/Add.03  
Page 105 
 
 
monitored). Additionally, decent work standards and occupational, health and safety guidelines 
will be promoted.  
40. PS3: Resource efficiency and pollution prevention . The project will promote 
practices and technologies that are aimed at improving efficiency in the use of natural resources 
(water, land, soil, energy) through  an inclusive and participatory approach that includes 
women, m en, youth, elders, persons with disabilities and Indigenous Peoples. The project  will 
incorporate an integrated water management approach, including water conservation 
measures, restoration of water catchment areas and watershed management;  conservation and 
restoration of riparian zones; development of several small -scale irrigation schemes rather than 
one large system; promoting water -saving irrigation technologies (drip irrigation, water -saving 
equipment, etc.); rehabilitation of existing irrigation schemes; promoting the rational use of 
industrial water (reuse, cascade use, water -saving processes and equipment); promot ing water 
and rainwater harvesting technologies/structure; and raising public awareness on efficient 
water use. In terms of energy, the p roject  will promote the use of solar energy and energy -
efficient machinery and equipment (e.g. tractors, ventilation systems, drying and storage 
systems, cooling devices).  
41. PS4: Community health, safety and security . Community health and safety risks are 
likely to arise from exposure to agro -chemicals, water and vector -borne diseases, zoonotic 
diseases, and pollution resulting from some project interventions. Risk s are likely  to include the 
marginalization of women, youth and persons with disabilities. Proposed mitigation measures 
include: integrated pesticides management plans, awareness creation and sensitization on 
social distancing for potential diseases, adoption of good industry practices and waste 
management plans. The project proposes to  establish a strong qu ota and target with a dedicated 
budget line for women , youth and vulnerable groups in selected value chains for each training, 
grant and investment opportunity; additionally, the project will adopt a participatory and demand -driven approach to assess community needs and develop support packages tailored to 
attend their needs, challenges and priorities. Education and awareness -raising with the local 
end users regarding sanitation and health issues related to w ater -borne and/or water -related 
diseases , as well as intermittent water quality monitoring of the main water reservoirs , will also 
be embraced and adopted . 
42. PS5: Land acquisition and involuntary resettlement . Based on the Secretariat  
assessment  of the AE’s submission , project activities are unlikely to lead to involuntary 
resettlement or displacement of people or communities; resources from the project will be used for land acquisition  and this is further reinforced by an exclusion list.  Proposed activities will 
imply the involvement of communities on a purely voluntary and demand -driven basis. The 
country reports historic difficulties related to land access and tenure security rights that could compromise the large -scale adoption of CRL CSA practices and increase competition over 
natural r esources among marginalized communities. Existing land uses and land ownership are 
considered as factors that might influence the adoption/selection of specific production 
technologies and practices, for example private landowners are more likely to conser ve land as 
they are assured of retaining the long -term gains from investments in conservation. Security of 
tenure might limit the participation of women, youth and persons with disab ilities in the project 
activities,  and can influence socioeconomic activit ies such as a change in the range of income 
generating activities, lifestyles, etc.  
43.  PS6: Biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of living natural 
resource s. The activities promoted by the project are expected to generate substantial positive 
environmental benefits from the scaling up of climate -resilient land use practices, increasing not 
only the resilience of ecosystems and local communities to climate change, but also reduc ing 
land degradation  and maintain ing/enhanc ing biodiversity and ecosystem functionality through 
an ecosystem -based approach. Considering that some agricultural and forestry activities could 
take place near or in the buffer zones of critical habitat or natural protected areas, there are


---
*Page 106*
---

 
GCF/B.41/02/Add.03  
Page 106 
 
 
restrictions regarding the type of activities that can be financed/promoted by the project to 
avoid any potential significant adverse impact on biodiversity or habitats (refer to the exclusion 
list in a ppendix 1).  
44. PS7: Indigenous Peoples . Pastoralist Indigenous Peoples are not engaged in the project 
activities. However, the project will develop and implement the county landscape management 
strategies which could have potential impact on Indigenous Peoples. The AE has provided an 
Indigenous Peoples  Plan to comply with the Indigenous Peoples  Policy, to be applied in case the 
project confirms the presence of Indigenous Peoples  in the project target areas. The AE is 
recommended to engage with the GCF Indigenous Peoples A dvisory Group on relevant 
Indigenous Peo ples' issues that may be encountered.    
45. PS8: Cultural her itage . The project will preserve and protect the cultural heritage 
(tangible and intangible), avoiding and reducing the adverse impacts that could potentially affect the cultural heritage. Based on the s ubmission of the AE, t he project does not aim to make 
use of intangible forms of culture such as cultural knowledge, innovations and practices of 
communities embodying traditional lifestyles for commercial purposes. Adequate consultation 
regarding a ctivities to be carried out  on Indigenous Peoples’ territories will include free, prior 
and informed consent  and an exclusion list has been developed . Activities related to planning 
process (Landscape Management Strategies of the counties) shall map or delimit these areas . 
Additionally,  a Chance Find Procedure has  been  developed .  
46. Grievance redress mechanism (GRM ): The project will establish one or more 
grievance mechanisms at the field level to file complaints in the location where the project is 
being implemented.  The GRM will serve as a mechanism for timely resolution of an issue 
directly linked to project activities and prevent escalation of problems into social conflict; and 
as an accountability mechanism where people who are directly or indirectly affected by the 
project can seek remedy. However, the GRM will also serve as a p roactive tool to facilitate 
dialogue with affected communities at all stages of the project cycle, including to determination of information the communities require and the best means for them to receive it.  
47. Sexual exploitation, abuse and h arassment (SEAH) . The project will adopt a zero -
tolerance approach to all forms of SEAH. Potential incidents may arise from interaction of 
project staff or partners with community members and lack of awareness about SEAH and how 
to address it. Specific SEAH risks and their level of significance will be determined during 
subactivity screening prior to implementation. Proposed mitigation measures to manage risks 
include regular SEAH sensiti zation campaigns and training to all project -related personn el, such 
as those from the EEs,  and putting in place a code of conduct for personnel. The farmer field 
schools curricula activities will be utili zed as an opportunity to raise awareness and incorporate 
good practices to address SEAH in specific value chains. In addition, SEAH provisions, such as 
training and disciplinary actions in case of incidents, will be included in agreements with EEs. 
The project -level grievance redress mechanism (GRM) will be accessible for reporting and 
responding to SEAH complaints . It will be survivor -centred and gender -responsive and will be 
used for monitoring SEAH incidents. In addition, the GRM will be complemented by referral pathways that may be used for survivors and victims. Project personnel will be trained on the 
GRM to handle such incidents and ensure safe work ing conditions. The AE is advised to pay 
particular attention to tea -related activities that will be supported by the project given the 
prevalence of sexual exploitation of women in this value chain as indicated in the ESMF.  
4.2 Gender policy  
48. The AE has provided a gender analysis and action plan and therefore complies with the 
GCF gender policy. The gender assessment identifies several key challenges faced by women in 
the LREB of Kenya, in the agricultural sector. Women make up a significant po rtion of the 
agricultural labour force but face substantial barriers in accessing land, finance and agricultural


---
*Page 107*
---

 
GCF/B.41/02/Add.03  
Page 107 
 
 
inputs. Given that climate change amplifies existing socioeconomic inequalities,  such as control 
over or access to resources, women smallholder farmers in Kenya lack secure land rights, 
livestock assets and technologies,  constrain ing them from adapting to and coping with climate 
shocks.  Added vulnerabilities include increased risk for gender -based violence due to conditions 
such as longer distances to collect water and loss of livelihoods,  creating tensions within 
households. Sociocultural norms limit their  participation in decision -making and high -value 
agricultural value chains, which are often dominated by men. Additionally, women face a 
disproportionate work burden, restricted access to agricultural extension services, and 
increased vulnerability to clim ate change impacts, all of which affect their ability to improve 
livelihoods and contribute to sustainable agriculture. Women also have limited autonomy in health -related decision -making, further hindering their socioeconomic progress.  
49. The Gender Action Plan  aims to address these barriers through targeted interventions  
and defines activities, indicators and targets for women's participation and benefits with 
corresponding timelines, budget and gender expertise. It addresses gender inequalities across several priority areas in agri -climate adaptation and mitigation. These inc lude closing gender 
gaps and existing inequalities in participation at the intra -household level and within producer 
organizations or cooperatives ; improving access to and use of productive resources such as 
climate information, technologies and credit ; and collective action by working with women’s 
groups as platforms for enhancing access, agency and voice in climate -smart agriculture. In 
addition, the project will provide access to extension services for women. The plan also includes measures for increasin g access to markets and developing agricultural landscape management 
strategies in consultation with women during  the development  stages .  
4.3 Risks  
4.3.1.   Overall pro ject  assessment  (medium  risk)  
50. The proposed cross -cutting project will help address climate change impact s in 14 
counties in Kenya (the LREB ) by investing in climate -resilient low -carbon,  sustainable 
agricultural production and will assist local government in supporting producers and value -
chain participants in agriculture.  Target GCF result areas are:  forestry and land use ; most 
vulnerable people and communities ; health and well -being ; and food and water security.  GCF is 
requested to provide a grant of USD 29.2 million. Together with gr ant contributions  of USD 14.0 
million from the Government of Kenya, USD 5.8 million from the Government of Denmark, and 
USD 0.98 million from FAO, the total project cost is USD 50 million. The c o-financing ratio is 
1:0.71 and will account for 42 per cent of total financing.  
4.3.2.  Accredited entity/executing entity capability to execute the current pro ject  
(medium risk)  
51. FAO and Agriterra will jointly share project implementation responsibility for this 
funding proposal. In addition to overall project management, FAO will serve as EE and handle the enabling of local government support, agricultural landscape management, so cial inclusion , 
and market opportunities and inclusive finance. On the other hand, Agriterra will be responsible 
for dissemination of climate technology, support to  smallholder farmers, business plan 
development and access to financial incentives for farmers.     
52. FAO was accredited by GCF as an AE in 2016.  FAO is a specialized agency established to 
fight hunger and achieve food security with operations in over 130 countries worldwide.  
Agriterra is a non -profit agricultural agency that works on farmer organization and cooperative 
development. Agriterra provides training and sharing of experiences to farmers ’ cooperatives 
by experts to strengthen governance, leadership, operational management and sustainable


---
*Page 108*
---

 
GCF/B.41/02/Add.03  
Page 108 
 
 
financial services. Since its founding 25 years ago, Agriterra has worked with over 320 farmers' 
organization s supporting over 1 million farmers across Africa, Asia and South America.  
4.3.3.  Project -specific execution risks ( medium risk)  
53.  The proposed project aims to reduce climate emission s via conservation of the 
ecosy stem, dissemination of technologies and assets,  and reorientation of business practices 
towards long -term sustainability. Grant funding will also be used by local government units for 
related climate support activities such as knowledge dissemination and upgrade of agricultural 
databases. At least 28  per cent  of the USD 50 million project funding is expected to be provided 
by the Government of Kenya.  However, the credit rating of the Government of Kenya was 
recently downgraded to B - (S&P and Fitch) and Caa1 (Moody's) over issues related to wider 
fiscal deficit, rising interest payment s, high debt burden and heightened sociopolitical risk. The 
government reporte d larger budget deficit compared to that original ly plan ned due to higher 
spending and tax revenue shortfall.  High external debt level, tight monetary policy, and revenue 
shortfalls put pressure on the national budget to support publicly financed projects.  This 
concern is partly mitigated by the government’s historically high level of commitment to natural 
resources conservation, good access to concessional external financing, and no immediate 
external liquidity pressure; 74 per cent  of total external debt is on concessional terms with long 
tenors.  In relation to weaker credit standing, the Kenyan Shilling has been depreciating against 
the U nited States  dollar in the last five years and is projected to further depreciate by 4  per cent  
per annum  in 2025 –27 in the S&P base case scenario. During the six -year implementation 
period, the project will be exposed to foreign currency depreciation and inflation risk which can lead to cost overrun.  
4.3.4.  Project viability and concessionality  
54. The proposed project has outlined numerous activities for a sustainable agricultural 
landscape and resilient livelihoods.  These include activities in communities to strengthen data 
management, manage community forest and agricultural land, climate resilient crop production 
and technologies, and strengthening the cooperative movement. Both FAO as  AE and EE and 
Agriterra as co -EE have extensive experience implementing these types of activities and should 
raise no concerns regarding project viability.  The prop osed GCF grant will represent 58 per  cent 
of total programme size in a wholly grant -funded project.  The proposed concessionality level is 
justified given the low country rating of Kenya (B - by S&P and Fitch and Caa1 by Moody's) and 
limited public resources. The project is expected to generate significant mitigation and adaptation benefits for Kenya. 
4.3.5.  Compliance risk ( low risk)   
55. The AE and Agriterra, a Netherlands -based cooperative development -focused 
international non- governmental organization , will be the EEs  for the activities funded by GCF 
proceeds, as well as those co -financed by FAO and the Government of Denmark. The 
Government of Kenya, through its NDA, the National Treasury, will execute the activities that it 
co-finances ( activit y 2.1.2) and will not administer GCF proceeds as an EE. The proposed project 
activities generally present relatively lower risks of money launderi ng and terrorist financing 
(ML/TF).  
56. The AE has assessed the ML/TF risk as low due to mitigants that will be put in place 
such as anti -money -laundering/countering the financing of terrorism (AML/CFT) clauses in 
project subsidiary agreement s, as well as its own AML/CFT controls. The AE also highlights that 
close monitoring and oversight during project implementation will be made possible through its 
Eastern Africa sub -regional office in Ethiopia and country representation office  in Kenya.


---

GCF/B.41/02/Add.03  
Page 109 
 
 
57. Given the nature of the proposed project activities, the AE’s established AML/CFT 
controls, and the additional on -the-ground oversight during implementation, the overall 
compliance risk is determined to be low.  
4.3.6.  GCF portfolio concentration risk (l ow risk)  
58.   In case of approval, the impact of this proposal on the GCF concentration risk remains 
within the monitoring thresholds of the Risk Appetite Statement in terms of results areas, single 
proposal or AE concentration.  
4.3.7.  Recommen ded risk rating  
59. It is recommended  that the Board consider the above factors in its decision.  
4.4 Fiduciary  
60. The Food and Agriculture Organization (FAO) will serve as the accredited entity ( AE) for 
this project, responsible for overall management, including project appraisal, administrative 
oversight, financial  supervision, and technical supervision throughout implementation. FAO will 
establish a Project Task Force to fulfil its AE functions, maintaining independence from executing entity f unctions.  
61. The project will be co -executed by FAO and Agriterra as executing entities (EEs). FAO-
Kenya will establish a Project Execution Team for its EE functions, while Agriterra, a global 
farmer advisory organization, will leverage its expertise in strengthening cooperatives and farmers' organizations. The Government of Kenya, through the National Treasury, will execute 
activities co -financed by the government but will not administer GCF proceeds.   
62. A Project Management Unit will be established in Nairobi with a regional antenna in 
Kisumu, led by the assistant FAO Representative and a National Project Coordinator. The Project Management Unit  will coordinate with relevant stakeholders and support day -to-day activities.   
63. The Project Steering Committee, co -chaired by the P rincipal Secretary of the Treasury 
and the Principal Secretary of the Ministry of Agriculture, will provide strategic guidance. The 
Project Technical Coordinating Committee, chaired by FAO -Kenya, will conduct monitoring, 
learning, reporting and risk management.  
64. Regarding fund flow, GCF proceeds received by FAO as AE will flow to the co -EEs (FAO -
Kenya and Agriterra) for project implementation. The arrangement is governed by various agreements, including the project agreement between FAO and the Government of Kenya, 
execution agreement between FAO and Agriterra, and co -financing agreement between FAO and 
Ministry of For eign Affairs of the Government of Denmark ( DMFA).   
65. Financial management and procurement will follow FAO rules and regulations, utilizing 
its Global Resources Management System. FAO will oversee Agriterra's financial management and procurement through regular supervision missions. Independent audits will be  performed 
by an internationally recognized auditing firm, selected competitively by FAO, to ensure compliance with agreed standards and practices.   Summary risk assessment

4.5 Results monitoring and reporting  
66. The main causal pathway that the funding proposal sets out to achieve as contained in 
the theory of change is based on the premise that empowering cooperatives , improving access 
of smallholder farmers and value- chain  actors to improv ed climate information, technologies, 
markets and finance to support production, processing and marketing of targeted value chains 
will lead to improved resilience and reduced emissions from the agriculture, forestry and other 
land use  sector. This pathway of change was clearly set out in the theory of change.  
67. The project aims to contribute to three GCF results  areas: forestry and land use 
(mitigation), most vulnerable people and communities (adaptation), and health and well -being 
and food and water security (adaptation) . As such, the project will report against GCF Core 
Indicator 1:  GHG emissions reduced, avoided or removed/sequestered ; Core Indicator 2:  Direct 
and indirect beneficiaries reached ; Core Indicator 4:  Hectares of natural resources brought 
under improved low -emission and/or climate -resilient management practice  to capture 
mitigation and adaptation results respectively ; and  Supplementary Indicator 2.5 : Beneficiaries 
(female/male) adopting innovations that stren gthen climate change resilience. Appropriate 
sources of information and means of verification have been specifie d in the logical framework 
and the monitoring and evaluation plan for the tracking and reporting  of these result s areas.  
68. The proposal also allocated around USD 1.5 million for all monitoring, evaluation and 
learning activities and the Secretariat deem s this sufficient to enable compliance with the 
requirements of the Integrated Results Management Framework and the GCF Evaluation Policy.  
4.6 Legal assessment  
69. The Accreditation Master Agreement was signed with the accredited entity on 8 June 
2018 (the “ AMA ”), and it became effective on 4 October 2018. The accredited entity’s five -year 
accreditation term lapsed on 3 October 2023 but was extended by d ecision B.37/18, paragraph 
(q) until the earlier of three years from the date of lapse of the five- year accreditation term or 
the date on which a revised accreditation framework is adopted by the Board. The Board 
approved the re- accreditation of the accredited entity p ursuant to d ecision B.37/18, and 
negotiation of the Amended and Restated Accreditation Master Agreement is ongoing.  
70. The accredited entity has provided a legal opinion/certificate confirming that it has 
obtained all internal approvals  and it has the capacity and authority to implement the project.  
71. The proposed project will be implemented in the Republic of Kenya in which GCF is not 
provided with privileges and immunities. This means that, among other things, GCF is not protected against litigation or expropriation in this country, which risks need t o be further 
assessed. Moreover, the ability of GCF to undertake redress activities and/or investigations in 
such countries may be hindered due to the absence of privileges and immunities for relevant 
GCF personnel.  
72. Therefore, it is recommended that the Board considers whether disbursements of GCF 
proceeds should only be made after GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation 
and expropriation in the country, or has been provided with appropriate privileges and 
immunities for GCF and its personnel . 
73.  To address the matters raised in this section  it is recommended that any approval by 
the Board is made subject to the following conditions:  
(a) Signature of the funded activity agreement in a form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat within 180 days from the date of Board  approval ; and 
(b) Completion of the legal due diligence to the satisfaction of the GCF Secretariat.

I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel
1. This is the first submission of the  funding proposal  “Transforming Livelihoods through
Climate Resilient, Low Carbon, Sustainable Agricultural Value Chains in the Lake Region
Economic Bloc, Kenya ” submitted to the independent Technical Advisory Panel (iTAP ) under
the standard proposal approval process . The accredited entity (AE) is the Food and Agricultur e
Organization of the United Nations (FAO ). FAO and Agriterra will be e xecuting entities (EE) for
GCF proceeds and for the co-financing from FAO and the Government of Denmark. The host
country, the Government of Kenya, act ing through the National Treasury, will be the EE for the
sub-activities co -funded by the Government of Kenya.
2. This cross -cutting funding proposal is aimed at  fostering the emergence of climate -
resilient, low -carbon, environmentally sustainable, and financially viable agricultur al valu e
c
hains by accelerating the transfer of technology, knowledge, assets and services with a focus on
agrifood cooperatives as key agents to leverage rural change.
3. The expected mitigation outcome is 4,268,492 tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (t
CO2 eq) over the 20- year lifespan of the project . The proposal focuses on the mitigation area o f
f
orestry and land use to achieve this outcome .
4. The expected adaptation outcome includes  572,000 direct and 2,098,140  indirect
beneficiaries , for which the project focuses on three adaptation result areas  (ARA s): most
vulnerable people and communities (ARA1) ; health and well- being, and food and water securit y
(
ARA2) ; and ecosystems and ecosystem services (ARA4).
5. The project will achieve its  mitigation and adaptation outcomes  throug h activitie s
o
rgani zed in  four  components:
(a) Enabling support for adaptation and mitigation;
(b) Sustainable resilient agricultural landscapes;
(c) Resilient livelihoods; and
(d) Scaling through climate -resilient, low -carbon, sustainable agriculture (CRLCSA) market
and finance.
6. The overall budget for the six years implementation period is USD 49,992,238, with  a
f
unding request to GCF of USD 29,215,309 as a grant. The Government of Kenya will contribute
USD 14 million, the Government of Denmark will provide USD 5,791,910 and FAO will provid e
USD
 985,910 as co -financing to this funding proposal, all in the form of  grants .

*Page 112*
